Friedman–Ramanujan functions in random hyperbolic geometry and application to spectral gaps
Nalini Anantharaman 1 and Laura Monk 2

Abstract
. This paper studies the Weil–Petersson measure on the moduli space of compact hyperbolic surfaces of genus g g . We define “volume functions” associated with arbitrary topological types of closed geodesics, generalising the “volume polynomials” studied by M. Mirzakhani for simple closed geodesics. Our programme is to study the structure of these functions, focusing on their behaviour in the limit g ⟶ + ∞ g\longrightarrow+\infty . Motivated by J. Friedman’s work on random graphs, we prove that volume functions admit asymptotic expansions to any order in powers of g − 1 g^{-1} , and claim that the coefficients in these expansions belong to a newly-introduced class of functions called “Friedman–Ramanujan functions”. We prove the claim for closed geodesics filling a surface of Euler characteristic 0 0 and − 1 -1 . This result is then applied to prove that a random hyperbolic surface has spectral gap ≥ 2 / 9 − ϵ \geq 2/9-\epsilon with high probability as g → + ∞ g\rightarrow+\infty , using the trace method and cancellation properties of Friedman–Ramanujan functions.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 58J50, 32G15; Secondary 05C80, 11F72

1.
Introduction

The aim of this article is to develop new geometric tools
for the study of
random hyperbolic surfaces
. Notably, we wish
to extend a variety of methods discovered by Mirzakhani

[
26
,
27
]
allowing the study of simple
geodesics to
non-simple geodesics
.

A
compact hyperbolic surface

X
is a connected, oriented,
compact surface, without boundary, equipped with a Riemannian metric
of constant curvature
-1
. Its topology is therefore entirely
determined by its
genus

g\geq 2
. We will be particularly
interested in surfaces of large genus. This large-genus limit can be
viewed as a large-scale limit, because the area of a compact
hyperbolic surface of genus
g
is
4\pi(g-1)
by the Gauss–Bonnet
formula.

1.1.
Random hyperbolic surfaces

Several different models of random hyperbolic surfaces exist

[
5
,
14
,
27
,
25
]
. In this article,
we will focus solely on the
Weil–Petersson model
, that
consists in equipping the
moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{g}:={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\{\text{compact hyperbolic surfaces of genus }g\}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3393pt}{$\displaystyle{\text{isometry}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\{\text{compact hyperbolic surfaces of genus }g\}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3393pt}{$\textstyle{\text{isometry}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\{\text{compact hyperbolic surfaces of genus }g\}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.35417pt}{$\scriptstyle{\text{isometry}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\{\text{compact hyperbolic surfaces of genus }g\}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{isometry}}$}}}

with the probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}
obtained by renormalisation of
the measure induced by the Weil–Petersson symplectic form on

\mathcal{M}_{g}
. This is a very natural probabilistic setting, in
which one can hope to accurately describe
typical
surfaces.

In her breakthrough articles
[
26
,
27
]
, Mirzakhani
developed a toolbox allowing to study the geometry of random hyperbolic surfaces
sampled according to the probability
\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}
, especially in the large-genus
limit. These tools have since then been applied in an ever-growing number of
articles, analysing the geometric properties of random surfaces

[
32
,
35
,
34
,
17
]
, their spectral gap

[
45
,
21
,
15
,
17
]
and eigenfunctions

[
11
]
, as well as the statistics of their length spectrum

[
28
,
44
]
and Laplacian spectrum

[
31
,
38
]
.

1.2.
The spectral gap of a compact hyperbolic surface

While many of the results presented in this article are purely geometric, they
are all deeply motivated by an important question in
spectral theory
,
which we shall now present.

The spectral gap of a compact hyperbolic surface is the smallest
non-zero eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}>0
of the (positive) Laplace–Beltrami
operator on the surface. Surfaces with a large spectral gap are known
to be well-connected
[
9
,
6
]
, fast-mixing for the
geodesic flow and random walks
[
37
,
12
]
, and of
small diameter
[
24
]
. Finding (rich) families of such surfaces has
been an objective shared by many, whether in the context of
arithmetics and number theory
[
40
,
20
]
, spectral
geometry
[
8
]
, and more recently random hyperbolic
geometry

[
25
,
45
,
21
,
16
,
17
,
22
]
.

In the large-genus regime, Huber
[
18
]
proved that the spectral gap
is bounded above by a quantity going to
1/4
as
g\rightarrow+\infty
(
1/4

being the bottom of the spectrum of the hyperbolic plane). The existence of
surfaces of large genus with a near-optimal spectral gap was conjectured by
Burger–Buser–Dodziuk
[
8
]
in the 80’s, and only solved very
recently by breakthrough work of Hide–Magee
[
17
]
using random
covers.

Our aim in this article is to provide new tools and ideas, towards a proof of
the fact that hyperbolic surfaces with a near-optimal spectral gap not only
exist, but are
typical
.

Conjecture 1.1
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{g\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{1}{4}-\epsilon\right)=1.

The literature so far contains two probabilistic spectral gap results in the
Weil–Petersson setting. First, Mirzakhani proved in 2013 that random hyperbolic
surfaces satisfy
\lambda_{1}>0.002
with probability going to
1
as

g\rightarrow+\infty

[
27
]
. This bound has been vastly
improved by two independent teams in 2021, Wu–Xue
[
45
]
and
Lipnowski–Wright
[
21
]
, who proved that for all
\epsilon>0
,

\lambda_{1}\geq 3/16-\epsilon
with probability going to
1
as
g\rightarrow+\infty
.

The geometric results obtained in this article can be used to obtain the following
improved spectral gap result.

Theorem 1.2

(
10.1
)
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{g\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon\right)=1.

In
Section

3.4
, we explain in detail why the intermediate values

3/16
and
2/9
appear naturally, and some of the steps that need to be taken
to reach the optimal value
1/4
. The proof relies on the classical
trace
method
, as used in
[
45
,
21
]
, with a major new ingredient
developed in this article, which allows us to exhibit
non-trivial
cancellations
.

1.1
is exactly analogous to Alon’s famous conjecture
[
1
]

stating that random
d
-regular graphs with
n\gg 1
vertices typically have a
near-optimal spectral gap. It was solved by Friedman in
[
10
]
,
after 20 years of active research. Compact hyperbolic surfaces and regular
graphs share a variety of geometric and spectral properties, and the results
presented in this article can be seen as analogues of several important steps of
Friedman’s proof of Alon’s conjecture.

1.3.
Averages of geodesic counting functions

A natural approach to access the geometry and spectrum of random
hyperbolic surfaces consists in reducing problems to the study of
averages of the form

(1.1)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\text{all}}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg]

where

•

\mathcal{P}(X)
is the set of primitive oriented closed geodesics

\gamma
on the surface
X
;

•

for any closed geodesic
\gamma
on
X
,
\ell_{X}(\gamma)
is the length of
\gamma
;

•

F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function with compact
support.

Such averages have been used to obtain geometric results in

[
27
,
28
,
32
,
34
]
. Importantly, they appear
in trace methods when taking the expectation of the
Selberg trace
formula
, a formula relating the eigenvalues of the Laplacian to the lengths
of all closed geodesics on the surface (see
Section

3.4
).

Unfortunately, the methods developed by Mirzakhani
in
[
26
,
27
]
only allow to study such
sums if they are restricted to
simple
geodesics,
i.e. geodesics with no self-intersection:

(1.2)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\text{ simple}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg].

This has proven to be very restrictive, and dealing with non-simple closed geodesics is often a
challenging aspect of the study of random hyperbolic surfaces

[
28
,
45
,
21
,
38
]
.

In this paper, we provide new information on the contribution of non-simple
geodesics to the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
. We hope the tools we develop can be used in
various settings.

Remark 1.3
.

Wu–Xue proved in
[
45
]
that, for any
\eta>0
,

(1.3)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}+\mathcal{O}_{\eta}\left(\frac{1}{g}\,\|F(\ell)\,e^{(1+\eta)\ell}\|_{\infty}\right).

As a consequence, at the leading order as
g\rightarrow+\infty
, non-simple
geodesics do not contribute to the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
.
However, we will see in this article that some non-simple geodesics yield
contributions decaying like
1/g
in the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
, which means that

Eq.

1.3
cannot be extended past the precision
1/g
.

We explain in
Section

3.4
why, in order to reach the optimal
spectral gap
1/4-\epsilon
, all computations need to be performed with
arbitrary high precision, i.e. with errors decaying in
1/g^{N}
for arbitrary
large
N
. The spectral gap
3/16
then appears to be the threshold at which
a description of the contribution of non-simple geodesics to the average

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
becomes essential.
Our spectral gap result,
1.2
, is obtained by entirely
analysing the contribution of size
1/g
of the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
.

1.4.
Local topological types of geodesics

In order to study the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
where the sum runs over all closed
geodesics, we regroup its terms according to what we call the
local
(topological) type

T
of
\gamma
. This is done in

Section

4
, and we refer the reader to
Section

2

for the definitions of topological notions appearing below.

The data of a local topological type is given by a pair
[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, where

S
is a
topological surface with boundary
, and
\gamma
is a

closed filling loop
on
S
. Several examples are represented in

Fig.

1
. For instance, all simple geodesics are grouped in a
local type, given by a simple loop in a cylinder. Another type, which we will
describe in detail in this article, is the
figure-eight
, i.e. geodesics
with exactly one self-intersection, represented at the top of

Fig.

1(b)
. For this type,
S
is a
pair of pants
(a surface
of signature
(0,3)
).

(a)

The type “simple”.

(b)

Types filling a pair of pants.

(c)

Other types.

Figure 1
.
A few examples of local types.

Now, we say a closed geodesic
\gamma_{g}
on a hyperbolic surface
X
of genus

g
is
of local type
T=[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
if there exists an embedding

S\rightarrow X
sending
\gamma
on
\gamma_{g}
. For a local type

T
and a measurable function
F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with compact support, we define

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma_{g}\text{ of type }T}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma_{g}))\Bigg]

which allows us to rewrite

(1.4)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\sum_{T\text{ local type}}\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}.

Remark 1.4
.

The word
local
is an emphasis on the fact that the notion of local type
only depends on the topology of the geodesic itself, and not the way it is
embedded in the surface of genus
g
. Notably, all simple closed geodesics
form one local type. This notion should not be confused with the notion of

topological type
, which is often used to refer to mapping-class-group
orbits (in Mirzakhani’s work for instance). We prove in
4.11

that the notion of mapping-class-group equivalence is finer than the notion of
local equivalence. Hence, every local type can be decomposed as a disjoint
union of topological types, which we refer to as its

realisations
. Realisations correspond to the different ways the pair

(S,\gamma)
can be embedded in a surface of genus
g
.

1.5.
Statement of the main results on the averages

In
5.5
, we provide an expression for the averages

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
in terms of Weil–Petersson volumes of moduli spaces of bordered
hyperbolic surfaces. We use this expression to prove the following.

Theorem 1.5

(Theorems
5.9
and

5.14
)
.

Let
T=[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type.

•

For any
g\geq 2
, there exists a unique continuous function

V_{g}^{T}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, called
volume function
,
such that, for any measurable function
F
with compact support,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)V_{g}^{T}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

•

There exists a unique family of continuous functions

(f_{k}^{T})_{k\geq|\chi_{S}|}
such that, for any
K\geq 0
, any large
enough
g
, any
\epsilon>0
,

(1.5)

\frac{V_{g}^{T}(\ell)}{V_{g}}=\sum_{k=|\chi_{S}|}^{K}\frac{f_{k}^{T}(\ell)}{g^{k}}+\mathcal{O}_{K,S,\epsilon}^{w}\left(\frac{\exp\left((1+\epsilon)\ell\right)}{g^{K+1}}\right).

Remark 1.6
.

The notation
\mathcal{O}^{w}\left(\cdot\right)
is a weak version of the usual Landau notation

\mathcal{O}\left(\cdot\right)
, and introduced in
Section

2.1
.

1.5
is new in all cases except for the local
type “simple”, where it comes as a consequence of

[
26
,
29
,
45
]
. In this case, we know the value of

f_{0}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)=\frac{4}{\ell}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
, and this
expression is an essential component of many recent results

[
28
,
45
,
21
,
38
]
.

We highlight the fact that the leading-order term of
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
for the type

T=[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
decays like
g^{\chi_{S}}
, where
\chi_{S}\leq 0
is the
Euler characteristic of the filled surface
S
. This is the reason why only
simple geodesics contribute to the leading term of
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
: for all other local
types,
\chi_{S}<0
.

Remark 1.7
.

Let us describe the obstacle to the study of non-simple geodesics in
Mirzakhani’s work. Mirzakhani’s integration formula
[
26
]

allows to write an explicit formula for the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}
. This
is done by considering a random hyperbolic surface
X
of genus
g
containing
a simple closed geodesic
\gamma
of length
\ell
, and analysing the topology
of the surface
X\setminus\gamma
obtained by
cutting

X
along the
geodesic
\gamma
. Because the geodesic is simple, the result is a (possibly
disconnected) hyperbolic surfaces with two
geodesic boundary components

of length
\ell
.

This unfortunately ceases to be true for non-simple geodesics. In order to
remedy this, we rather cut
X
along the boundary components of the surface

S_{\gamma}
filled by the non-simple geodesic
\gamma
. This approach was used
to a certain extent in
[
28
,
45
,
21
]
, but we push
it further, which allows us to write a formula for
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}

(
5.5
). The formula is more involved, because it
contains an average on all possible hyperbolic metrics on the filled surface

S_{\gamma}
for which
\gamma
has length
\ell
.

Example 1.8
.

In
5.7
, we compute
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
for the type

T=[P,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, where
P
is a pair of pants and
\gamma
a
figure-eight. A metric on the pair of pants
P
is entirely described by the
lengths
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
of its three boundary components. As a
consequence, the formula for
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
in this setting takes the form of an
integration on the two-dimensional level-set

(1.6)

\left\{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}\,:\,\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)=2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)\right\}

which corresponds to the metrics on
P
for which the length of the figure-eight is
exactly
\ell
.

In
Section

6
, we explain how to adapt

1.5
to the overall average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
, obtained by
summing over all closed geodesics.

Theorem 1.9

(Theorems
6.2
and

6.3
)
.

•

There exists a unique continuous function

V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that, for any
measurable function
F
with compact support,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

•

There exists a unique family of continuous functions

(f_{k}^{\mathrm{all}})_{k\geq 0}
such that, for any
A\geq 1
,

K\geq 0
,
\epsilon>0
, and any large enough
g
,

(1.7)

\frac{V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)}{V_{g}}\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}(\ell)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{f_{k}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)}{g^{k}}\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}(\ell)+\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon,K,A}^{w}\left(\frac{\exp{(1+\epsilon)\ell}}{g^{K+1}}\right).

Remark 1.10
.

The indicator function in
Eq.

1.7
is used to reduce
the number of local topological types that need to be summed when computing

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
. Indeed, if we do not assume that we only look at geodesics of
length
\leq A\log(g)
, we a priori need to take into account the
geodesics filling the whole surface of genus
g
, for instance.

Now that we know that the averages
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
and
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
admit asymptotic
expansions in powers of
1/g
, we shall be concerned with the form of the
coefficients
(f_{k}^{T})_{k\geq 0}
and
(f_{k}^{\mathrm{all}})_{k\geq 0}

appearing in these expansions.

1.6.
Friedman–Ramanujan functions

An essential step in Friedman’s proof of Alon’s conjecture is the introduction
of a notion of
Ramanujan functions

[
10
, Section 7]
. We
adapt this notion to the context of random hyperbolic geometry. We then show
that this class of functions arises naturally when studying the lengths of
geodesics on random hyperbolic surfaces, and its relevance to the spectral gap
problem.

Definition 1.11

(
3.1
)
.

A continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is said to be a

Friedman–Ramanujan function
if there exists a polynomial function
p

and a constant
c>0
such that

f(\ell)=p(\ell)\,e^{\ell}+\mathcal{O}\left((\ell+1)^{c}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\right).

We denote as
\mathcal{F}
the class of Friedman–Ramanujan functions, and as
\mathcal{R}

the subset of Friedman–Ramanujan function for which
p=0
.
We similarly define a notion of Friedman–Ramanujan function
in the
weak sense
using the weaker
\mathcal{O}^{w}\left(\cdot\right)
.

This is a natural adaptation of Friedman’s definition of
Ramanujan
functions
for
d
-regular graphs, namely functions
f:\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

such that

f(k)=p(k)\,(d-1)^{k}+\mathcal{O}\left(k^{c}(d-1)^{\frac{k}{2}}\right)

for a polynomial function
p
and a constant
c>0
. The quantities

e^{\ell}
and
(d-1)^{k}
are the growth-rate of balls in the
hyperbolic plane and the
d
-regular tree respectively.

Remark 1.12
.

The name “Ramanujan” was chosen by Friedman in relationship to the
breakthrough work by Lubotzky–Phillips–Sarnak
[
23
]
, in which
the authors prove the existence of large
d
-regular graphs with an optimal
spectral gap (such graphs were called
Ramanujan graphs
due to the use
of the Ramanujan conjecture in
[
23
]
). We have chosen the name
“Friedman–Ramanujan” with the wish to both maintain the link with the
original article that inspired this work and emphasise Friedman’s impressive
contribution to the study of random
d
-regular graphs.

Remark 1.13
.

An alternative way to understand the definition of
Friedman–Ramanujan function and its relation to the spectral gap
problem is to look at the prime number theorem with error terms,
proven by Huber
[
18
]
(see also
[
7
, Theorem
9.6.1]
). This theorem states that, for a fixed
hyperbolic surface
X
and a large
\ell
,

N_{X}(\ell):=\#\{\gamma:\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq\ell\}=\li(e^{\ell})+\sum_{j:0<\lambda_{j}<\frac{3}{16}}\li\big(e^{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-\lambda_{j}}\right)\,\ell}\big)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{\frac{3}{4}\ell}}{\ell}\right)

where
\li(x):=\int_{2}^{x}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\tau}{\log\tau}\sim x/\log(x)
.
The leading term
\li(e^{\ell})\sim e^{\ell}/\ell
comes from the eigenvalue

\lambda_{0}=0
. We observe that small eigenvalues (or at least the ones
smaller than
3/16
) correspond to subdominant contributions to

N_{X}(\ell)
. The exponent gap in the definition of Friedman–Ramanujan
functions, between the exponent
e^{\ell}
in the main term and the exponent

e^{\ell/2}
in the remainder, corresponds exactly to the gap between the
trivial eigenvalue
0
and the optimal spectral gap
1/4
.

1.7.
Link between Friedman–Ramanujan functions and spectral gaps

The motivation behind the introduction of Friedman–Ramanujan functions is that
one can exhibit
cancellations
in the Selberg trace formula thanks to
their structure. We discuss this relationship in
Section

3.4
. It
motivates the following objective.

Objective (FR)
.

Let
T
be a local type, and
s_{T}=1
if
T
is “simple” and
0

otherwise. Prove that, for any
k\geq 0
, the function

\ell\mapsto\ell^{s_{T}}f_{k}^{T}(\ell)
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function in
the weak sense.

For the local type “simple”, we recall that we know that

f_{0}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)=\frac{4}{\ell}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
, where the function

\ell\mapsto 4\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function. For
higher-order terms in
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}
, we show in
3.3
that
our previous work
[
2
]
implies our claim for the type
“simple”. In this article, we prove it for the following local types.

Theorem 1.14
.

For any local type
T
filling a surface of Euler characteristic
-1
, all
functions
(f_{k}^{T})_{k\geq 0}
are Friedman–Ramanujan in the weak sense.

Surfaces of Euler characteristic
-1
are the pair of pants and the once-holed
torus. The proofs are quite technical, notably due to the difficulties hinted at
in
1.7
. They are presented in Sections

7
(for the figure-eight filling a pair of
pants) and
8
(for all other loops filling a pair of
pants or once-holed torus). Because the expansion in

1.5
starts with the term
k=|\chi_{S}|
,

1.14
has the following immediate consequence.

Corollary 1.15
.

For
any
local type
T
, the function

\ell\mapsto\ell^{s_{T}}f_{k}^{T}(\ell)
is Friedman–Ramanujan in the weak sense
for
k=0
and
1
.

This result is the key ingredient to the proof of
1.2
. We hope
to fulfil
Objective (FR)
for
any
local type in a forthcoming
paper. The analysis presented in
Section

3.4
explains why it
would be a significant step towards a proof of
1.1
.

1.8.
The challenge of tangles

Another striking demonstration of the intimate relationship between
Friedman–Ramanujan functions and the spectral gap problem can be found in our
proof of the following statement.

Theorem 1.16

(
9.1
)
.

The function
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is
not
a
Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense.

This might seem surprising, because

1.15
implies that

\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is a countable sum of
Friedman–Ramanujan functions in the weak sense, and this condition
is stable by linear combination. The proof of this result consists
in proving that, if the counting functions are Friedman–Ramanujan,
then we can obtain quantitative information on the spectral gap.

Lemma 1.17

(
9.6
)
.

If
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is a Friedman–Ramanujan
function in the weak sense, then for small
\delta>0
and large enough
g
,

(1.8)

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta<\lambda_{1}<\frac{5}{72}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{\delta}\left(\frac{1}{g^{5/4}}\right).

The contradiction then arises from the following estimate on the probability for
a surface to have a small eigenvalue.

Theorem 1.18

(
9.2
)
.

There exists
c_{1}>0
and
c_{2}>0
such that, for small enough
a>0
and large
enough
g
,

c_{1}\frac{a^{2}}{g}\leq\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)\leq c_{2}\frac{a}{g}\cdot

In particular the rate of growth
g^{-5/4}
in
1.17

is too fast.
1.18
is obtained by observing that, for
a small
a>0
, the probability for a random surface to contain a once-holed torus
with a boundary of length
\leq a
is roughly
a^{2}/g
. By the min-max principle,
if a surface contains such a piece, then
\lambda_{1}\leq a
.

More generally, embedded subsurfaces with a short boundary are linked to the
presence of small eigenvalues, because in this case the surface is poorly
connected
[
9
,
6
]
. We call such subsurfaces
tangles

– this notion appears in
[
32
,
21
]
.

The value
3/16
is the threshold at which tangles start to
manifest, because their probability is of size
1/g
. In order to go
past
3/16
, we need to
remove tangles
.
In Friedman’s proof of the Alon conjecture, the presence of tangles is a
significant challenge: those issues are explained in
[
10
, Section
2]
and are the motivation for introducing a notion of

selective trace
.

In our proof of
1.2
, we remove tangles using an
inclusion-exclusion argument similar to the one use by
Lipnowski–Wright in
[
21
]
. Our notion of
tangle-freeness is close to the notion defined by Bordenave in his
proof of Friedman’s theorem
[
4
]
, and studied by the
second author and Thomas in
[
32
]
. Unfortunately, our
inclusion-exclusion argument relies on a tedious topological
enumeration, much more complex than the one
in
[
21
]
. We aim to present a more general method to
remove tangles, based on Friedman’s approach, in an upcoming
article. We believe that proving
Objective (FR)
in all
generality, and extending Friedman’s tangle-removal techniques to
hyperbolic surfaces, will allow us to conclude to a proof of

1.1
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce many objects relevant to this article, for the
sake of clarity and self-containment. For a more detailed exposition of these
notions, we refer the reader to
[
7
]
for hyperbolic geometry, and

[
43
,
30
]
for the theory of random hyperbolic surfaces.

2.1.
Notations

For two quantities
F_{1},F_{2}
, we write
F_{1}=\mathcal{O}\left(F_{2}\right)
if there exists a
constant
C>0
such that
|F_{1}|\leq CF_{2}
for any choice of parameters
within the allowed ranges. If the constant depends on a parameter
\alpha
, we
write
F_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\left(F_{2}\right)
.

For a Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a positive measurable function

F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we say that
\mu=\mathcal{O}^{w}\left(F\right)
if
\mu
is bounded
by
F
in a
weak sense
, i.e. if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
the total mass of
|\mu|/F
is smaller than
C
for any choice of
parameters. If
C
depends on a parameter
\alpha
, we rather write

\mu=\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}^{w}\left(F\right)
.

2.2.
Hyperbolic geometry and closed geodesics

2.2.1.
Compact and bordered surfaces

All surfaces in this article are assumed to be oriented, connected and of finite
type (with a finitely generated fundamental group).

A
compact hyperbolic surface

X
is a closed surface equipped with a
Riemannian metric of constant curvature
-1
. The topology of
X
is therefore
entirely determined by its genus
g\geq 2
. By the Gauss–Bonnet formula,
X

has finite area, equal to
2\pi|\chi_{X}|
, where
\chi_{X}=2-2g<0
is the
Euler characteristic of
X
, which shall be denoted as
\chi_{g}
.

The study of compact hyperbolic surfaces is the core focus of this
article. However, in doing so, we will need to cut these surfaces along some
simple closed geodesics – which shall lead us to consider surfaces with a
geodesic boundary. A
bordered hyperbolic surface
is a surface equipped
with a Riemannian metric of curvature
-1
, with a (finite) set of boundary
components, labelled
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, which are either closed geodesics or
cusps (which we will abusively refer to as components of length
0
). The
signature of
X
is the pair
(g,n)
, where
g
is its genus. The Gauss–Bonnet
formula extends to this setting, with
\chi_{X}=2-2g-n
. The case
n=0

corresponds to the compact case above.

2.2.2.
Primitive closed geodesics

A
loop
on
X
is a continuous map
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow X
such
that
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)
. Two loops
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
are said to be

(freely) homotopic
if there exists a continuous map

h:[0,1]\times[0,1]\rightarrow X
such that

h_{|\{0\}\times[0,1]}=\gamma_{0}
and
h_{|\{1\}\times[0,1]}=\gamma_{1}
;
we then write
\gamma_{0}\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-1.87503pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{h}$}\hskip-1.87503pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}$}\,\gamma_{1}
. We say the loop
\gamma
is

non-primitive
if there exists an integer
m\geq 2
and a loop
\gamma_{0}

such that
\gamma\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-1.87503pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{h}$}\hskip-1.87503pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}$}\,\gamma_{0}^{m}
; otherwise,
\gamma
is
primitive
. A
loop is called
essential
if it is neither contractible nor homotopic to a
boundary component or a cusp of
X
(the second condition only matters if
X
is
a bordered surface).

We denote as
\mathcal{P}(X)
the set of homotopy-classes of primitive essential
loops on
X
. It can alternatively be seen as the set of
primitive
(oriented) closed geodesics
on
X
, because each homotopy class in

\mathcal{P}(X)
contains a unique geodesic representative. For

\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, we denote as
\ell_{X}(\gamma)
the length of the
geodesic representative in the homotopy class
\gamma
.

In the following, we will often abusively refer to elements of
\mathcal{P}(X)

as homotopy classes, loops, or closed geodesics; in the latter two cases we will
always talk about them up to homotopy. In particular, we say that two elements

\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
of
\mathcal{P}(X)
are
distinct
(and denote

\gamma_{0}\neq\gamma_{1}
) if
\gamma_{0}
is not homotopic to
\gamma_{1}
. These
elements are called
disjoint
if
\gamma_{0}\neq\gamma_{1}
,

\gamma_{0}\neq\gamma_{1}^{-1}
, and if the homotopy classes
\gamma_{0}
,

\gamma_{1}
admit representatives which have no intersections. An element of

\mathcal{P}(X)
is
simple
if it admits a representative with no
self-intersections (which implies that the geodesic representative does). A

multi-curve
is an ordered family
(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
of
disjoint simple elements of
\mathcal{P}(X)
; taking the geodesic representative
of each homotopy class in this family yields a family of simple, disjoint
geodesics on
X
(i.e. the geodesics have no self-intersections and no mutual
intersections).

2.2.3.
Geodesic counting

The set of primitive closed geodesics on a hyperbolic surface is discrete, and
we shall need to count geodesics of a bounded length. Several counting arguments
will appear in this article, the simplest being the following.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
X
be a hyperbolic surface, compact or bordered. For any
L>0
,

\#\{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)\,|\,\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\}\leq 205\,|\chi_{X}|\,e^{L}.

Proof.

First, if
X
is compact of genus
g
, then by
[
7
, Theorem 4.1.6 and Lemma
6.6.4]
,

\#\{\gamma\text{ primitive geodesic }|\,\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\}\leq 3g-3+(g-1)\,e^{L+6}

which implies the result, because
|\chi_{X}|=2g-2
.

Following the proof of
[
28
, Proposition 4.5]
, we extend the
result to surfaces with a boundary, by doubling the surface: we take two
copies of the surface
X
and glue them along their matching boundary
components. We obtain a compact surface
X^{\prime}
, of Euler characteristic

2\chi_{X}
by additivity of the Euler characteristic. Each primitive closed
geodesic on
X
can be sent injectively on two primitive closed geodesics
on
X^{\prime}
of the same length, and hence the number of primitive closed
geodesics
\leq L
on
X
is smaller than half the number of primitive closed
geodesics on
X^{\prime}
.
∎

2.2.4.
Filling geodesics and Wu–Xue’s improved geodesic counting

When studying a closed geodesic
\gamma
on a surface
X
, it is often very
convenient to introduce a subsurface of
X
that is filled by
\gamma
in the
following sense.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
S
be a topological surface, possibly with a boundary. We say a loop

\gamma
on
S

fills
the surface
S
if each connected component of

S\setminus\gamma
is either contractible or an annular region around a
boundary component of
S
.

For a fixed
S
, one can wonder how many geodesics of length
\leq L
fill

S
. An impressive counting result on this quantity was obtained by Wu–Xue in

[
45
]
.

Theorem 2.3
.

For any
\eta>0
, any topological surface
S
with boundary, there
exists a constant
C_{S,\eta}>0
such that, for any hyperbolic metric
Y

on
S
, any
L>0
,

\#\{\gamma\text{ primitive loops filling }S\,:\,\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\leq L\}\leq C_{S,\eta}\exp\left(L-\frac{1-\eta}{2}\,\ell_{Y}(\partial S)\right).

This result is an improvement of the naive bound from

2.1
, thanks to the decaying properties of the
term
\exp((1-\eta)\ell_{Y}(\partial S)/2)
. It is a central part of
Wu–Xue’s proof that typical surfaces have a spectral gap at least
3/16-\epsilon
.

2.3.
Random hyperbolic surfaces

Let
g\geq 2
. In this article, we sample random hyperbolic surfaces of genus

g
according to the Weil–Petersson probabilistic setting, which we shall now
introduce briefly.

2.3.1.
The moduli space

We sample our random surfaces in the
moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{g}:=\{\text{compact hyperbolic surfaces }X\text{ of genus }g\}\diagup\text{isometry}.

In order to study the moduli space, it is very convenient to introduce its
universal covering, the
Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}_{g}
, which can be
seen as the set of
marked hyperbolic surfaces
. More precisely, we fix a
surface
S_{g}
of genus
g
, which we call the
base surface
. Then,

\mathcal{T}_{g}=\left\{(X,\phi),\quad\parbox{176.407pt}{$X$ compact hyperbolic surface \\
$\phi:S_{g}\rightarrow X$ positive homeomorphism}\right\}\diagup\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 7.9862pt\hskip-7.9862pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Teich}$}\hskip-7.9862pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 7.9862pt}}$}\,

where the quotient is defined by saying that
(X_{1},\phi_{1})\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 7.9862pt\hskip-7.9862pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Teich}$}\hskip-7.9862pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 7.9862pt}}$}\,(X_{2},\phi_{2})

if there exists an isometry
m:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
such that
m\circ\phi_{1}

and
\phi_{2}
are isotopic. The
mapping class group

\mathrm{MCG}_{g}:=\{\text{positive homeomorphisms }\psi:S_{g}\rightarrow S_{g}\}\diagup\text{isotopy}

naturally acts on the Teichmüller space by precomposition of the marking:

\psi\cdot(X,\phi):=(X,\phi\circ\psi^{-1}).

Then the moduli space, as the space of “unmarked” hyperbolic surfaces, is
obtained by forgetting the marking, i.e.

\mathcal{M}_{g}=\mathcal{T}_{g}\diagup\mathrm{MCG}_{g}.

2.3.2.
Length functions

Closed geodesics on a marked surface
(X,\phi)
are in a natural correspondence
with homotopy-classes of loops on the base surface
S_{g}
, thanks to the marking

\phi:S_{g}\rightarrow X
. Indeed, if we denote as

\mathcal{P}_{g}:=\mathcal{P}(S_{g})
the set of homotopy classes of primitive
loops on
S_{g}
, then for any
(X,\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
, the marking

\phi:S_{g}\rightarrow X
provides a one-to-one correspondence between

\mathcal{P}_{g}
and
\mathcal{P}(X)
. We can therefore define, for a

(X,\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
, the length

\ell_{(X,\phi)}(\gamma):=\ell_{X}(\phi(\gamma))
to be the length of the
geodesic representative in the homotopy class
\phi(\gamma)
on
X
. Note that
we will often abusively remove the mention of the marking, so that we will
sometimes write
\ell_{X}(\gamma)
for a
X\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
and

\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
; in this case, it is implied that the overall
quantity that we are studying is
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
-invariant, so that the marking does not
need to be emphasised.

The mapping class group
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
naturally acts on loops on the base surface

S_{g}
, by composition
\psi\cdot\gamma:=\psi\circ\gamma
. The orbit of

\gamma
for this action is denoted as
\mathrm{Orb}(\gamma)
, and the stabilisator

\mathrm{Stab}(\gamma)
. We write
\gamma_{1}\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 7.9515pt\hskip-7.9515pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{MCG}$}\hskip-7.9515pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.1988pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 7.9515pt}}$}\,\gamma_{2}
if there exists a

\psi\in\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
such that
\psi\cdot\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
, in which case

\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are said to have the same
(global) topological
type
. This action also extends naturally to an action on multi-curves, or on
families of loops, and we use the same notations in these cases.

Remark 2.4
.

In most papers of the field,
e.g.
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
45
,
21
,
38
]
,
geodesics are considered to be non-oriented, and orbits and stabilisers are
defined for non-oriented loops, and therefore different from ours. Here, we
choose to consider all loops and multi-curves to be oriented, because the
Selberg trace formula classically runs over all oriented geodesics. We believe
this convention to make a few discussions about constants appearing in
formulas slightly simpler.

2.3.3.
Weil–Petersson form and probability measure

The
Weil–Petersson form

\omega_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}}
is a natural
symplectic structure on the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g}
, which is
invariant by the action of
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
and therefore descends to the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{g}

[
41
]
.

A
pair of pants
is a surface of signature
(0,3)
, and a
pair of
pants decomposition
of
S_{g}
is a multi-curve

(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{3g-3})
, that cuts
S_{g}
into
2g-2
pairs of
pants. For
(X,\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
, after homotopy, this multi-curve is
sent to a decomposition of
X
in hyperbolic pairs of pants, with boundary
lengths and twists
(\ell_{i},\tau_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3g-3}
. These numbers,
called
Fenchel–Nielsen parameters
, are global coordinates on the
Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g}\simeq(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R})^{3g-3}
. Wolpert
proved in
[
42
]
that Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates are symplectic
coordinates for the Weil–Petersson form:

(2.1)

\omega_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3g-3}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{i}\wedge\,\mathrm{d}\tau_{i}.

As any symplectic form does, the Weil–Petersson form induces a volume form on
the Teichmüller space and moduli space, defined by

\operatorname{Vol}_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}}:=(\omega_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}})^{\wedge(3g-3)}/(3g-3)!
. This volume form is the Lebesgue measure

\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3g-3}\,\mathrm{d}\tau_{3g-3}
in Fenchel–Nielsen
parameters. The total mass of the moduli space is finite, and we shall denote
it as
V_{g}
. As a consequence, we can renormalise the Weil–Petersson volume
form, and hence equip the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g}
with the

Weil–Petersson probability measure

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}}:=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\operatorname{Vol}_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}}.

2.3.4.
Spaces of bordered surfaces

As mentioned in
Section

2.2.1
, we will need to consider not only compact surfaces but
also bordered surfaces for the purposes of this article. The definitions above naturally extend to
define, for
(g,n)
such that
2g-2+n>0
and
n>0
,
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{g,n}(\mathbf{x}):=\left\{\parbox{233.3125pt}{bordered hyperbolic surface $X$ of signature $(g,n)$ \\
$\forall i$, the $i$-th component of $X$ has length $x_{i}$}\right\}\diagup\text{isometry}

where the quotient is over positive isometries that preserve each individual boundary component
set-wise. Similarly, we fix a base surface
S_{g,n}
of signature
(g,n)
, which allows us to write

\mathcal{M}_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{T}_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})\diagup\mathrm{MCG}_{g,n},

where the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})
is the space of marked bordered hyperbolic
surfaces and
\mathrm{MCG}_{g,n}
is the mapping class group of
S_{g,n}
(considering only homeomorphisms
fixing each individual boundary component of
S_{g,n}
set-wise).

In this more general setting, there is also a Weil–Petersson symplectic form

\omega_{g,n,\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{WP}}
defined on both
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})
and

\mathcal{T}_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})
, which has the same expression (
2.1
)
for any pair of pants decomposition
(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{3g-3+n})
of the
base surface
S_{g,n}
. The volume form induced by this symplectic structure is
denoted as
\operatorname{Vol}_{g,n,\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{WP}}
. The quantity
V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})
denotes the
total mass of the moduli space, with the exception that

V_{1,1}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Vol}_{1,1,x}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1}(x))
(this symmetry
constant
1/2
reflects the existence of an involution symmetry for every
once-holed torus with boundary – see
[
43
, Section 2.8]
). We shall
omit the mention of the length-vector
\mathbf{x}
whenever it is equal to

(0,\ldots,0)
, i.e. when all boundary components are cusps, hence making
sense of the notations
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{g,n}
and
V_{g,n}
.

2.4.
Mirzakhani’s integration formula

Let
g\geq 2
,
k\geq 1
and
\Gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
be a
multi-curve on the base surface
S_{g}
. For a measurable function

F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with compact support (or decaying fast
enough) and an element
X\in\mathcal{M}_{g}
, we define

(2.2)

F^{\Gamma}(X):=\sum_{(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\in\mathrm{Orb}(\Gamma)}F(\ell_{X}(\alpha_{1}),\ldots,\ell_{X}(\alpha_{k})).

For any
(X,\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
, cutting the surface
X
along the
multi-geodesic representative of the multi-curve
\phi(\Gamma)
yields a family of

q\geq 1
bordered hyperbolic surfaces. We pick a numbering for these surfaces,
and for
1\leq i\leq q
denote as
(g_{i},n_{i})
the signature of the
i
-th
surface. If
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
is a list of values for the respective
lengths of
\phi(\gamma_{1}),\ldots,\phi(\gamma_{k})
on
X
, then for every
i
,
the lengths of the boundary components of the
i
-th surface is a vector

\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n_{i}}
. Note that each component of
\mathbf{x}
is present
exactly twice in the overall family of vectors
(\mathbf{x}^{(i)})_{1\leq i\leq q}
,
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}=2k
, because the
\phi(\gamma_{i})
s each have two sides.
Then, Mirzakhani’s integration formula reads as follows.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
26
]
)
.

For
g\geq 3
, the integral of
F^{\Gamma}
over the moduli space can be computed as

(2.3)

\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g}}F^{\Gamma}(X)\,\mathrm{d}\operatorname{Vol}_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}}(X)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}}F(\mathbf{x})\,\prod_{i=1}^{q}V_{g_{i},n_{i}}(\mathbf{x}^{(i)})\,\prod_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}.

Example 2.6
.

Let us demonstrate how we can use
2.5
to compute
the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}
, defined by summing over all
simple geodesics (see
Eq.

1.2
). We define the following loops on the base
surface
S_{g}
.

•

\gamma_{0}
is a simple loop such that
S_{g}\setminus\gamma_{0}
is
connected (we call such a loop a
non-separating
loop).

•

For
1\leq i\leq g-1
,
\gamma_{i}
is a simple loop such that

S_{g}\setminus\gamma_{i}
has two connected components: on the left side of

\gamma
, a surface of signature
(i,1)
, and on the right side, a surface
of signature
(g-i,1)
.

Then, any simple (oriented) loop on
S_{g}
lies in the orbit of exactly one

\gamma_{i}
for a
i\geq 0
. Hence,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\text{ simple}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg]=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\sum_{i=0}^{g-1}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g}}F^{\gamma_{i}}(X)\,\mathrm{d}\operatorname{Vol}_{g}^{\mathrm{WP}}(X).

We then apply Mirzakhani’s integration formula to each of these multi-curves,
to conclude that

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\left(V_{g-1,2}(\ell,\ell)+\sum_{i=1}^{g-1}V_{i,1}(\ell)V_{g-i,1}(\ell)\right)\ell\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

Remark 2.7
.

2.5
appears in the literature in various forms, and there is
always a symmetry factor
c_{\Gamma}\in(0,1]
in the right hand side of

Eq.

2.3

[
26
,
27
,
43
,
45
,
21
]
. No such
constant appears for us due to the combination of the following choices.

•

A factor
2^{-M(\Gamma)}
, where
M(\Gamma)
is the number of components of

S_{g}\setminus\Gamma
that are of signature
(1,1)
, is removed thanks to our convention

V_{1,1}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Vol}_{1,1,x}^{\mathrm{WP}}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1}(x))
.

•

There is often a symmetry factor
1/\mathrm{Sym}(\Gamma)
, which varies
throughout literature depending on the conventions that are adopted. For
instance, in
[
26
]
,
\mathrm{Sym}(\Gamma)
is said to be the
index of the subgroup
\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Stab}(\gamma_{i})
of

\mathrm{Stab}(\Gamma)
. The reason for this discrepancy is that, in

[
26
]
, the function
F^{\Gamma}
is defined by averaging a
function
F
that is invariant by permutations, and hence, when
\Gamma
has
non-trivial symmetries, several terms in the function
F^{\Gamma}
are
systematically identical.

•

Additional factors, depending on the symmetries of
\Gamma
with respect
to changing orientations of some of its components, appear in

[
43
,
45
,
21
]
. They come from the fact that the
multi-curves are usually considered to be non-oriented, as opposed to our
convention (see
2.4
, and

2.8
below).

•

The presence of an additional factor
1/2
whenever
g=2
, mentioned in
[
43
]
,
due to the existence of the hyper-elliptic involution for surfaces of genus
2
, is the reason
why we assume that
g\geq 3
.

Remark 2.8
.

In their proof of the
3/16-\epsilon
spectral gap result, both
teams
[
45
,
21
]
rely heavily on the presence of
a factor
1/2
in the right hand side of
Eq.

2.3
,
whenever we apply
2.5
to a single simple
non-separating closed geodesic
\gamma
. This argument is
reproduced in
Section

3.4.4
. The distinction here comes
from the fact that
\gamma
is non-oriented for them, and oriented
for us. We can compare the two formulas by observing that

\sum_{\gamma\text{ non-oriented}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\gamma\text{ oriented}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma)).

Contrarily, we do not expect our new approach to the
1/4
conjecture to require much knowledge on
the constants appearing (or not) in
2.5
.

2.5.
Estimates on Weil–Petersson volumes

2.5
allows us to reduce the question of estimating

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}[F^{\Gamma}]
to the study of the Weil–Petersson volumes.
Many estimates are known on the behaviour of

V_{g,n}=V_{g,n}(0,\ldots,0)
in terms of
g
and
n

[
27
,
29
,
28
,
34
]
. We
shall use several of these estimates throughout this article,
referencing them carefully.

In terms of asymptotic expansions, Mirzakhani and Zograf have proved
in
[
29
]
the existence of coefficients

(a_{k,n})_{k>0}
and
(b_{k,n})_{k>0}
, for
n\geq 0
, such that for
K\geq 0
,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\frac{V_{g-1,n+2}}{V_{g,n}}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{a_{k,n}}{g^{k}}+\mathcal{O}_{K,n}\left(\frac{1}{g^{K+1}}\right)

(2.5)

\displaystyle\frac{V_{g,n+1}}{8\pi^{2}gV_{g,n}}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{b_{k,n}}{g^{k}}+\mathcal{O}_{K,n}\left(\frac{1}{g^{K+1}}\right).

As a function of
\mathbf{x}
, Mirzakhani has proven in
[
26
]
that

V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})
is a polynomial function of degree
6g-6+2n
. The bound
[
27
, Lemma
3.2]
on its coefficients directly implies the following two
upper bounds:

(2.6)

\displaystyle V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})\leq V_{g,n}\left(1+\max_{i}|x_{i}|\right)^{6g-6+2n}

(2.7)

\displaystyle V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})\leq V_{g,n}\exp\left(\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}}{2}\right).

The former is good to use for fixed values of
g,n
while the latter is better-suited to the
description of the large-genus limit.
The first-order approximation of
V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})
in the large-genus limit is well-known (see

[
28
, Proposition 3.1]
and
[
2
, Proposition 2.5]
):

(2.8)

\frac{V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})}{V_{g,n}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{2}{x_{i}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{n}\left(\frac{1+\max_{i}|x_{i}|}{g}\exp\left(\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}}{2}\right)\right).

In our previous paper, we have shown the following asymptotic expansion, which
will be useful for expanding the averages
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
2
, Corollary 1.4]
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
. There exists a unique family
(v_{k,n})_{k\geq 0}
of
functions
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that, for any order
K\geq 0
, any genus

g\geq 1
, any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
,

(2.9)

\frac{V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})}{V_{g,n}}=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{v_{k,n}(\mathbf{x})}{g^{k}}+\mathcal{O}_{K,n}\left(\frac{(1+\max_{i}|x_{i}|)^{3K+1}}{g^{K+1}}\exp\left(\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}}{2}\right)\right).

Furthermore, for any
k\geq 0
, the function
v_{k,n}
is a linear
combination of functions

(2.10)

\mathbf{x}\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2k_{i}}\prod_{i\in I_{+}}\cosh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)\prod_{i\in I_{-}}\frac{1}{x_{i}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)

where
(k_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
are integers and
I_{\pm}
are two
disjoint subsets of
\{1,\ldots n\}
.

Remark 2.10
.

The fact that the powers
x_{i}^{2k_{i}}
in
Eq.

2.10
are only even is not
explicitly stated in
[
2
]
, but comes as a straightforward consequence of the
fact that
V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})
is even in every variable.

3.
Friedman–Ramanujan functions

In this section, we introduce and study the main object of this
article, Friedman–Ramanujan functions. We explain in

Section

3.2
how these functions naturally appear in
random hyperbolic geometry. We prove their stability by convolution
in
3.3
, and explain their relevance to the spectral
gap question in
Section

3.4
.

3.1.
Definition and notations

Definition 3.1
.

A continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is said to be a

Friedman–Ramanujan function
if there exists a polynomial
p\in\mathbb{C}[X]

and constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}>0
such that

(3.1)

\forall\ell>0,\quad|f(\ell)-p(\ell)\,e^{\ell}|\leq c_{1}\,(\ell+1)^{c_{2}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}.

A measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is said to be a

Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense
if there exists a
polynomial
p\in\mathbb{C}[X]
and constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}>0
such that

(3.2)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{|f(\ell)-p(\ell)\,e^{\ell}|}{(\ell+1)^{c_{2}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell\leq c_{1}.

Of course, these sets of functions form two vector spaces, that we denote as

\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{w}
respectively. As the name suggests, the strong definition
implies the weak one.

If
f
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function (weakly or strongly), then the
polynomial
p
satisfying the definition is uniquely defined. We will use the
following terms when talking about a Friedman–Ramanujan function:

•

the term
p(\ell)\,e^{\ell}
is called the
principal term
of
f
,
and
p
its
polynomial
;

•

the
degree
of
f
, denoted as
\deg(f)
, will refer to the degree
of the polynomial
p
;

•

any constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
satisfying (
3.1
), or
(
3.2
), will be called the
constant
and

exponent
of
f
respectively, and sometimes denoted as

c_{f}
and
e_{f}
.

For the sake of convenience in our following estimates, we introduce
a semi-norm on
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{w}
, using the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm

\|p\|_{\ell^{\infty}}:=\max_{i}|a_{i}|
for

p(X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}X^{i}\in\mathbb{C}[X]
.

Definition 3.2
.

We define a semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{F}}
on
\mathcal{F}
by setting

\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}}:=\|p\|_{\ell^{\infty}}
for any Friedman–Ramanujan
function
f
of polynomial
p
. We shall denote as
\mathcal{R}
the
space of “remainders”, i.e. functions
f\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}}=0
.

We similarly define the semi-norm
\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}_{w}}
on
\mathcal{F}_{w}
and the set of remainders

\mathcal{R}_{w}\subset\mathcal{F}_{w}
.

3.2.
Motivation to geodesic counting: the case of simple geodesics

One of the motivations to study Friedman–Ramanujan functions is that they appear
naturally when counting closed geodesics on random hyperbolic surfaces
(or closed paths on random
d
-regular graphs, for Friedman). Let us illustrate
this in the most elementary case, the counting of
simple
closed
geodesics.

We saw in
2.6
that Mirzakhani provided an explicit
formula for a function
V_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that,
for any measurable function
F
with compact support,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\text{ simple}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg]=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)V_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

We prove the following.

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists a unique family of functions
(f_{k}^{\mathrm{s}})_{k\geq 0}
such
that, for any integer
K\geq 0
, any
\ell>0
, any large enough
g
,

(3.3)

\frac{V_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)}{V_{g}}=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{f_{k}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)}{g^{k}}+\mathcal{O}_{K}\left(\frac{(1+\ell)^{c_{K}}e^{\ell}}{g^{K+1}}\right).

Furthermore, for all
k
,
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{k}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)
is a
Friedman–Ramanujan function.

In other words, Friedman–Ramanujan functions naturally appear when
computing the terms of the asymptotic expansion of

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}
. This result means that

1.5
and
Objective (FR)
hold for
the local topology “simple”.

Remark 3.4
.

One can show that
f_{0}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)=\frac{4}{\ell}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)

by using the expression of
V_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}
and the first-order estimates
(
2.4
) and (
2.8
), as well
as
[
27
, Lemma 3.3]
. It is clear that

\ell\mapsto\ell f_{0}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)=4\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
is a
Friedman–Ramanujan function.

Proof.

Let us fix a
K\geq 0
. We recall that the expression of
V_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}

is:

(3.4)

V_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)=\ell\,V_{g-1,2}(\ell,\ell)+\sum_{i=1}^{g-1}\ell\,V_{i,1}(\ell)V_{g-i,1}(\ell).

Let us break down this expression and examine its terms.

We first observe that we can reduce the number of terms in

Eq.

3.4
so that it only depends on
K
, and not on

g
. Indeed, applying (
2.7
) and
[
27
, equation
(3.19)]
yields:

\sum_{\frac{K}{2}+1\leq i\leq g-\frac{K}{2}-1}V_{i,1}(\ell)V_{g-i,1}(\ell)=\mathcal{O}_{K}\left(\frac{e^{\ell}V_{g}}{g^{K+1}}\right).

Hence, provided that
g
is large enough, we can rewrite (
3.4
) as

(3.5)

\frac{\ell V_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}(\ell)}{V_{g}}=\frac{V_{g-1,2}}{V_{g}}\frac{\ell^{2}V_{g-1,2}(\ell,\ell)}{V_{g-1,2}}+2\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil\frac{K}{2}\rceil}\ell V_{i,1}(\ell)\frac{V_{g-i,1}}{V_{g}}\frac{\ell V_{g-i,1}(\ell)}{V_{g-i,1}}+\mathcal{O}_{K}\left(\frac{\ell\,e^{\ell}}{g^{K+1}}\right).

Note that we have used the symmetry of the sum to only have terms for which
i\leq\lceil\frac{K}{2}\rceil
.

Now, we observe that
2.9
taken with
n=1
and
2

directly implies that for any fixed
i
,

\frac{\ell V_{g-i,1}(\ell)}{V_{g-i,1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\ell^{2}V_{g-1,2}(\ell,\ell)}{V_{g-1,2}}

admit an asymptotic expansion of the desired form, with all coefficients
belonging in
\mathcal{F}
. Indeed, after multiplication by
\ell^{n}
, the coefficients
of these expansions are proven to be linear combinations of
functions of the form

•

\ell^{2k+1}\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
,
\ell^{2k}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)

and
\ell^{2k+1}
for
n=1
;

•

\ell^{2k+2}\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
,

\ell^{2k+1}\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
,

\ell^{2k}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
,
\ell^{2k+2}\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
,

\ell^{2k+1}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
and
\ell^{2k+2}
for
n=2
;

for
k
non-negative integers, all of which are Friedman–Ramanujan functions.

We know by equations (
2.4
) and
(
2.5
) that the quantities
V_{g-1,2}/V_{g}
and

V_{g-i,1}/V_{g}
(for any fixed
i
) have an asymptotic expansion in powers of

1/g
. Also, for any fixed
i
, the function

\ell\mapsto\ell\,V_{i,1}(\ell)
is a polynomial function. This is all we
need to conclude to the existence and form of the asymptotic expansion.

Now that the existence of an expansion is established, the uniqueness is
obtained by fixing an arbitrary value of
\ell
and using the uniqueness of
asymptotic expansions in powers of
1/g
.
∎

3.3.
Stability by convolution

When proving Alon’s conjecture, Friedman proved a statement analogous to
3.3
, for
more complicated paths. This is achieved by using a decomposition of a general path into simple
paths, together with the result for simple paths. In doing so, a key argument is the stability of the class of

d
-Ramanujan functions by
convolution
, Theorem 7.2 in
[
10
]
. Indeed, as
represented in
Fig.

2
, the length
\ell
of a non-simple path can be written
as a sum of lengths
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
of simpler closed paths. In the expectation for a random graph,
this becomes a sum over all possible values of
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
such as
\ell=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
,
i.e. a convolution.

Figure 2
.
Decomposition of a figure-eight on a graph.

In our new setting, we shall also prove that the class
\mathcal{F}
is
stable by convolution. For two continuous functions

f_{1},f_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we define

f_{1}\ast f_{2}(\ell):=\int_{0}^{\ell}f_{1}(t)f_{2}(\ell-t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\ell}f_{1}(\ell-t)f_{2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Let us prove the following, which is a direct adaptation of the proof
given by Friedman in the case of graphs
[
10
, Theorem
7.2]
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
. Then,
f_{1}\ast f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
.

Remark 3.6
.

In the following, we will not use Proposition
3.5
exactly
as such : unfortunately, in hyperbolic geometry, when we
“concatenate” two closed geodesics by creating an intersection
point, the length of the newly created closed geodesic is not the
sum of the two original length (see
Fig.

10
). This is
a major difference between negative (but finite) curvature, and
curvature
-\infty
(i.e. the case of graphs). However, we
believe the proof is quite enlightening in its simplicity – very
similar techniques, yet more complex, are used in

Section

7
.

Proof.

Write
f_{i}(\ell)=p_{i}(\ell)\,e^{\ell}+r_{i}(\ell)
with

|r_{i}(\ell)|\leq c_{i}(\ell+1)^{c_{i}}\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}
. We may assume that
c_{i}
are
integers. Then,

\displaystyle f_{1}\ast f_{2}=(p_{1}\exp)\ast(p_{2}\exp)+(p_{1}\exp)\ast r_{2}+(p_{2}\exp)\ast r_{1}+r_{1}\ast r_{2}.

First, we observe that

(p_{1}\exp)\ast(p_{2}\exp)(\ell)=\int_{0}^{\ell}p_{1}(t)\,e^{t}\,p_{2}(\ell-t)\,e^{\ell-t}\,\mathrm{d}t=P(\ell)\,e^{\ell}

where
P=p_{1}\ast p_{2}
is a polynomial.
Next, we have

\displaystyle|r_{1}\ast r_{2}(\ell)|\leq c_{1}c_{2}\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t+1)^{c_{1}}(t+1)^{c_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq c_{1}c_{2}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}(\ell+1)^{c_{1}+c_{2}+1}.

Finally, we examine the crossed term
(p_{1}\exp)\ast r_{2}
.

\displaystyle(p_{1}\exp)\ast r_{2}(\ell)

\displaystyle=e^{\ell}\int_{0}^{\ell}p_{1}(\ell-t)\,r_{2}(t)\,e^{-t}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=e^{\ell}\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{1}(\ell-t)\,r_{2}(t)\,e^{-t}\,\mathrm{d}t-e^{\ell}\int_{\ell}^{\infty}p_{1}(\ell-t)\,r_{2}(t)\,e^{-t}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The function
\ell\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{1}(\ell-t)\,r_{2}(t)\,e^{-t}\,\mathrm{d}t

is a polynomial function. For the last term, it is bounded by

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|p_{1}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}c_{2}\,e^{\ell}\int_{\ell}^{\infty}(t-\ell+1)^{\deg p_{1}}(t+1)^{c_{2}}e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

By an integration by parts, (
3.6
) is a function of the form

\ell\mapsto q(\ell)e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}
with
q
a polynomial, and in
particular is bounded by
c^{\prime}(\ell+1)^{c^{\prime}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}
for a constant

c^{\prime}>0
. The same argument of course applies to
(p_{2}\exp)\ast r_{1}
and shows
the announced result.
∎

3.4.
Cancellations in the Selberg trace formula

We have seen in
Section

3.2
that Friedman–Ramanujan functions arise
naturally when computing expectations of sums over (simple) closed
geodesics. The aim of this section is now to show how this information can be
used, in particular in the study of the spectrum of the
Laplacian.

The computations presented in this section are rather technical, and
we invite the reader mostly interested in our new geometric
techniques to skip it at first read. Indeed, while the ideas and
results that we present below are a motivation for many results of
Sections
4
to
8
,
they only come into play in Sections
9
and

10
, when we actually study the spectral gap
of random hyperbolic surfaces.

3.4.1.
The Selberg trace formula

This beautiful formula, proven by Selberg in
[
39
]
, relates the
spectrum of the Laplacian on a hyperbolic surface to the lengths of all its
closed geodesics. It reads, for a smooth even function
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

(3.7)

\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\hat{h}(r_{j}(X))=(g-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{h}(r)\tanh(\pi r)r\,\mathrm{d}r+\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)\,h(k\ell_{X}(\gamma))}{2\sinh\left(\frac{k\ell_{X}(\gamma)}{2}\right)}

where for all
j
,
r_{j}(X)\in\mathbb{R}\cup i[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
is
a solution of
\lambda_{j}(X)=\frac{1}{4}+r_{j}(X)^{2}
, and the Fourier
transform
\hat{h}
is defined by

\hat{h}(r):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(\ell)\,e^{-ir\ell}\,\mathrm{d}\ell
. The
formula is valid for a class of “nice” functions
h
; for our
purposes, we will only consider functions
h
of compact support, in
which case the Selberg trace formula holds and both sums are absolutely
convergent
[
3
, Theorem 5.8]
.

Let us briefly describe the three terms of
Eq.

3.7
.

•

The left hand side term is called the
spectral side
of the trace
formula, and we will use this term to try and access information on the
spectral gap
\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{4}+r_{1}^{2}
.

•

The first term on the right hand side is called the
topological
term
, or
integral term
. The name “topological” refers to the fact
that this term does not depend on the hyperbolic structure on the surface
X
,
but only on its genus
g
. In particular, when studying random hyperbolic
surfaces of genus
g
, this term is deterministic.

•

The last term is the
geometric term
, in which appear
every closed geodesic on the surface. We draw the attention to the
fact that non-simple geodesics appear here, and there is a priori
no known similar formula including only simple geodesics. Dealing
with non-simple closed geodesics in the Selberg trace formula is
one of the challenges we address in this article.

3.4.2.
Spectral gap v.s. exponential growth

Due to the presence of a Fourier transform, and summations on the whole spectrum
and all closed geodesics, the link made by the Selberg trace formula between
geometry and spectrum is quite intricate, and using this formula requires a good
choice of test function. A classic approach to access information on the
spectral gap
\lambda_{1}
using the Selberg trace formula, used in

[
45
,
21
]
notably, is to observe that, if

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{4}+r_{1}^{2}<\frac{1}{4}
, then
r_{1}\in i\mathbb{R}
, and hence

\hat{h}(r_{1})=2\int_{0}^{+\infty}h(\ell)\cosh(\ell|r_{1}|)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

The Fourier transform is therefore an integral against a growing exponential, at
the rate
|r_{1}|=\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-\lambda_{1}}
, rather than an oscillatory
term.

We make the following choice of test function, similarly to

[
45
,
21
]
, that will allow us to exploit this
exponential increase.

Notation 3.7
.

Let
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a smooth even function, with compact
support
[-1,1]
, such that
\hat{h}
is non-negative on

\mathbb{R}\cup i[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. For any
L\geq 1
, let

h_{L}(\ell):=h(\frac{\ell}{L})
.

Remark 3.8
.

Such a
h
can be obtained by taking a square convolution

h:=H\ast H
of a smooth function
H\geq 0
supported on

[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
, so that
\hat{h}=\hat{H}^{2}\geq 0
.

Remark 3.9
.

The scaling parameter
L
plays the role of a
length-scale. Indeed, since the support of
h_{L}
is
[-L,L]
, only
geodesics of length
\leq L
will contribute to the geometric term
of the Selberg trace formula applied to
h_{L}
.

By analogy with graphs, we expect the natural length-scale we need
to consider for the spectral gap problem to be
L=A\log(g)
,
where
A\geq 1
is a fixed constant. In

[
45
,
21
]
, the value
A=4
is used to obtain the
spectral gap
\frac{3}{16}-\epsilon
. We shall take
A=6
to
prove that
\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon
, and see that
arbitrarily large values of
A
are required to reach

\frac{1}{4}-\epsilon
.

The following lemma allows us to relate the size of the spectral gap of a
surface
X
with the rate of exponential growth of the term
\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1}(X))

of the Selberg trace formula.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. For any

0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}
, there exists a constant

C_{\alpha,\epsilon}>0
(depending on
h
) such that, for any
hyperbolic surface
X
, any
L\geq 1
,

(3.8)

\lambda_{1}(X)\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1}(X))\geq C_{\alpha,\epsilon}\,e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)L}.

Proof.

If
\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon<\frac{1}{4}
, then in
particular
r_{1}\in i\mathbb{R}
. Then, by definition of
h_{L}
,

(3.9)

\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})=2L\int_{0}^{1}h(\ell)\,e^{|r_{1}|L\ell}\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

The hypothesis on
\lambda_{1}
further implies that

|r_{1}|\geq\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+\epsilon}
. For
\alpha<1/2
,
we have that
\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}<1-2\alpha
, and hence

\epsilon<1-2\alpha
, which implies

\alpha+\epsilon<\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+\epsilon}
. Hence,

\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})\geq 2\int_{\frac{\alpha+\epsilon}{\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+\epsilon}}}^{1}h(\ell)\,e^{\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+\epsilon}\,L\ell}\,\mathrm{d}\ell\geq C_{\alpha,\epsilon}\,e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)L}

since
h\geq 0
by the hypothesis in
3.7
. The implied
constant can be taken to be

C_{\alpha,\epsilon}:=2\int_{(\alpha+\epsilon)/\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+\epsilon}}^{1}h(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell
, which
is positive because the support of the non-negative function
h

is exactly
[-1,1]
.
∎

The following handy lemma clears up the Selberg trace formula, so that we can
focus only on the terms which shall be crucial to our analysis.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
L\geq 1
, and
F:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth even function, supported
on
[-L,L]
, with
\hat{F}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}\cup i[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. Then, for any
g\geq 2
,

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\hat{F}(r_{1}(X))\right]\leq\left\langle\ell\,F(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\right\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}+C_{F}\,L^{2}g

for a constant

C_{F}:=c\lVert F\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert r\hat{F}(r)\rVert_{\infty}<+\infty
, where

c
is a universal constant independent of
g
,
L
and
F
.

Remark 3.12
.

The constant
C_{F}
is finite because
F
is compactly supported, and hence

\hat{F}
decays faster than any polynomial at infinity.

Remark 3.13
.

The function
h_{L}
defined in
3.7
clearly satisfies the hypotheses
of the lemma. We have formulated the result in terms of a function
F
with
precise hypotheses because we shall later apply it to other test functions.

Lemmas
3.10
and
3.11
provide us with a strategy
to prove probabilistic lower bounds on
\lambda_{1}
. First, we use

3.10
to write

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})\geq C_{\alpha,\epsilon}\,e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)L}\right).

Using Markov’s inequality allows us to obtain that

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})\right]}{C_{\alpha,\epsilon}\,e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)L}}.

We can then use
3.11
to obtain that, for
L:=A\log(g)
,

(3.10)

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)=\mathcal{O}_{\alpha,\epsilon,A}\left(\frac{\left\langle\ell h_{L}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\right\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}}{g^{(\alpha+\epsilon)A}}+(\log g)^{2}g^{1-(\alpha+\epsilon)A}\right),

since the constant
C_{h_{L}}
can be bounded uniformly in
L
.

Let us pick a value of
A>1/(\alpha+\epsilon)
, such as

A:=1/\alpha
, so that

(\log g)^{2}g^{1-(\alpha+\epsilon)A}\rightarrow 0
.
Equation

3.10
then reduces the spectral gap
problem to proving that the geometric average

\langle\ell h_{L}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
is negligible compared to

e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)L}
.

As a conclusion, the trace method allows to bound

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)
in terms
of the geometric average
\langle\ell h_{L}(\ell)\,e^{-\ell/2}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
for

L=A\log(g)
. The parameter
\alpha
needs to be small in order to
obtain a spectral gap close to
1/4
. This naturally requires to
look a length scale
L=A\log(g)
with
A\geq 1/\alpha
, due to
the presence of linear terms in the Selberg trace formula.

Proof of
3.11
.

By the positivity hypothesis on
\hat{F}
,
\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}[\hat{F}(r_{1})]
is
smaller than the expectation of the Selberg trace formula. Its integral term
is independent of the surface, and smaller than
g\lVert r\hat{F}(r)\rVert_{\infty}

because
\tanh\leq 1
. We hence are left with comparing

(3.11)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)\,F(k\ell_{X}(\gamma))}{2\sinh\left(\frac{k\ell_{X}(\gamma)}{2}\right)}\Bigg]

with the average
\langle\ell F(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
.

Let us first prove a bound on the sum over
k\geq 2
, i.e. the
sum for non-primitive geodesics. Note that

x/\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left((x+1)\,e^{-\frac{x}{2}}\right)
for
x>0
. Hence,
for any
0<\ell\leq L
,

\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}\frac{\ell\,F(k\ell)}{2\sinh\left(\frac{k\ell}{2}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(L\|F\|_{\infty}\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{k\ell}{2}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(L\|F\|_{\infty}\frac{e^{-\ell}}{\min(\ell,1)}\right)

because
\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}e^{-k\ell/2}=e^{-\ell}/(1-e^{-\ell/2})
and
1-e^{-\ell/2}\geq c\min(\ell,1)

for a
c>0
.

We recall that
F
is identically equal to zero outside
[-L,L]
. Hence, for a
compact hyperbolic surface
X
of genus
g
, we can apply the previous
estimate to each
\ell=\ell_{X}(\gamma)
appearing in the contribution of
X

to the expectation (
3.11
) and deduce

(3.12)

\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)}\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)\,F(k\ell_{X}(\gamma))}{2\sinh\left(\frac{k\ell_{X}(\gamma)}{2}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{L\|F\|_{\infty}}{\min(\mathrm{sys}(X),1)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\right)

where
\mathrm{sys}(X)
is the length of the systole of
X
, its shortest
closed geodesic. We bound uniformly in
X
the sum above:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\lceil L\rceil}e^{-j}\,\#\{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)\,:\,j\leq\ell_{X}(\gamma)<j+1\}=\mathcal{O}\left(Lg\right)

by
2.1
. As a consequence, taking
the average of
Eq.

3.12
yields

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)}\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)\,F(k\ell_{X}(\gamma))}{2\sinh\left(\frac{k\ell_{X}(\gamma)}{2}\right)}\Bigg]=\mathcal{O}\left(L^{2}g\|F\|_{\infty}\,\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\min(\mathrm{sys}(X),1)}\Bigg]\right).

Mirzakhani proved in
[
27
, Corollary 4.2]
that
the expectation above is finite and bounded uniformly in
g
,
which is enough to conclude for this term.

All that is left to do to conclude is to substitute the

2\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
by the very close value
e^{\ell/2}
in the
term
k=1
of
Eq.

3.11
. Because

\frac{1}{2\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}-e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}=\frac{1}{2\,e^{\ell}\,\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}\leq\frac{e^{-\ell}}{\ell},

the error in doing so is bounded by

\|F\|_{\infty}\,\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\min(\mathrm{sys}(X),1)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\Bigg]

which we proved is bounded by a constant multiple of
gL\|F\|_{\infty}
.
∎

3.4.3.
Necessity of expansions in powers of
1/g

In
[
45
,
21
]
, Wu–Xue and Lipnowski–Wright
obtained the spectral gap
3/16-\epsilon
by using the method
above. More precisely, the intermediate value
3/16
arises because
the average
\langle\ell h_{L}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
is estimated
at the
leading order
as
g\rightarrow+\infty
, i.e. the
computations are made up to errors with a
1/g
decay. We now
explain why, in order to reach the optimal spectral gap

1/4-\epsilon
, we need to go further and perform
asymptotic
expansions
of averages
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
in powers of
1/g
, which is one
of the core objectives of this article.

Let us imagine that we are able to compute, exactly, any average

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
up to error terms decaying as
1/g^{K}
for a
K\geq 1
.
This means that we will know the average

\langle\ell h_{L}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
up to errors of size
roughly
e^{L/2}/g^{K}
, because the number of primitive closed
geodesics shorter than
L
behaves like
e^{L}/L
, by
[
18
]

(the factor
e^{L/2}
comes from the presence of the exponential
decay
e^{-\ell/2}
in the average).

We recall that we saw in
Eq.

3.10
that,
for
\alpha>0
, in order to prove that

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq 1/4-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)
goes to
0
as

g\rightarrow+\infty
, we need to prove that

\langle\ell h_{L}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
grows slower than

e^{\alpha L}
, for
L=A\log(g)
and
A\geq 1/\alpha
. In
particular, we will need the error term
\approx e^{L/2}/g^{K}

produced when estimating this average to be smaller than

e^{\alpha L}
, which requires to assume that

A/2-K\leq\alpha A
. Hence, the hypotheses made so far on the
parameters in the trace method can be listed as:

\frac{A}{2}-K\leq\alpha A\quad\text{and}\quad A\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}.

These conditions imply that
\alpha\geq 1/(2(K+1))
, which is a
lower bound on the precision
\alpha
that can be attained.

In other words, computing asymptotic expansions with remainders
decaying like
1/g^{K}
puts a natural limitation on the spectral
gap
\lambda_{1}\geq 1/4-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon
that can be
obtained. These critical levels are summed up in

Table

1
; we see that the spectral gap

1/4-\epsilon
requires expansions of arbitrary precision.

Order in expansion

Length scale
L

Parameter
\alpha

Hoped spectral gap

Leading (error
1/g
)

4\log g

1/4

\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{3}{16}-\epsilon

Second (error
1/g^{2}
)

6\log g

1/6

\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon

…

…

…

…

Error
1/g^{K}

2(K+1)\log g

1/(2(K+1))

\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4(K+1)^{2}}-\epsilon

Table 1
.
The spectral gap one can hope to obtain using the trace method,
depending on the order of the asymptotic expansion in powers of
1/g
at
which we compute
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
.

The value
2/9-\epsilon
, obtained in this article, corresponds to
understanding the second-order term, which is done here. We hope to generalise
our methods in further articles, and ultimately reach the optimal
spectral gap
1/4-\epsilon
.

3.4.4.
The issue of the trivial eigenvalue

Unfortunately, the contribution of the trivial eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=0
, for
which
r_{0}=i/2
, will always be the dominant term in the Selberg trace
formula. Indeed,
\hat{h}_{L}(i/2)
grows almost like
e^{L/2}
by

Eq.

3.9
. This is much bigger than the size
e^{\alpha L}
we need
to bound it with in order to prove that
\lambda_{1}\geq 1/4-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon
with high
probability. Actually, this rate of growth
e^{L/2}

is exactly what we obtain by using Huber’s counting result
[
18
]
on
the number of closed geodesics
\leq L
.

As a consequence, the method sketched in
Section

3.4.2
will
necessarily fail, if one does not find a mechanism to deal with the contribution
of the trivial eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=0
. The fact that the spectral gap only
appears as a sub-dominant contribution in the trace method, which is hidden by a
much bigger leading order, is always a challenge in spectral gap problems, see

[
10
,
4
]
for instance in the case of graphs.

In
[
45
,
21
]
, when proving that
\lambda_{1}\geq 3/16-\epsilon

typically, both teams rely on quite a miraculous phenomenon. They observe that
the contribution of the trivial eigenvalue,
\hat{h}_{L}(i/2)
, and the average of
the term corresponding to primitive
simple
geodesics in the Selberg trace
formula are very close at the first order in
1/g
. Indeed, by using our
first-order approximation for simple geodesics,
3.3
, and the
value of
f_{0}^{\mathrm{s}}
from
3.4
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\ell\,h_{L}(\ell)}{2\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}\right\rangle_{g}^{\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\ell\,h_{L}(\ell)}{2\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}\,\frac{4}{\ell}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\ell+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{L^{c}e^{\frac{L}{2}}\|h_{L}\|_{\infty}}{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{2\int_{0}^{+\infty}h_{L}(\ell)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\ell}_{\hat{h}_{L}(i/2)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\|h_{L}\|_{\infty}\left(1+\frac{L^{c}e^{\frac{L}{2}}}{g}\right)\right).

It is difficult to see how this approach could still function beyond the
first-order estimate, and if it did, it would require tremendous effort and very
accurate computation of all the coefficients appearing in the asymptotic
expansion.

In this article, we suggest a fundamentally different approach to the one used
in
[
45
,
21
]
, which we believe to be significantly more
robust, and a good candidate to reach the optimal bound

\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{1}{4}-\epsilon
. The idea is to modify our test function
to
create a cancellation
at the trivial eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=0
. More
precisely, we want to apply the Selberg trace formula to a function of Fourier
transform
(\frac{1}{4}+r^{2})^{m}\hat{h}_{L}(r)
, which therefore has a zero of order

m
at
r_{0}=i/2
. This is achieved by considering the new test function

\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}
, where
\mathcal{D}
is the differential operator
\frac{1}{4}-\partial^{2}
. We
prove the following reformulation of the spectral gap problem.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
h
be a function satisfying the hypotheses of
3.7
, and let us
fix real numbers
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
and
A\geq 1
. For any
\delta>0
,
any
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}
, any integer
m\geq 1
, there exists a
constant
C=C(h,\alpha,A,\epsilon,\delta,m)
such that, for any large
enough integer
g
and for the length-scale
L=A\log g
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{C}{g^{(\alpha+\epsilon)A}}\left\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\right\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}+C\,\frac{g(\log g)^{2}}{g^{(\alpha+\epsilon)A}}\cdot

Remark 3.15
.

The parameter
\delta
is there because, if
\lambda_{1}
is very small, then

\lambda_{1}^{m}\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})
will be small. However, this shall not matter,
because we already know that
\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\delta\right)
goes to
0
as

g\rightarrow+\infty
provided
\delta
is small enough

[
27
,
45
,
21
]
.

The hope is that, by cancelling the leading order
\hat{h}_{L}(i/2)
on the
spectral side of the Selberg trace formula, we have created enough cancellations
