for the average of the geometric term

\langle\ell e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
to be much smaller than
it would be without the application of the differential operator

\mathcal{D}^{m}
. We shall provide methods to exhibit such cancellations in

Section

3.4.5
, which will further demonstrate the importance of
Friedman–Ramanujan functions for the study of the spectral problem.

The proof is almost the same as the one sketched in
Section

3.4.2
,
with a few small modifications. We provide the details here, because
multiplication by the operator
\mathcal{D}^{m}
makes us loose the fact that all
terms in the Selberg trace formula are non-negative, which means we need to
proceed with extra caution.

Proof.

By
3.10
applied to the function
h_{L}
, if

\delta\leq\lambda_{1}(X)\leq 1/4-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon
, then

\lambda_{1}(X)^{m}\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1}(X))\geq\delta^{m}C_{\alpha,\epsilon}\,e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)L}
for a constant
C_{\alpha,\epsilon}>0
(depending
on
h
). As a consequence, by Markov’s inequality, using the non-negativity
of
\lambda_{1}^{m}\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{1}{C_{\alpha,\epsilon}\delta^{m}g^{(\alpha+\epsilon)A}}\,\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\lambda_{1}^{m}\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})\right].

We then apply the Selberg trace formula to the function
\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}
,
and more precisely the simplified version we have proven in

3.11
. Note that the function
\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}
satisfies the
hypotheses of
3.11
, because it is even, its support is included
in the support of
h_{L}
, which is
[-L,L]
, and its Fourier transform is
non-negative on
\mathbb{R}\cup i[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. Then,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{C_{\alpha,\epsilon,\delta}}{g^{(\alpha+\epsilon)A}}\,\left(\langle\ell e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}+C_{\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}}L^{2}g\right)

and there is a universal constant
c
such that

C_{\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}}\leq c\|\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}\|_{\infty}+\left\|r\,\left(\frac{1}{4}+r^{2}\right)^{m}\hat{h}_{L}(r)\right\|_{\infty}.

This quantity is bounded by a constant depending only on
m
and

h
, because, for
L\geq 1
, the derivatives of

h_{L}(\ell):=h(\ell/L)
are controlled by the derivatives of
h
,
and because
\hat{h}_{L}(r)=L\hat{h}(rL)
, so the second norm is
bounded by
\|r\left(1/4+r^{2}\right)^{m}\hat{h}\|_{\infty}
.
∎

3.4.5.
Friedman–Ramanujan functions and cancellations

The reason for the introduction of Ramanujan functions in Friedman’s work

[
10
]
is that they are functions which exhibit some

cancellations
when computing
averages of trace formulas
. We shall
extend this observation to hyperbolic surfaces: we show that Friedman–Ramanujan
functions are functions which create non-trivial on-average cancellations in the
Selberg trace formula.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
f
be a Friedman–Ramanujan function in weak sense, and let
c_{f}
,
e_{f}

denote its constant and exponent. Then, for any integer
m>\deg(f)
, there
exists a constant
C_{m}
such that for any
L\geq 1
,

(3.13)

\left|\int_{0}^{L}f(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\right|\leq C_{m}(\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{w}}+c_{f}(L+1)^{e_{f}+1}).

Remark 3.17
.

The constant
C_{m}
depends on our fixed test function
h
. More precisely, it
can be bounded by
C_{m}^{\prime}\max_{0\leq i\leq 2m}\|h^{(i)}\|_{\infty}
for a
constant
C_{m}^{\prime}
depending only on
m
.

In other words, the integral in
3.16
has at-most
polynomial growth in
L
, as opposed to the exponential growth one
could expect, due to the fact that
f(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}

is of size
\ell^{\deg f}\,e^{\ell/2}
for large
\ell
. The
definition of Friedman–Ramanujan function is made so that their
principal term is always cancelled in integrals of the form
(
3.13
). The reason for these cancellations is that
functions of the form
p(\ell)\,e^{\ell/2}
with
\deg(p)<m

lie in the kernel of the operator
\mathcal{D}^{m}
.

Proof of
3.16
.

We write
f(\ell)=p(\ell)\,e^{\ell}+r(\ell)
for a polynomial function

p
and a remainder satisfying

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{|r(\ell)|}{(\ell+1)^{e_{f}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell\leq c_{f}.

Let us first estimate the integral of the remainder term:

\left|\int_{0}^{L}r(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\right|\leq\|\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{L}\frac{|r(\ell)|(L+1)^{e_{f}}}{(\ell+1)^{e_{f}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell=\mathcal{O}_{h,m}\left(c_{f}(L+1)^{e_{f}+1}\right)

because the derivatives of
h_{L}(\ell)=h(\ell/L)
are bounded by that of
h

for
L\geq 1
.

We are therefore left with the integral

\int_{0}^{L}p(\ell)\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

We can estimate it using several integration by parts. Indeed, for any smooth
functions
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
, if
\ell\mapsto H_{2}(\ell)
is identically equal to
zero for
\ell\geq L
, then

(3.14)

\int_{0}^{L}H_{1}(\ell)\,\mathcal{D}H_{2}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell=\int_{0}^{L}\mathcal{D}H_{1}(\ell)\,H_{2}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell-H_{1}^{\prime}(0)H_{2}(0)+H_{1}(0)H_{2}^{\prime}(0)

because
H_{2}(L)=H_{2}^{\prime}(L)=0
. We apply this
m
times to our integral, using
the fact that
h_{L}
and its derivatives vanish above
L
, and obtain that

(3.15)

\int_{0}^{L}(p(\ell)\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}})\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell=\int_{0}^{L}\mathcal{D}^{m}[p(\ell)e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}](\ell)\,h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell+\mathcal{O}_{h,m}\left(\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}_{w}}\right)

because the boundary terms appearing in (
3.14
) are
linear combinations of products of the form

\partial^{i}h_{L}(0)\,\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial\ell^{j}}[p(\ell)e^{\ell/2}](0)
for
i,j\leq 2m
. The integral in the
right hand side of
Eq.

3.15
is equal to
zero, because
\mathcal{D}^{m}[p(\ell)\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}]\equiv 0
as soon
as
m>\deg p
.
∎

In order to conclude this section with a strong motivation for the study of
Friedman–Ramanujan functions in the context of the spectral gap question, we
prove the following consequence of
3.16
. This last statement uses
some notations and results obtained in Sections
4
and

5
, but will not be used until Sections

9
and
10
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
T
be a local topological type. If
Objective (FR)
is
true, i.e. for all
k\geq 0
,

\ell\mapsto\ell^{s_{T}}f_{k}^{T}(\ell)
is Friedman–Ramanujan in the
weak sense, then, for any integer
K\geq 1
, there exists
constants
c_{K}^{T},m_{K}^{T}\geq 0
such that for any large enough

g
, any
m\geq m_{K}^{T}
, any
\eta>0
and
L\geq 1
,

\left\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\right\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\mathcal{O}_{m,T,K,\eta}\left(L^{c_{K}^{T}}+\frac{e^{\frac{L}{2}+\eta L}}{g^{K}}\right).

This result is our motivation to prove
Objective (FR)
in all
generality, which we expect to do in a follow-up paper. Indeed,
recall that we presented in
3.14
a
reformulation of the trace method, where we reduced the spectral gap
problem to proving that the average

\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
is negligible
compared to
g^{(\alpha+\epsilon)A}
, where
L=A\log g
,

A=2(K+1)=1/\alpha
, as explained in

Table

1
. Here,
3.18
tells us
that,
if
Objective (FR)
is true for a type
T
, then this
objective is attained for the contribution of geodesics of type
T

to the overall geometric average

\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
.

For now, we have proved that
Objective (FR)
holds for the type
“simple” in
3.3
, and will extend it to any loop
filling a pair of pants or once-holed torus in
Sections
7

and
8
. In particular,

3.18
is true in these cases.

Proof of
3.18
.

Let
K\geq 1
. By
1.5
,

\left\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\right\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\int_{0}^{L}F^{T}_{g,K}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell+\mathcal{O}_{T,K,\eta}\left(\frac{\|e^{\frac{\ell}{2}+\eta\ell}\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}\|_{\infty}}{g^{K}}\right),

where
F_{g,K}^{T}(\ell):=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\ell f_{k}^{T}(\ell)/g^{k}
. By hypothesis,

\ell\mapsto F_{g,K}^{T}(\ell)
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense, of degree at
most
d_{K}^{T}:=\max_{0\leq k<K}\deg(\ell f_{k}^{T})
, of exponent at most

e_{K}^{T}:=\max_{0\leq k<K}e_{\ell f_{k}^{T}}
, of constant at most

\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}c_{\ell f_{k}^{T}}=\mathcal{O}_{T,K}\left(1\right)
, and of norm

\lVert F_{g,K}^{T}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{w}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\lVert\ell f_{k}^{T}(\ell)\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{O}_{T,K}\left(1\right).

We take
m_{K}^{T}=d_{K}^{T}+1
and
c_{K}^{T}:=e_{K}^{T}+1
, and apply
3.16

to conclude.
∎

4.
Local topological types of closed loops

One of the aims of this article is to generalise methods to compute averages for

simple
geodesics to more elaborate topologies. In order to do so, we need
to introduce a few notations and concepts related to non-simple closed geodesics
on a surface.

4.1.
Surface filled by a loop

A challenge faced when studying general loops is that the machinery developed
by Mirzakhani in
[
26
,
27
]
only applies to

multi-curves
, i.e. families of
simple
disjoint loops. A way around this
difficulty, already used in
[
28
,
45
,
21
]
, is to
associate to any loop a surface that it fills, using the following procedure.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a compact hyperbolic surface, and
\gamma
be a loop on
X
. We
assume that
\gamma
is in minimal position, i.e. that it minimises the number
of self-intersections in its free-homotopy class. We define the
surface

S(\gamma)
filled by
\gamma
the following way.

(1)

We take a regular neighbourhood of
\gamma
in
X
,

\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma):=\{x\in X\,:\,\dist(x,\gamma)<\epsilon\}
for

\epsilon>0
small enough so that
\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)
retracts to
\gamma
.

(2)

The bordered surface
X\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)
has

q_{0}\geq 1
connected components
C_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup C_{q_{0}}
. We
take

S(\gamma):=\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\cup\bigcup_{i:C_{i}\text{ is a disk}}C_{i},

i.e. we add every disk to
\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)
, to form
S(\gamma)
.

The surface
S(\gamma)
is a subsurface of
X
, possibly with a boundary, filled
by
\gamma
. It does not depend on the choice of
\epsilon>0
, in the sense
that the filled surfaces obtained using two small values of
\epsilon
are
isotopic. Similarly, the metric on
X
is solely used to define the regular
neighbourhood, and replacing it by another metric yields the same filled surface,
up to isotopy. The notion of filled surface only depends on the topology of the
loop
\gamma
within the topological surface
X
.

The boundary of
S(\gamma)
is a family of simple loops, which we orient so that

S(\gamma)
lies on the left side of each boundary components.
The motivation for introducing
S(\gamma)
is that the boundary the surface
filled by
\gamma
is (almost) a multi-curve, and can hence be dealt with using
Mirzakhani’s tools (almost because pairs of these boundary loops might satisfy

\gamma_{1}\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-1.87503pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{h}$}\hskip-1.87503pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}$}\,\gamma_{2}^{-1}
whenever there is a cylinder in

X\setminus S(\gamma)
).

(a)

A simple curve.

(b)

Two non-simple curves filling a pair of pants.

Figure 3
.
Examples of filled surfaces.

Example 4.2
.

The surface filled by a simple essential loop is a cylinder. If
\gamma
is a loop with exactly
one self-intersection, then
S(\gamma)
is a pair of pants.

Three examples of loops and their filled surfaces are represented in
Fig.

3
. Note
that, in the first example of
Fig.

3(b)
, we added a disk to the regular
neighbourhood to form the filled surface. In the last picture, we replaced the filled surface by
another isotopic surface, for the sake of readability.

We prove the following, using a classic result of Graaf–Schrijver
[
13
]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
X
be a compact hyperbolic surface. If
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are two closed loops in
minimal position in
X
in the same free-homotopy class, then there exists an isotopy of
X

sending
S(\gamma)
on
S(\gamma^{\prime})
and
\gamma
on a closed loop freely homotopic to
\gamma^{\prime}
in

S(\gamma^{\prime})
.

As a consequence, the surface
S(\gamma)
filled by
\gamma
is well-defined (up to isotopy) for a
free-homotopy class
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)
. We shall see in the proof that this is true thanks
to the fact that we added all disks to the regular neighbourhood of
\gamma
.

Proof.

First, we observe that if
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are
isotopic
, that is
to say if there exists an isotopy
\phi_{t}:X\rightarrow X
,

0\leq t\leq 1
, such that
\phi_{1}\circ\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
, then the claim
is trivially satisfied. Indeed, in this case, for small enough
\epsilon>0
,
we can modify the isotopy
(\phi_{t})_{t}
to obtain a new isotopy
(\psi_{t})_{t}

which coincides with
(\phi_{t})_{t}
on all points of
\gamma
and sends the
regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)
onto the regular
neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\prime})
of
\gamma^{\prime}
. Then, the isotopy

\psi_{1}
is an homeomorphism from each connected components of

X\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)
to each component of

X\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\prime})
, and in particular sends
contractible components to contractible components. Hence, the isotopy

(\psi_{t})_{t}
sends
S(\gamma)
on
S(\gamma^{\prime})
and
\gamma
on
\gamma^{\prime}
, and
our claim is satisfied.

More generally, by
[
13
]
, because
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are freely homotopic and both
in minimal position, there is a finite sequence of third Reidemeister moves that send
\gamma
to
a loop
\tilde{\gamma}
isotopic to
\gamma^{\prime}
. As a consequence, we simply need to prove our claim
for two loops
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
differing by a third Reidemeister move, as represented in

Fig.

4
.

Figure 4
.
On the left hand side, a portion of two loops
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
differing only by a
third Reidemeister move. On the right hand side, in orange, their respective regular
neighbourhood, to which we added the central disk. We labelled the (possibly non-distinct)
connected components of the complement of this region to highlight their correspondence.

We observe on
Fig.

4
that, thanks to the
addition of the central contractible component in the construction
of
S(\gamma)
and
S(\gamma^{\prime})
, there exists an isotopy

(\phi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 1}
(identically equal to the identity
outside the neighbourhood where the Reidemeister move occurs)
sending
S(\gamma)
to
S(\gamma^{\prime})
. The image of
\gamma
by such
an isotopy is represented by the dotted line in the last part of

Fig.

4
, and it is clear that

\phi_{1}\circ\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are freely homotopic within

S(\gamma^{\prime})
.
∎

The following observation on the boundary length of
S(\gamma)
will be useful.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
X
be a compact hyperbolic surface, and
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)
. Let
\hat{S}(\gamma)
denote the surface isotopic to
S(\gamma)
in
X
with
geodesic boundary. Then,
\ell_{X}(\partial\hat{S}(\gamma))\leq 2\ell_{X}(\gamma)
.

Proof.

For any
\eta>0
, we can pick the
\epsilon
for defining the regular
neighbourhood of
\gamma
such that the length of its boundary is

\leq 2\ell_{X}(\gamma)+\eta
. Then, the length of the boundary only
diminishes when adding disks to the complement, and when replacing every
component of the boundary by a geodesic representative, so

\ell_{X}(\partial\hat{S}(\gamma))\leq 2\ell_{X}(\gamma)+\eta
. We obtain the
result by letting
\eta\rightarrow 0
.
∎

4.2.
Definition of local topological type

Let us define a notion of
local (topological) type
. Examples of local types are presented in

Fig.

5
, the type “simple” being the leftmost one.

Figure 5
.
Examples of local topological types.

Notation 4.5
.

To any pair of integers
(g_{S},n_{S})
such that

-\chi_{S}:=2g_{S}-2+n_{S}\geq 0
, we shall associate a
fixed
smooth
oriented surface
S
of signature
(g_{S},n_{S})
. We further fix a numbering of
the
n_{S}
boundary components of
S
, and denote for each
1\leq i\leq n_{S}

as
\gamma_{i}
the
i
-th boundary loop of
S
, oriented so that
S
lies on
the left hand side of
\gamma_{i}
. The data of the pair of integers

(g_{S},n_{S})
, or equivalently of the surface
S
, is called a
filling
type
.

Definition 4.6
.

A
local loop
is a pair
(S,\gamma)
, where
S
is a filling type and

\gamma
is a primitive loop filling
S
. Two local loops
(S,\gamma)
and

(S^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
are said to be
locally equivalent
if
S=S^{\prime}
(i.e.

g_{S}=g_{S^{\prime}}
and
n_{S}=n_{S^{\prime}}
), and there exists a positive homeomorphism

\psi:S\rightarrow S
, possibly permuting the boundary components of
S
,
such that
\psi\circ\gamma
is freely homotopic to
\gamma^{\prime}
. This defines
an equivalence relation
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.32297pt\hskip-4.32297pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{loc}$}\hskip-4.32297pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 4.32297pt}}
on local loops. Equivalence classes for this
relation are denoted as
[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
and called
local (topological)
types
of loops.

Example 4.7
.

Figure 6
.
Two representatives of the local type “simple”.

There is exactly one local topological type filling a cylinder (i.e. of filling type
(0,2)
),
which we shall refer to as the local type “
simple
”. Indeed, there are exactly two homotopy
classes of primitive loops filling a cylinder, represented in
Fig.

6
. Taking a
positive homeomorphism permuting the two boundary components of the cylinder allows to observe that
these two local loops are equivalent.

Example 4.8
.

Figure 7
.
Six representatives of the local type “eight”.

We call “
figure-eight
” the local type of which six representatives are depicted in

Fig.

7
. The figure-eight is of filling type
(0,3)
, i.e. it fills a pair
of pants. As in the previous example, these different representatives can be shown to be equivalent
by applying a positive homeomorphism of the pair of pants permuting its boundary components. More
precisely, the six representatives correspond to the six permutations of the set
\{1,2,3\}
; in
reading order,
\id
,
(123)
,
(132)
,
(12)
,
(13)
,
(23)
.

4.3.
Local topological type of a loop on a surface of genus
g

Let us now define a notion of local topological type for loops on a compact
hyperbolic surface of genus
g
, for
g\geq 2
. We shall do this for loops on
the base surface
S_{g}
, which we endow with a fixed hyperbolic metric for the
purpose of defining regular neighbourhoods.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type. A loop
\gamma_{g}
on the
base surface
S_{g}
is said to
belong to the local topological
type

[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
if there exists a positive homeomorphism

\phi:S(\gamma_{g})\rightarrow S
such that the loops
\phi\circ\gamma_{g}

and
\gamma
are freely homotopic in
S
. In that case, we write

\gamma_{g}\in[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}^{g}
. We say that two loops
\gamma_{g}
,

\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
on
S_{g}
are
locally equivalent
, and write

\gamma_{g}\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.32297pt\hskip-4.32297pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{loc}$}\hskip-4.32297pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 4.32297pt}}$}\,\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
, if
\gamma_{g}
and
\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
belong to the same
local topological type.

It is clear that the definition does not depend on the choice of the representative
(S,\gamma)
in
the local topological type. The fact that it does not depend on the choice of representative in the
free-homotopy classes
\gamma_{g}
and
\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
either is a consequence of
4.3
.

Example 4.10
.

The loops on the base surface
S_{g}
belonging to the local type “simple” are exactly all
simple loops on
S_{g}
.
Indeed, take
\gamma_{g}\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
.

•

If
\gamma_{g}
is simple, then its regular neighbourhood is a
cylinder. Because
\gamma_{g}
is not contractible,
S(\gamma_{g})

is exactly this cylinder, and then
\gamma_{g}
is its core. It
directly follows that
\gamma_{g}
belong to the local type
“simple”.

•

If
\gamma_{g}
belongs to the local type “simple”, then
S(\gamma_{g})
is a topological
cylinder, and
\gamma_{g}
is homotopic to its core. As a consequence, the homotopy class

\gamma_{g}
admits a simple representative, which means that
\gamma_{g}
is simple.

4.4.
Comparison with mapping-class-group equivalence

4.10
shows that the notion of local equivalence and
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
-equivalence do not
coincide. Indeed, there are several distinct orbits of simple loops for
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
: non-separating
loops, and loops separating two surfaces on their left and right side, of respective signatures

(i,1)
and
(g-i,1)
for a
1\leq i\leq g-1
. This is a general fact: equivalence classes for

\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
are included in equivalence classes for
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.32297pt\hskip-4.32297pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{loc}$}\hskip-4.32297pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 4.32297pt}}
, as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\gamma_{g}
,
\gamma_{g}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
. If
\gamma_{g}\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 7.9515pt\hskip-7.9515pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{MCG}$}\hskip-7.9515pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.1988pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 7.9515pt}}$}\,\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
, then

\gamma_{g}\,\raisebox{-2.84526pt}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.32297pt\hskip-4.32297pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{loc}$}\hskip-4.32297pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 4.32297pt}}$}\,\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We assume that there exists a homeomorphism
\phi:S_{g}\rightarrow S_{g}
sending
\gamma_{g}
on

\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
. Then, by definition of the filled surface associated to a loop, the image of

S(\gamma_{g})
by
\phi
is isotopic to
S(\gamma_{g}^{\prime})
. As a consequence, by composition, there
exists a homeomorphism
\psi:S_{g}\rightarrow S_{g}
sending
S(\gamma_{g})
on
S(\gamma_{g}^{\prime})
and

\gamma_{g}
on
\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
, which implies our claim.
∎

The choice of the name “local equivalence” comes from the fact that this notion only encaptures
the topology of the filled surface
S(\gamma_{g})
and of the loop
\gamma_{g}
within it, but does no
say anything about the topology of the complement
S_{g}\setminus S(\gamma_{g})
. To the contrary, if
two loops
\gamma_{g},\gamma_{g}^{\prime}
are in the same
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
-orbit, then the topologies of

S_{g}\setminus S(\gamma_{g})
and
S_{g}\setminus S(\gamma_{g}^{\prime})
need to be “the same”, in a way which
will be made more precise in the following section.

4.5.
Realisations of a filling type

In order to describe more precisely the way that a local equivalence class
[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}^{g}
is
partitioned in
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
-orbits, we introduce the following notion.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
S
be a filling type, and
g\geq 2
. We call
realisation of
S

in
S_{g}
any pair
R=(\vec{I},\vec{g})
, where:

•

\vec{I}
is a partition of
\partial S=\{1,\ldots,n_{S}\}
into

q\geq 1
non-empty sets
I_{1},\ldots,I_{q}
, numbered such that

j\mapsto\min I_{j}
is an increasing function;

•

\vec{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{q})
is a vector of non-negative integers;

•

for any
1\leq j\leq q
,
\chi_{j}:=2-2g_{j}-n_{j}\leq 0
, where
n_{j}:=\#I_{j}
;

•

for
\chi_{g}:=\chi(S_{g})=2-2g
and
\chi_{S}:=2-2g_{S}-n_{S}
, we have

(4.1)

\chi_{S}+\sum_{j=1}^{q}\chi_{j}=\chi_{g}.

The set of realisations of
S
in
S_{g}
is denoted as
R_{g}(S)
.

Remark 4.13
.

We observe that, since
\vec{I}
is a partition of
\partial S
, we have that

\sum_{j=1}^{q}n_{j}=n_{S}
, and we can therefore rewrite
Eq.

4.1
as

(4.2)

\sum_{j=1}^{q}g_{j}+g_{S}+n_{S}-q=g.

Notation 4.14
.

Let
S
be a filling type,
g\geq 2
, and
R=(\vec{I},\vec{g})\in R_{g}(S)
.
We can construct a compact surface
S_{g}(S,R)
by gluing, for all

1\leq j\leq q
, a surface of signature
(g_{j},n_{j})
on the boundary
components of
S
belonging in
I_{j}
. By
Eq.

4.1
, the
resulting surface is a compact surface of genus
g
, and hence there exists a
positive homeomorphism
\phi_{g,S,R}
from
S_{g}(S,R)
to
S_{g}
.

Remark 4.15
.

Note that, if
(g_{j},n_{j})=(0,2)
, then the surface we glue is a cylinder, which corresponds to
gluing two boundary components of
S
together. In all other cases, we glue an honest surface of
negative Euler characteristic to
\partial S
.

Notation 4.16
.

For any local type
T=[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, we fix a choice of representative
(S,\gamma^{T})
, meaning
that
\gamma^{T}
is a fixed loop on the topological surface
S
, in the local equivalence class

T
. Then, for any
g\geq 2
and any realisation
R\in R_{g}(S)
, we denote as
\mathrm{Orb}_{g}^{T}(R)
the
orbit of
\phi_{g,S,R}(\gamma^{T})
for the action of
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
.

We furthermore denote as
\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)
the multi-curve

\phi_{g,S,R}(\partial S)
on
S_{g}
, with the numbering and orientation of

\partial S
, and the following convention. For any index

j\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
such that
I_{j}=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
with
i<i^{\prime}
and
g_{j}=2

(if such an index exists), then we do not include the
i^{\prime}
-th component of

\partial S
in
\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)
. We do this so that all components of

\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)
are distinct, because when
(g_{j},n_{j})=(0,2)
, we glue a
cylinder to
\partial S
, so its
i
-th and
i^{\prime}
-th boundary components become
homotopic.

Remark 4.17
.

The orbit
\mathrm{Orb}_{g}^{T}(R)
does not depend on the choice of the
homeomorphism
\phi_{g,S,R}
. However, it does a priori depend on the choice
of the representative
\gamma
of the local type, which is why we fixed such a
representative.

It is clear with these notations that, for any loop
\gamma_{g}\in T^{g}
, there exists a realisation

R
of
S
in
S_{g}
such that
\gamma_{g}\in\mathrm{Orb}_{g}^{T}(R)
. So, realisations do enumerate all possible
embeddings of a local type in a surface of genus
g
. The following immediate lemma allows to count
the number of appearances of each
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
-orbit whilst listing all realisations
R
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
T
be a local type of filling type
S
, and
g\geq 2
. If

R=(\vec{I},\vec{g})
and
R^{\prime}=(\vec{I}^{\prime},\vec{g}^{\prime})
are two realisations of

S
in
S_{g}
, we have that
\mathrm{Orb}_{g}^{T}(R)=\mathrm{Orb}_{g}^{T}(R^{\prime})
if and only if the
following conditions are satisfied:

•

there exists a positive homeomorphism
\phi:S\rightarrow S
, possibly permuting the
boundary components of
S
, such that
\phi(\gamma^{T})
and
\gamma^{T}
are freely homotopic in

S
;

•

for all
1\leq j\leq q
,
\phi
sends the components of
\partial S
lying in
I_{j}
on the
components lying in
I_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}
for a
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq q
such that
g_{j}=g_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}
.

We notice that, whenever
\phi
acts trivially on
\partial S
, these conditions imply that
R=R^{\prime}

thanks to our numbering convention for
\vec{I}
. As a consequence, several realisations can get
associated to the same orbit, but only in the case where the loop
\gamma^{T}
has non-trivial
symmetries in
S
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.19
.

Let
T
be a local topological type of filling type
S
. We call

multiplicity of
T
the cardinal
m(T)
of the subgroup of permutations
of
\partial S=\{1,\ldots,n_{S}\}
induced by positive homeomorphisms

\phi:S\rightarrow S
such that
\gamma^{T}
and
\phi(\gamma^{T})
are homotopic.

Note that, while the subgroup in
4.19
depends on the choice of
the representative
\gamma^{T}
, its cardinality does not, so the multiplicity of
a local type is well-defined.

Example 4.20
.

The image of the figure-eight by all permutations of
\{1,2,3\}
is
represented in
Fig.

7
; we see that, in this case,

m(T)=1
. However, in the third example of
Fig.

5
, the
multiplicity is
2
because the permutation
(13)
stabilises the loop

\gamma
.

5.
Average over a local type

The aim of this section is to define the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
of a test function over a local type

T
, and to provide a method to express and estimate it. In particular, we shall:

•

provide a formula for
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
in terms of Weil–Petersson volumes in

5.5
;

•

prove it can be written as a density in
5.9
;

•

expand it in powers of
1/g
in
5.14
.

5.1.
Definition

Let us define the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
of a test function
F
over a local topological type
T
.

Definition 5.1
.

We call
admissible test function
any measurable function

F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
that is either compactly supported, or
non-negative.

Remark 5.2
.

Actually, the results in this paper hold for a more general class of test
functions, we only need to assume that they decay sufficiently fast at
infinity so that all quantities mentioned converge.

The invariance of local types by action of the mapping-class group allows us to make the following
definition.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
T
be a local topological type. For any admissible test function
F
, any
g\geq 2
, we
define the
T
-average
of
F
over surfaces of genus
g
to be

(5.1)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma_{g}\in T^{g}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma_{g}))\Bigg].

We notice that this coincides with the definition of
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}
for
the local type “simple”. Obviously, we have that

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\sum_{T\text{ local type}}\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}.

Remark 5.4
.

An interesting benefit from splitting the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
by local
topological type rather than
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
-orbit is that the set of local types is
fixed and independent of the genus
g
, whilst the number of
\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
-orbits of
loops grows as a function of
g
.

5.2.
Integration formula for averages over a local type

We are now ready to write an integration formula for the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
,
for any local type
T
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
T
be a local topological type of filling type
S
. Then, for any
g\geq 3
, any admissible
test function
F
,

(5.2)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}}\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]\,\phi_{g}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

where

(5.3)

\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]:=\frac{1}{V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{Orb}_{S}(\gamma^{T})}F(\ell_{Y}(\alpha))\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y)

with
\mathrm{Orb}_{S}(\gamma^{T})
the orbit of
\gamma^{T}
for the action of the mapping-class group of
S

(fixing the boundary components of
S
), and

(5.4)

\phi_{g}^{S}(\mathbf{x}):=x_{1}\ldots x_{n_{S}}\frac{V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}{V_{g}}\sum_{R\in R_{g}(S)}\prod_{j=1}^{q}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}(\mathbf{x}^{(j)}),

with the convention that
V_{0,2}(x,y):=\frac{1}{x}\,\delta(x-y)
where
\delta
is the Dirac
delta distribution, and
\mathbf{x}^{(j)}=(x_{i},i\in I_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}
.

Example 5.6
.

The multiplicity of the type “simple” is
1
, and the definition of

\phi_{g}^{(0,2)}(x,y)
contains a
\delta(x-y)
, so we shall only compute it on
the diagonal
x=y
. We have

(5.5)

\phi_{g}^{(0,2)}(x,x)=\frac{x}{V_{g}}\left(V_{g-1,2}(x,x)+\sum_{i=1}^{g-1}V_{i,1}(x)V_{g-i,1}(x)\right).

Furthermore, the conditional expectation defined in
Eq.

5.3
is equal to

F(x)
, because the length of a simple loop and the boundary geodesics of the cylinder it fills
coincide. We therefore recover the expression for
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}
that was obtained in

Section

3.2
.

Example 5.7
.

Let
\gamma
be a loop filling the pair of pants
P
(i.e. our fixed surface
of signature
(0,3)
). Then, the length of
\gamma
is an analytic function of
the lengths of the three boundary components
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
of
P
, which
we shall denote as
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We
then have that

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{[P,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}}}=\frac{1}{m(\gamma)}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}F(\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}))\,\phi_{g}^{P}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x_{3}

where
m(\gamma)\in\{1,2,3,6\}
is the number of permutations of
\{1,2,3\}
stabilising
the homotopy class of
\gamma
, and

\phi_{g}^{P}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}{V_{g}}\left[V_{g-2,3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})+\sum_{g_{1}+g_{2}+g_{3}=g}V_{g_{1},1}(x_{1})V_{g_{2},1}(x_{2})V_{g_{3},1}(x_{3})\right.\\
\left.+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}\\
=\{1,2,3\}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\delta(x_{i_{1}}-x_{i_{2}})}{x_{i_{1}}}V_{g-1,1}(x_{i_{3}})+\sum_{i=1}^{g-2}V_{i,2}(x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}})V_{g-i-1,1}(x_{i_{3}})\right)\right].

Remark 5.8
.

In the two previous examples, the conditional expectation (
5.3
) has a
very simple expression because the length of the loop
\gamma
is entirely determined by the
lengths of the boundary components of the surface it fills. However, in all cases but these ones,
the moduli space of the filled surface is a space of
>0
dimension on which an average takes
place, and the sum over the mapping-class group is not reduced to an element. Understanding the
behaviour of the function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]
,
for a general local type
T
, is a significant challenge which is postponed to a later article.

Proof of
5.5
.

By definition,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\in T^{g}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg]=\frac{1}{m(T)}\,\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{(Y,\alpha)\in\mathrm{Orb}_{g}(S,\gamma^{T})}F(\ell_{X}(\alpha))\Bigg]

where the sum runs over the orbit of the pair
(S,\gamma^{T})
for the action of

\mathrm{MCG}_{g}
, and the coefficient
1/m(T)
removes the multiplicities arising from
the arbitrary choice of numbering of the boundary components of
S
.
We then split the orbit according to realisations:

(5.6)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\frac{1}{m(T)}\sum_{R\in R_{g}(S)}\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{Orb}_{g}^{T}(R)}F(\ell_{X}(\alpha))\Bigg].

The definition (
5.3
) for the function

\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}\ni\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]
makes sense
as soon as
(g_{S},n_{S})
is different from
(0,2)
and
(0,3)
, because the average over the orbit
of
\gamma^{T}
for the mapping class group of
S
is
\mathrm{MCG}(S)
-invariant, and hence a well-defined
quantity on
\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}
. The meaning of this quantity is made clear in
the Examples
5.6
and
5.7
when
(g_{S},n_{S})
is equal to
(0,2)
and

(0,3)
respectively.

Then, for each realisation
R\in R_{g}(S)
, we can rewrite the term associated
to
R
in
Eq.

5.6
as:

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{Orb}_{g}^{T}(R)}F(\ell_{X}(\alpha))\Bigg]=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\Psi_{T,R}^{\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)}\right]

where
\Psi_{T,R}^{\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)}
is the geometric function obtained from the
multi-curve
\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)
and the function

\Psi_{T,R}\,:\,(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k}})\mapsto\mathbb{E}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]

with
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the length vector obtained by completing
(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k}})
with
the identifications from the missing components of
\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)
corresponding to cylinders
(i.e. indices such that
(g_{j},n_{j})=(0,2)
). We can apply Mirzakhani’s integration formula to
compute the average of this geometric function, and obtain that

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\Psi_{T,R}^{\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{k}}\prod_{(g_{j},n_{j})\neq(0,2)}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})\,x_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i_{1}}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}x_{i_{k}}.

We observe that our definition of
V_{0,2}
allows us to rewrite this integral as

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\Psi_{T,R}^{\Gamma_{g}^{S}(R)}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n_{S}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}x_{n_{S}}

which then allows us to conclude.
∎

5.3.
Writing of the average as a density

Let us now justify that the averages
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
can be written as densities
against the Lebesgue measure.

Proposition 5.9
.

For any local type
T
, there exists a unique continuous function

V_{g}^{T}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that, for any admissible test
function
F
,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)V_{g}^{T}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

Definition 5.10
.

We call
V_{g}^{T}
the
volume function
associated with local type
T
on surfaces of genus

g
.

Remark 5.11
.

By the collar lemma (see e.g.
[
7
, Theorem 4.2.2]
), the length of any non-simple
closed geodesic on a compact hyperbolic surface is greater than
2\argcosh(3)
. In particular,
for any local type
T
other than “simple” and any
g\geq 2
, the volume function
V_{g}^{T}
is
identically equal to
0
on
[0,2\argcosh(1)]
. In this article, we will focus mostly on the
behaviour of
V_{g}^{T}
at infinity.

The proof relies on the two following lemmas.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
S
be a filling type. We define

\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star}:=\{(\mathbf{x},Y)\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}},\,Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})\}.

Then,
\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star}
is a real-analytic manifold that can be
identified with
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}\times(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R})^{3g_{S}-3+n_{S}}

through Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates, and the measure

\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\star}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(\mathbf{x},Y):=\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\times\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y)

is the Lebesgue measure in these coordinates. Furthermore, for any filling
loop
\gamma
on
S
, the function

\begin{cases}\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}\\
(\mathbf{x},Y)&\mapsto\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\end{cases}

is a real-analytic function, which satisfies

(5.7)

\forall(\mathbf{x},Y)\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star},\quad\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\geq\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n_{S}}}{2}\cdot

Proof.

We refer to
[
7
, Section 6.3]
for a description of the
real-analytic structure of Teichmüller spaces. The inequality on the length
function is exactly
4.4
.
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a connected open set, and let
\lambda_{\Omega}

denote the Lebesgue measure on
\Omega
. For any non-constant real-analytic
function
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, if

(5.8)

\forall L>0,\quad\int_{\Omega}\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}(f(\mathbf{x}))\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\Omega}(\mathbf{x})<+\infty,

then, the push-forward of
\lambda_{\Omega}
under
f
admits a continuous
density. This statement also holds when pushing forward

v(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\Omega}(\mathbf{x})
for any continuous function

v:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, replacing
\lambda_{\Omega}
in
(
5.8
) with the measure
v(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\Omega}(\mathbf{x})
.

Proof.

First, we observe that under these hypotheses, the set
C\subset\Omega
of critical points of

f
has
0
-Lebesgue measure. Indeed, we prove by induction on the dimension
d
that, for any
real-analytic function
\tilde{f}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
not identically equal to
0
, the set of
zeros of
\tilde{f}
has
0
-Lebesgue measure. For
d=1
, this comes from the fact that zeros are
isolated. The induction from
d
to
d+1
uses the Fubini theorem. Our claim then follows by
applying this intermediate result to the partial derivatives of
f
.

Then, the Lebesgue measure on
\Omega
coincides with the Lebesgue measure restricted to

\Omega\setminus C
. We can cover
\Omega\setminus C
by a countable number of open sets

\Omega_{i}
, such that on each of those we can find a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{i}
such that

f\circ\varphi_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=x_{1}
. The push-forward of
\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\Omega_{i}}
, or

v(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\Omega_{i}}(\mathbf{x})
, then obviously has a continuous density.
∎

Proof of
5.9
.

Let
T
be a local type of filling type
S
. Let us first notice that, by

2.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
for any
L\geq 0
,

(5.9)

\langle\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}\rangle_{g}^{{T}}\leq\langle\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}\leq Cge^{L}<+\infty.

Now, we observe that the formula from
5.5
can be rewritten as

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\sum_{R\in R_{g}(S)}\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star}}F(\ell_{Y}(\gamma^{T}))\prod_{j=1}^{q}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n_{S}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\star}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)

and therefore, in order to prove our claim, it is enough to apply

5.13
to push forward the measure

\frac{1}{m(T)}\prod_{j=1}^{q}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n_{S}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\star}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)

under the function
(\mathbf{x},Y)\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star}\mapsto\ell_{Y}(\gamma^{T})
, for each
realisation
R\in R_{g}(S)
.

Provided that
R
is a realisation for which
(g_{j},n_{j})\neq(0,2)
for all
j
, the hypotheses
of the lemma are satisfied, thanks to
Eq.

5.9
and

5.12
. Indeed,
Eq.

5.7
implies that

(\mathbf{x},Y)\mapsto\ell_{Y}(\gamma^{T})
is not a constant function.

Let us now explain how to treat the case where some of the indices
1\leq j\leq q
satisfy

(g_{j},n_{j})=(0,2)
. Rather than applying
5.13
to the whole space

\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star}
, we apply it to the lower-dimensional subspace where

x_{i}=x_{i^{\prime}}
for every pair of indices
(i,i^{\prime})
such that
I_{j}=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
and
g_{j}=0
. Once
again, (
5.7
) implies the length function is also non-constant on this
new space, and we can conclude the same way.
∎

5.4.
Existence of an asymptotic expansion

Let us now prove that the averages
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
admit an asymptotic expansion in powers of
1/g
.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
T
be a local topological type of filling type
S
. There exists a unique
family of continuous functions
(f_{k}^{T})_{k\geq|\chi_{S}|}
such that, for
any
K\geq 0
,
\epsilon>0
, any large enough
g
,

\frac{V_{g}^{T}(\ell)}{V_{g}}=\sum_{k=|\chi_{S}|}^{K}\frac{f_{k}^{T}(\ell)}{g^{k}}+\mathcal{O}_{K,S,\epsilon}^{w}\left(\frac{\exp\left((1+\epsilon)\ell\right)}{g^{K+1}}\right).

Remark 5.15
.

We notice that the leading term of the asymptotic expansion of
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
has order

1/g^{|\chi_{S}|}
. In particular, in all cases but the local type “simple”, the leading order of

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
decays as
1/g
at least.

5.4.1.
Rank of a realisation

In order to compute asymptotic expansions in powers of
1/g
, it will be
convenient to introduce a notion of rank for a realisation, which corresponds to
the height at which it appears in the expansion in powers of
1/g
.

Definition 5.16
.

Let
S
be a filling type, and
g\geq 2
. We define the
rank
of a
realisation
(\vec{I},\vec{g})\in R_{g}(S)
by

r_{g}^{S}(\vec{I},\vec{g}):=|\chi_{g}|-\max_{1\leq j\leq q}|\chi_{j}|=|\chi_{S}|+\sum_{j\neq j_{+}}|\chi_{j}|

where
j_{+}
is an index in
\{1,\ldots,q\}
realising

\max\{|\chi_{j}|,1\leq j\leq q\}
.

Remark 5.17
.

The rank of a realisation is always greater than
|\chi_{S}|
. In particular, the only filling
type that can have realisations of rank
0
is the filling type
(0,2)
, corresponding to simple
loops.

Remark 5.18
.

Because cylinders, which have Euler characteristic
0
, are allowed in the definition of a
realisation, there exists several realisations of minimal rank
|\chi_{S}|
. Amongst them,
there is a special one that we call the
connected realisation
, obtained by taking
q=1
,

I_{1}=\partial S
and
g_{1}=g-g_{S}-n_{S}+1
. This is the only realisation for which the
complement of
S
in
S_{g}
is connected.

Example 5.19
.

Let us compute the ranks of all realisations of the filling type
(0,2)
.

•

The connected realisation
q=1
,
I_{1}=\{1,2\}
and
g_{1}=g-1
, has rank
0
.

•

Any other realisation satisfies
q=2
,
I_{1}=\{1\}
,
I_{2}=\{2\}
, and

g_{1},g_{2}\geq 1
such that
g_{1}+g_{2}=g
. The rank of such a realisation
is
2\min(g_{1},g_{2})-1\geq 1
.

Note that the Weil–Petersson volume that appears in Mirzakhani’s integration formula is

V_{g-1,2}/V_{g}\sim 1
for the connected realisation, and

V_{i,1}V_{g-i,1}/V_{g}\sim C(i)/g^{2i-1}
for realisations of fixed rank
2i-1
. The notion of
rank does correspond to the height of the realisation in the asymptotic expansion of

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}
in powers of
1/g
, as we intended.

The following lemma will allow to reduce ourselves to a bounded number of ranks
when computing the densities
\phi_{g}^{S}
introduced in
5.5
.

Lemma 5.20
.

For any filling type
S
, any integer
K\geq|\chi_{S}|
, any large enough

g
,

\frac{V_{g_{S},n_{S}}}{V_{g}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{I},\vec{g})\in R_{g}(S)\\
r_{g}^{S}(\vec{I},\vec{g})\geq K\end{subarray}}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq q\\
\chi_{j}<0\end{subarray}}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}=\mathcal{O}_{S,K}\left(\frac{1}{g^{K}}\right).

Proof of
5.20
.

We start by proving the result for the order
K=|\chi_{S}|
.
First, we observe that the factor
V_{g_{S},n_{S}}
is unimportant because

V_{g_{S},n_{S}}=\mathcal{O}_{S}\left(1\right)
. We split the quantity that we want to estimate
depending on the partition
\vec{I}
of
\partial S
. Because
\partial S
has

\mathcal{O}_{S}\left(1\right)
partitions, estimating each individual term is enough. For a large
enough
g
, the condition that
\sum_{j=1}^{q}g_{j}=g-g_{S}-n_{S}+q
implies
that at least one coefficient
g_{j}
is positive. We shall further split the
sum we want to estimate depending on the subset

J\subsetneq\{1,\ldots,q\}
of indices for which
(g_{j},n_{j})=(0,2)
,
because there are
\mathcal{O}_{S}\left(1\right)
such subsets. As a consequence, we are left to
bound for a fixed
\vec{I}
and
J\subsetneq\{1,\ldots,q\}
the sum

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{g}:(\vec{I},\vec{g})\in R_{g}(S)\\
\text{and }\chi_{j}=0\Leftrightarrow j\in J\end{subarray}}\hskip 2.0pt\prod_{j\notin J}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}.

We observe that the condition on the indices
\vec{g}
to obtain a realisation can be rewritten as

\sum_{j\notin J}(2g_{j}-2+n_{j})=2(g-g_{S})-n_{S}=2g^{\prime}-2+n_{S},

for
g^{\prime}:=g-g_{S}-n_{S}+1
, because the summand is equal to zero for indices in

J
.

By
[
28
, Lemma 3.2]
, for any
k\geq 1
,

n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}>0
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{k}n_{j}
and
n
have the same
parity, there exists
C=C(n,k,(n_{i})_{i})
such that, for any large enough
g
,

(5.10)

\sum_{(g_{i})_{i}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{k}V_{g_{i},n_{i}}\leq C\frac{V_{g,n}}{g^{k-1}}

where the sum runs over all families of integers
(g_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
such that

2g_{i}-2+n_{i}\geq 1
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(2g_{i}-2+n_{i})=2g-2+n
.
We apply this result with
k=q-\#J\geq 1
, the integers

(n_{j})_{j\notin J}
,
n=n_{S}
and the genus
g^{\prime}=g-g_{S}-n_{S}+1
. The parity
condition on the integers is true because
\sum_{j\notin J}n_{j}
has the same
parity as
\sum_{j=1}^{q}n_{j}=n_{S}
. We obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{g}:(\vec{I},\vec{g})\in R_{g}(S)\\
\text{and }\chi_{j}=0\Leftrightarrow j\in J\end{subarray}}\hskip 2.0pt\prod_{j\notin J}V_{g_{j},n_{j}}=\mathcal{O}_{S}\left(\frac{V_{g-g_{S}-n_{S}+1,n_{S}}}{(g-g_{S}-n_{S}+1)^{q-\#J-1}}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{S}\left(\frac{V_{g}}{g^{|\chi_{S}|}}\right)

because
q-\#J\geq 1
and
V_{g-g_{S}-n_{S}+1,n_{S}}=\mathcal{O}_{S}\left(V_{g}/g^{2g_{S}-2+n_{S}}\right)

by
[
27
, Lemma 3.2]
. This is exactly our claim for
K=|\chi_{S}|
.

Let us now prove the result when
K>|\chi_{S}|
. The connected
realisation is the only realisation for which
q=1
, and it has rank

|\chi_{S}|<K
; as a consequence, all realisations in the sum now satisfy

q\geq 2
. Let us pick a realisation
(\vec{I},\vec{g})
of rank
\geq K
,
and let
j_{+}
denote an index such that

|\chi_{j_{+}}|=\max_{j}|\chi_{j}|
. By definition of the rank,

2g-2g_{j_{+}}-n_{j_{+}}\geq K
. On the other hand,

|\chi_{j_{+}}|\geq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}|\chi_{j}|\geq\frac{2(g-g_{S})+n_{S}}{n_{S}}\underset{g\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}+\infty

and hence, provided
g
is large enough,

2g_{j_{+}}-2+n_{j_{+}}\geq K
.

Let us single out the term
V_{g_{j_{+}},n_{j_{+}}}
in the quantity we want to estimate. We use the
previous method to bound the summation over all possible
(g_{j})_{j\neq j_{+}}
, in which we now
include
V_{g_{S},n_{S}}
, observing that

\displaystyle(2g_{S}-2+n_{S})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq q\\
j\neq j_{+}\end{subarray}}(2g_{j}-2+n_{j})=2(g-g_{j_{+}})-n_{j_{+}}=2g^{\prime\prime}-2+n_{j_{+}}

for
g^{\prime\prime}:=g-g_{j_{+}}-n_{j_{+}}+1
. We therefore obtain that the sum we wish to
estimate is bounded by a constant depending only on
S
times

(5.11)

\sum_{q=2}^{n_{S}}\sum_{j_{+}=1}^{q}\sum_{n_{j_{+}}=1}^{n_{S}-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{j_{+}}:\,2g_{j_{+}}-2+n_{j_{+}}\geq K\\
\text{and }2g-2g_{j_{+}}-n_{j_{+}}\geq K\end{subarray}}V_{g_{j_{+}},n_{j_{+}}}V_{g-g_{j_{+}}-n_{j_{+}}+1,n_{j_{+}}}.

But
[
27
, Corollary 3.7]
states that for any
K,n\geq 1
and
any large enough
g
,

(5.12)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}\\
g_{1}+g_{2}+n-1=g\\
2g_{i}-2+n\geq K\end{subarray}}V_{g_{1},n}V_{g_{2},n}=\mathcal{O}_{K,n}\left(\frac{V_{g}}{g^{K}}\right).

This allows us to conclude that (
5.11
) is

\mathcal{O}_{S,K}\left(V_{g}/g^{K}\right)
, as announced.
∎

5.4.2.
Asymptotic expansion of the function
\phi_{g}^{S}

The key ingredient to proving
5.14
is the following
asymptotic expansion on the function
\phi_{g}^{S}
associated to a filling type

S
.

Proposition 5.21
.

For any filling type
S
, there exists a unique family of distributions
(\psi_{k}^{S})_{k\geq|\chi_{S}|}

satisfying the following. For any
K\geq 0
, there exists a constant
c_{K}^{S}
such that, for any
large enough
g
, any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}
,

(5.13)

\phi_{g}^{S}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k=|\chi_{S}|}^{K}\frac{\psi_{k}^{S}(\mathbf{x})}{g^{k}}+\mathcal{O}_{K,S}^{w}\left(\frac{(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1)^{c_{K}^{S}}}{g^{K+1}}\exp{\left(\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n_{S}}}{2}\right)}\right).

Furthermore, for all
k
, the function
\psi_{k}^{S}
can be uniquely written as a
linear combination of distributions of the form

(5.14)

\prod_{i\in I_{0}}x_{i}^{2k_{i}+1}\prod_{i\in I_{+}}x_{i}^{2k_{i}+1}\cosh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)\prod_{i\in I_{-}}x_{i}^{2k_{i}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)\prod_{(i,i^{\prime})\in J}x_{i}\delta(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}}),

where
I_{0},I_{+},I_{-}
are disjoint subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n_{S}\}
,

(k_{i})_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{+}\cup I_{-}}
are integers, and
J
is a set of
disjoint pairs of distinct integers outside of
I_{0}\cup I_{+}\cup I_{-}
.

The proof of this proposition is similar to the proof of
3.3
.

Proof of
5.21
.

It comes as an easy consequence of
5.20
and the upper
bound (
2.7
) on Weil–Petersson volumes that it is
equivalent to prove this expansion for the full function
\phi_{g}^{S}
or its
restriction
\phi_{g}^{S,K}
to all realisations of rank
=K
for all

K\geq 0
. We shall therefore do the latter. The number of possibilities for
the partition
\vec{I}
of
\partial S
is fixed and independent of
g
, so it
is furthermore enough to prove the result for the function

\phi_{g}^{S,K,\vec{I}}
restricted to realisations of partition
\vec{I}
, for
every fixed partition
\vec{I}
.

Let
\vec{g}
be a vector of integers such that
(\vec{I},\vec{g})
is a
realisation of rank
K
of
S
in
S_{g}
. Let
j_{+}
be an integer in

\{1,\ldots,q\}
realising the maximum
\max_{j}|\chi_{j}|
. The
definition of the rank implies that, for all
j\neq j_{+}
,

|\chi_{j}|\leq K
. But the fact that
(\vec{I},\vec{g})
is a realisation in
S_{g}

means that

\sum_{j=1}^{q}|\chi_{j}|=2(g-g_{S})-n_{S}\underset{g\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}+\infty

and hence there exists an index
g_{0}=g_{0}(S,K)
such that
|\chi_{j_{+}}|>K
if

g\geq g_{0}
. In particular, if
g\geq g_{0}
, the maximal index
j_{+}
is a uniquely defined index
in
\{1,\ldots,q\}
. Hence we can further restrict ourselves to studying the restriction of the
function
\phi_{g}^{S,K,\vec{I}}
to all realisations for which the maximal index is exactly
j_{+}
,
for every fixed
1\leq j_{+}\leq q
. For the sake of readability, we shall only treat the case

j_{+}=1
, the others are similar.

For such a realisation to exist, by definition of the rank and of
R_{g}(S)
, we need to assume that

K+n_{1}
is an even integer
2m
, and then the genus
g_{1}
of the surface attached to the
boundary components of
S
lying in
I_{1}
is determined to be equal to
g-m
. The quantity we
need to estimate can therefore be rewritten as

(5.15)

\frac{(\prod_{i\in I_{1}}x_{i})V_{g-m,n_{1}}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)})}{V_{g}}\cdot V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{2},\ldots,g_{q}\\
\sum_{j=2}^{q}|\chi_{j}|=K\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=2}^{q}\Big(\prod_{i\in I_{j}}x_{i}\Big)V_{g_{j},n_{j}}(\mathbf{x}^{(j)}).

The only term in this equation that depends on the genus
g
is the ratio

\frac{(\prod_{i\in I_{1}}x_{i})V_{g-m,n_{1}}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)})}{V_{g}}=\frac{V_{g-m,n_{1}}}{V_{g}}\frac{(\prod_{i\in I_{1}}x_{i})V_{g-m,n_{1}}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)})}{V_{g-m,n_{1}}}

which we can expand in powers of
1/g
using
2.9
and the expansions
(
2.4
) and (
2.5
) by Mirzakhani and
Zograf. The dependency of the terms of this expansion as a function of
\mathbf{x}^{(1)}
is made explicit
in
2.9
. The conclusion then follows directly, because
V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})
is
a polynomial function of
\mathbf{x}
, even in each variable, while the

(\prod_{i\in I_{j}}x_{i})V_{g_{j},n_{j}}(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})
for
j\geq 2
are polynomial functions in

(x_{i})_{i\in I_{j}}
odd in each variable if
(g_{j},n_{j})\neq(0,2)
, or equal to

x_{i}\delta(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}})
if
(g_{j},n_{j})=(0,2)
and
I_{j}=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
.

The bound on the remainder comes from the fact that, by

2.9
, the error term in the approximation of order
K
of

V_{g-m,n_{1}}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)})/V_{g-m,n_{1}}
is

\mathcal{O}_{K,n_{1}}\left(\frac{(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1)^{3K+1}}{g^{K+1}}\,\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in I_{1}}x_{i}\Big)\right)

which, once multiplied by the other volume polynomials and Dirac masses,
yields a remainder that is of the claimed form.
∎

5.4.3.
Proof of
5.14

In order to go from
5.21
to
5.14
, let us introduce
the conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{\mathrm{all}}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]:=\frac{1}{V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\sum_{\alpha\text{ filling }S}F(\ell_{Y}(\alpha))\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y)

so that

\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{\mathrm{all}}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]=\sum_{T\text{ filling }S}\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}].

We shall prove the following uniform upper bound on this quantity, a straightforward consequence of
Wu–Xue’s counting result (
2.3
).

Lemma 5.22
.

For any filling type
S
, any
\epsilon>0
, any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}
and
any admissible function
F
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{\mathrm{all}}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]=\mathcal{O}_{S,\epsilon}\left(\|F(\ell)\exp\left((1+\epsilon)\ell\right)\|_{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{1+\epsilon/4}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}x_{i}\right)\right).

Proof of
5.22
.

Let
Y\in\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})
. We recall from
4.4

that, if
\gamma
is a closed geodesic filling
S
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}x_{i}\leq 2\ell_{Y}(\gamma)
. It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\text{ fills }S}\exp\left(-(1+\epsilon)\,\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\#\{\gamma\text{ filling }S\,:\,k\leq\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\leq k+1\}\exp\left(-\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)k-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}x_{i}\right).

2.3
applied with
L=k+1
and
\eta=\epsilon/4
states that, for any

k
, the cardinal above is

\mathcal{O}_{S,\epsilon}\left(\exp\left(k-\frac{1-\epsilon/4}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}x_{i}\right)\right).

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\text{ fills }S}\exp\left(-(1+\epsilon)\,\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{S,\epsilon}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon}{2}k-\frac{1+\epsilon/4}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}x_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{S,\epsilon}\left(\exp\left(-\frac{1+\epsilon/4}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}x_{i}\right)\right).

We conclude by writing that, for any
Y\in\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\text{ fills }S}F(\ell_{Y}(\gamma))

\displaystyle\leq\|F(\ell)\exp\left((1+\epsilon)\ell\right)\|_{\infty}\sum_{\gamma\text{ fills }S}\exp(-(1+\epsilon)\ell_{Y}(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{S,\epsilon}\left(\|F(\ell)\exp\left((1+\epsilon)\ell\right)\|_{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{1+\epsilon/4}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}x_{i}\right)\right)

and integrating this uniform bound over
Y\in\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})
.
∎

We are now ready to prove
5.14
.

Proof.

For a
k\geq|\chi_{S}|
, inspired by
5.5
, we define

a_{k}^{T}[F]:=\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}}\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]\,\psi_{k}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

By the results of
Section

5.3
, there exists a unique continuous
density
f_{k}^{T}
such that

a_{k}^{T}[F]=\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)f_{k}^{T}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell

for any
F
.
In order to conclude, we simply need to weakly bound the error

\mathrm{Err}_{g,K}^{T}(\ell):=\frac{V_{g}^{T}(\ell)}{V_{g}}-\sum_{k=|\chi_{S}|}^{K}\frac{f_{k}^{T}(\ell)}{g^{k}}\cdot

Let
F
be an admissible test function. Then, by definition,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\,\mathrm{Err}_{g,K}^{T}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell=\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]\,\mathrm{err}_{g,K}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

where
\mathrm{err}_{g,K}^{S}
is the remainder of the asymptotic expansion of order
K
of

\phi_{g}^{S}
. We observe that
m(T)\geq 1
and

|\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]|\leq\mathbb{E}_{S}[|F|^{\mathrm{all}}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]

because any
\gamma^{\prime}
in the orbit of
\gamma
for
\mathrm{MCG}(S)
fills
S
. It
then follows, by the bound on
\mathrm{err}_{g,K}^{S}
from
5.21
,

5.22
and by definition of
\mathcal{O}^{w}
, that there
exists a constant
C_{\epsilon,S,K}
such that

\left|\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\,\mathrm{Err}_{g,K}^{T}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\right|\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,S,K}}{g^{K+1}}\|F(\ell)\exp((1+\epsilon)\ell)\|_{\infty}.

This implies the claimed weak bound on
\mathrm{Err}_{g,K}^{T}
, by taking for every
L>0

as an admissible test function
F
the function

\ell\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{sign}(\mathrm{Err}_{g,K}^{T}(\ell))}{\exp((1+\epsilon)\ell)}\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}(\ell).

∎

Remark 5.23
.

Let
T=[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type.
The proof actually yields that, for any
k\geq 0
, the
k
-th term
f_{k}^{T}
of the asymptotic
expansion of
V_{g}^{T}/V_{g}
can be computed using the following relation, true for any admissible
function
F
:

(5.16)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\,f_{k}^{T}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}}\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]\,\psi_{k}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

(5.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m(T)}\frac{1}{V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\int_{\mathcal{T}_{g_{S},n_{S}}^{\star}}F(\ell_{Y}(\gamma))\,\psi_{k}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y)

where
\psi_{k}^{S}
is the
k
-th term of the asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{g}^{S}
(see

5.21
).

5.5.
Useful generalisations

We briefly explain here how to extend the notations and results of Sections

4
and
5
to broader settings.

A first useful observation is that we can extend the definition of local
topological types to types
T=[S,\vec{\gamma}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, where
\vec{\gamma}
is
a
multi-loop
filling
S
, i.e. an ordered family

(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m})
of closed loops such that all connected
components of
S\setminus(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\gamma_{i})
are disks or annular
regions around boundary components of
S
. For any family of admissible test
functions
F_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, we can
naturally define

\langle F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\rangle_{g}^{{T}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m})\in T^{g}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}F_{i}(\ell_{X}(\alpha_{i}))\Bigg].

Note that, more generally, we could also have averaged any measurable function

\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with compact support (i.e. not necessarily a product of
m
functions).

In this new setting, the integration formula proven in

5.5
remains identical, except that the multiplicity
constant
m(T)
now counts permutations of
\partial S
stabilising
the multi-loop
\vec{\gamma}
, and we now have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{S}[(F_{1},\ldots,F_{m})^{T}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\sum_{(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m})\in\mathrm{Orb}_{S}(\vec{\gamma})}\prod_{i=1}^{m}F_{i}(\ell_{Y}(\alpha_{i}))\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y).

Notably, the function
\phi_{g}^{S}
present in the integration formula is
unchanged. We can therefore write the average
\langle F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
as an integral
against a generalised volume function
V_{g}^{T}
, now a function of
m
variables:

\langle F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}F_{i}(x_{i})\right)V_{g}^{T}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

The ratio
V_{g}^{T}/V_{g}
can be expanded in powers of
1/g
by replacing the
function
\phi_{g}^{S}
by its asymptotic expansion as in the case of one single
loop.

Actually, the multi-loop
(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m})
does not necessarily need to fill the
surface
S
for the definition of the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
to make sense. A case that can be
interesting to consider is the case where
m:=n_{S}
and

(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n_{S}}):=\partial S
.

Another useful generalisation consists in considering families of local types

\vec{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{m})
, where for all
i
,
T_{i}=[S^{i},\gamma_{i}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
with
S^{i}=(g_{i},n_{i})
. We then say a family of loops

(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m})
on
S_{g}
belongs to the local type
T
if:

•

the filled surfaces
S(\alpha_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq m
are

disjoint
;

•

there exists a family of positive homeomorphisms

\phi_{i}:S(\alpha_{i})\rightarrow S^{i}
such that the loops

\phi\circ\alpha_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}
are homotopic in
S^{i}
.

We then can write for a family of admissible functions

F_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\langle F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\rangle_{g}^{{\vec{T}}}=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m})\in\vec{T}^{g}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}F_{i}(\ell_{X}(\alpha_{i}))\Bigg]=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}F(x_{i})\,V_{g}^{\vec{T}}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

for a generalised density
V_{g}^{\vec{T}}
, which can be computed using

\langle F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\rangle_{g}^{{\vec{T}}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{m}}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{m(T_{i})}\mathbb{E}_{S_{i}}[F^{T_{i}}\,|\,\ell(\partial S^{i})=\mathbf{x}^{(i)}]\right)\,\phi_{g}^{\vec{S}}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

Here
\phi_{g}^{\vec{S}}
is a function enumerating all possible realisations of

S^{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup S^{m}
in
S_{g}
(as in the case
m=1
, we allow realisations containing
cylinders, i.e. it is possible to glue a boundary component of
S^{i}
to a boundary component of

S^{i^{\prime}}
for
i\neq i^{\prime}
, unless they are both cylinders). In this situation, the function

\phi_{g}^{\vec{S}}
can once again be expanded in powers of
1/g
, which yields an asymptotic
expansion of
V_{g}^{\vec{T}}/V_{g}
in powers of
1/g
.

6.
Average over all geodesics

Let us now extend some of the observations of
Section

5
to
the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
over
all
primitive closed geodesics.

6.1.
Expression of the average and writing as a density

Adding all local topological types leads to the following statement, extending

5.5
to
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
.

Theorem 6.1
.

For any
g\geq 3
, any admissible test function
F
,

(6.1)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\sum_{S\text{ filling type}}\frac{1}{n_{S}!}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}}\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{\mathrm{all}}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]\,\phi_{g}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

where we recall that

(6.2)

\mathbb{E}_{S}[F^{\mathrm{all}}\,|\,\ell(\partial S)=\mathbf{x}]=\frac{1}{V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\sum_{\alpha\text{ filling }S}F(\ell_{Y}(\alpha))\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{S},n_{S},\mathbf{x}}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y).

We also observe that the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
can be written as a density.

Proposition 6.2
.

There exists a unique continuous function

V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that, for any
admissible test function
F
,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

The proofs of both these statements are the same that in the case of one local
type.

6.2.
Asymptotic expansion

Let us now prove the following result, which is an expansion for the average

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
obtained by summing over
all
closed geodesics.

Theorem 6.3
.

There exists a unique family of continuous functions

(f_{k}^{\mathrm{all}})_{k\geq 0}
such that, for any
A\geq 1
,

K\geq 0
,
\epsilon>0
, and any large enough
g
,

(6.3)

\frac{V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)}{V_{g}}\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}(\ell)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{f_{k}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)}{g^{k}}\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}(\ell)+\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon,K,A}^{w}\left(\frac{\exp{((1+\epsilon)\ell)}}{g^{K+1}}\right).

The proof of this result is very similar to the proof of
5.14
. The only
difference is that we would
a priori
need to sum over all possible filling types
S
in order
to expand
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
, which would be an issue since all constants in
5.14

depend on
S
. It is in order to address this difficulty that we restrict ourselves to examining
geodesics of length
\leq A\log(g)
, by including
\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}
in

Eq.

6.3
. The following lemma will then be used to limit the summation over
filling types
S
to a finite number of terms.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
N\geq 0
. For any large enough
g
, there exists a set

\mathcal{A}_{g}^{N}\subset\mathcal{M}_{g}
such that

1-\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{g}^{N}\right)\leq 1/g^{N}
and, for any
X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{N}
,
for any closed geodesic
\gamma
on
X
,

|\chi_{S(\gamma)}|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma)}{\log g}\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma)}{\log g}+N\right)\right).

We shall postpone the proof of this lemma to
Section

6.3
.

Proof of
6.3
.

Let
K\geq 0
. We apply
6.4
to
N=K+2
, and obtain a
set
\mathcal{A}_{g}^{K+2}
of probability greater than
1-g^{-K-2}
such that,
for all
X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{K+2}
,

\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq A\log(g)\quad\Rightarrow\quad|\chi_{S(\gamma)}|\leq M

for
M
a constant depending only on
A
and
K
.
We shall now write

\displaystyle\langle F\,\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{S}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ of filling type }S\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq A\log(g)\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\langle F\,\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}\rangle_{g}^{{\leq M}}+\langle F\,\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}\rangle_{g}^{{>M}}

where the two averages are defined by summing over filling types
S
such that

|\chi_{S}|\leq M
or
|\chi_{S}|>M
respectively.

In order to bound the second average, we observe that

\langle F\,\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}\rangle_{g}^{{>M}}=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[(1-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{K+2}}(X))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S:|\chi_{S}|>M\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ of filling type }S\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq A\log(g)\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg]

because the sum is identically equal to zero if
X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{K+2}
.
We prove using
2.1
that, for any

X\in\mathcal{M}_{g}
,

\Bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S:|\chi_{S}|>M\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ of filling type }S\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq A\log(g)\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg|\leq\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(X)}|F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))|=\mathcal{O}\left(g\,\|(\ell+1)^{2}F(\ell)\,e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}\right).

Integrated against
1-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{K+2}}(X)
, this uniform bound implies that

\langle F\,\mathds{1}_{[0,A\log(g)]}\rangle_{g}^{{>M}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\|(\ell+1)^{2}F(\ell)e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}}{g^{K+1}}\right).

As a consequence, we only need to prove the existence of an asymptotic
expansion for every fixed
S
such that
|\chi_{S}|\leq M
, which there is
a finite number of. The rest of the proof is exactly identical to the proof of

5.14
from there on, using

6.1
in lieu of
5.5
.
∎

6.3.
The tangle-free hypothesis at higher orders

The aim of this section is to prove
6.4
. This is done by
introducing the following probabilistic assumption, called a “tangle-free
hypothesis”, following the terminology for regular graphs

[
10
,
4
]
, already extended to hyperbolic surfaces in

[
32
]
and
[
21
]
.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
N\geq 1
be an integer and
L>0
. We say a compact hyperbolic surface

X
is
(N,L)
-tangled
if there exists a connected subsurface

S\subset X
with geodesic boundary such that:

•

the Euler characteristic of
S
is
-N

•

the total boundary length of
S
is smaller than
(N+1)L
.

Otherwise, we say that
X
is
(N,L)
-tangle-free
.

Remark 6.6
.

The definition extends the notion of tangle-freeness from
[
32
]
and

[
21
]
, which both correspond to the case
N=1
.

6.4
comes as a direct consequence of the two following
results, which respectively estimate the probability for a surface to be

(N,\log(g))
-tangle-free and deduce some geometric consequences from the
tangle-free hypothesis.

Proposition 6.7
.

For any integer
N>1
, there exists a constant
C_{N}>0
such that for any
large enough
g
,

1-\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(X\text{ is }\left(N,\log g\right)\text{-tangle-free}\right)\leq C_{N}\frac{(\log g)^{C_{N}}}{g^{\frac{N-1}{2}}}\cdot

Theorem 6.8
.

There exists a
C>0
such that, for all
N\geq 1
,
L>0
, if
X
is a

(N,L)
-tangle-free compact hyperbolic surface, then for any closed geodesic

\gamma
on
X
,

\lvert\chi_{S(\gamma)}\rvert\leq C\,\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)}{L}\left(\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)}{L}+N\right).

We shall prove
6.7
and
6.8
in this order.

Proof of
6.7
.

Let
g\geq 3
,
N>1
,
L:=\log g
. By Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(X\text{ is }(N,\log(g))\text{-tangled}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{S:\chi_{S}=-N}\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\#\text{ copies of }S\text{ in }X\,:\,\ell(\partial S)\leq(N+1)\log(g)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{S:\chi_{S}=-N}\frac{1}{n_{S}!V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}}\mathds{1}_{[0,(N+1)\log(g)]}(x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n_{S}})\,\phi_{g}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

as soon as
2g-2>2N
, by Mirzakhani’s integration formula, with
(\phi_{g}^{S})_{S}
the functions
defined in
Eq.

5.4
.

Let
S
be a filling type. Using the upper bound (
2.7
) on Weil–Petersson
volume polynomials, together with
5.20
for
K=|\chi_{S}|
, we obtain

\phi_{g}^{S}(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{O}_{S,N}^{w}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{x}\|^{n_{S}}}{g^{N}}\exp\left(\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n_{S}}}{2}\right)\right).

We note that constants depending on
S
can be bounded in terms of
N
alone because there is a
finite number of filling types
S
such that
\chi_{S}=-N
. Then,

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{S}}}\mathds{1}_{[0,(N+1)\log(g)]}(x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n_{S}})\,\phi_{g}^{S}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\mathcal{O}_{N}\left(\frac{(\log g)^{2n_{S}}}{g^{\frac{N-1}{2}}}\right).

The conclusion directly follows, summing the bounds for the finite number of
filling types of Euler characteristic
-N
, and using
1/V_{g_{S},n_{S}}(\mathbf{x})\leq 1/V_{g_{S},n_{S}}=\mathcal{O}_{N}\left(1\right)
.
∎

Let us now proceed to the proof of
6.8
. The key
ingredient is the following statement, which describes balls of radius
L/4
on

(N,L)
-tangle-free surfaces, and generalises the result
[
32
, Proposition
11]
or
[
21
, Lemma 2.7]
for
N=1
. The construction
for
N>1
is significantly more involved.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
L>0
. Let
X={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\Gamma}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\Gamma}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Gamma}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Gamma}$}}}
be a compact

(N,L)
-tangle-free surface. Then, for any point
z\in\mathcal{H}
, the set

\Gamma_{L}(z):=\left\{\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{\id\}\;:\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(z,\gamma\cdot z)<L\right\}

is included in a subgroup of
\Gamma
of rank at most
N
.

In order to prove this result, we shall fix a point
z
on
X
. We view the elements of

\Gamma_{L}(z)
as geodesic arcs based at
z
of length
<L/2
. Let us pick an numbering

(\gamma_{j})_{0\leq j\leq J}
of this family so that the lengths of the arcs are in a
non-decreasing order.

Lemma 6.10
.

There exists an increasing family of graphs
(G_{k})_{0\leq k\leq K}
on
X
and of regular
neighbourhoods
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{0\leq k\leq K}
of these graphs such that

(A)

for all
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
,

–

the edges of
G_{k}
are simple geodesic segments or loops, included in

\cup_{j}\gamma_{j}
, which do not intersect each other except at their endpoints;

–

if
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{+}
denotes the subset of
X
obtained by adding to
\mathcal{V}_{k}
all
the connected components of
X\setminus\mathcal{V}_{k}
homeomorphic to disks or cylinders,
then
\chi_{\mathcal{V}_{k}^{+}}=-k
and
\ell(\partial\mathcal{V}_{k}^{+})<(k+1)L
;

(B)

\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{J}
are homotopic with fixed endpoints to
loops on the graph
G_{K}
.

Let us first prove that this construction allows us to conclude.

Proof of
6.9
assuming
6.10
.

For any integer
k
such that
1\leq k\leq K
, when we replace the boundary
components of
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{+}
by the closed geodesics in their free homotopy
class, we obtain an embedded surface
S_{k}
of Euler characteristic
-k
with
total boundary length
<(k+1)L
. But by the
(N,L)
-tangle-free hypothesis,

X
does not contain any embedded surface of Euler characteristic
-N
with
total boundary length smaller than
(N+1)L
. Hence,
k\neq N
. Since this is
true for all
k\leq K
,
K<N
.

For any
j\in\{0,\ldots,J\}
,
\gamma_{j}
is homotopic with fixed endpoints to a loop on the
graph
G_{K}\subset\mathcal{V}^{+}_{K}
, and therefore
\Gamma_{L}(z)
can be seen as a subset of
the fundamental group of
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
. This allows us to conclude, because the Euler
characteristic of
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
is
-K
so the fundamental group of
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
is
generated by
K+1\leq N
elements.
∎

We can now proceed to the proof of the lemma.

Construction of the family of graphs.

We initiate the construction with the graph
G_{0}
with one vertex,
z
, and one
edge,
\gamma_{0}
. By minimality of the length,
\gamma_{0}
is simple. Furthermore,

\ell(\gamma_{0})<L/2
, so we can pick a regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{0}
of
G_{0}
such that

\ell(\partial\mathcal{V}_{0})<L
. Such a regular neighbourhood is a cylinder of Euler
characteristic
0
, and with two boundary components both freely homotopic to
\gamma_{0}
, which is
not contractible. Therefore,
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{+}=\mathcal{V}_{0}
and
(G_{k})_{k=0}
satisfies the
hypotheses (A) and (B) for
j=0
.

Let
j\geq 1
. We assume that we have constructed a family
(G_{k})_{0\leq k\leq K}
satisfying
the hypotheses (A) and (B) for
i<j
. Let us now take into account the loop
\gamma_{j}
, by
adding (if necessary) vertices and edges to the graph
G_{K}
.

Let us first prove that if
\gamma_{j}
is
not simple
, then it is already homotopic with
fixed endpoints to a loop on
G_{K}
, so hypothesis (B) is already valid for
\gamma_{j}
, and there
is nothing to do. Indeed, if we pick a parametrisation
\gamma_{j}:[0,1]\rightarrow X
and two
parameters
0\leq t<s<1
such that
\gamma_{j}(t)=\gamma_{j}(s)
, then we can write

\gamma_{j}=\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{2}
, where
\alpha_{1}
,
\beta
and

\alpha_{2}
are respectively the restrictions of
\gamma_{j}
to
[0,t]
,
[t,s]
and
[s,1]
. We
can assume without loss of generality that
\ell(\alpha_{1})\leq\ell(\alpha_{2})
(by changing the
orientation of
\gamma_{j}
). We note that
\gamma_{j}
is homotopic with fixed endpoints to the
concatenation of
\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{1}^{-1}
and

\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{2}
.

•

We observe that the length of the loop
\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{2}
based at
z
is
strictly smaller than the length of
\gamma_{j}
(because we removed the loop

\beta
). Therefore, either
\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{2}
is contractible, or there exists
an index
i<j
such that
