\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{2}
is homotopic with fixed endpoint to

\gamma_{i}
, and hence to a loop on the graph
G_{K}
by the induction hypothesis.

•

Let us replace the loop
\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{1}^{-1}
based at

z
by the geodesic arc
\tilde{\beta}
in its homotopy class with fixed endpoints. Since

\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{1}^{-1}
is not a geodesic arc (because its
angle at the base-point
z
is equal to
0
),

\ell(\tilde{\beta})<\ell(\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{1}^{-1})=\ell(\beta)+2\ell(\alpha_{1})\leq\ell(\beta)+\ell(\alpha_{1})+\ell(\alpha_{2})=\ell(\gamma_{j})

which implies as before that
\tilde{\beta}
(and hence

\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{1}^{-1}
) is homotopic with fixed endpoints to a
loop on
G_{K}
.

We now assume that
\gamma_{j}
is
simple
. Its intersections with the graph
G_{K}
are
transverse, because otherwise
\gamma_{j}
would coincide on an interval with a
\gamma_{i}
for a

i<j
, and would therefore contain a sub-arc
\gamma_{i^{\prime}}
of
\gamma_{i}
based at
z
, for a

i^{\prime}\leq i
. This would imply that
\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{i^{\prime}}
because
\gamma_{j}
is simple (and
therefore only visits
z
once) and
\ell(\gamma_{i^{\prime}})\leq\ell(\gamma_{j})
, which is impossible.

We can then write
\gamma_{j}=\alpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\alpha_{n}
where

(\alpha_{i})_{i}
are disjoint simple geodesic segments or arcs, which meet
G_{K}
at their
endpoints and only there. By retracting the regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{K}
if necessary, we
can assume that each
\alpha_{i}
meets the boundary of
\mathcal{V}_{K}
exactly twice, at two points

o_{i}
and
e_{i}
. Let
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
denote the portion of
\alpha_{i}
outside of

\mathcal{V}_{K}
.

Let us first consider the segment
\alpha_{1}
. There are two possible
topological configurations for this segment, represented in

Fig.

8
.

(a)

\alpha_{1}
connects on component to itself.

(b)

\alpha_{1}
connects two different components.

Figure 8
.
Illustration of the topological situations
encountered in the proof of
6.10
, when
adding a segment
\alpha_{1}
to the graph
G_{K}
.

(a)

The exit points
o_{1}
and
e_{1}
of
\alpha_{1}
belong to the same connected component

\beta
of
\partial\mathcal{V}_{K}
, as represented in
Fig.

8(a)
. Then,
since the path
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
is entirely included the complement of
\mathcal{V}_{K}
, the
path
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
is on the same side of
\beta
at its two extremities. The regular
neighbourhood of
\beta
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
is therefore a pair of pants, with three boundary
components which we call
\beta^{\prime}
(for the one freely homotopic to
\beta
),
\beta_{1}
and

\beta_{2}
.

–

If
\beta_{1}
or
\beta_{2}
is contractible, then
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
is
homotopic with fixed endpoints to a path on
\beta
, and hence
\alpha_{1}

to one on
G_{K}
.

–

Otherwise, we define
G_{K+1}
by adding the path
\alpha_{1}
and its
endpoints to the graph
G_{K}
.
G_{K+1}
automatically satisfies the first
condition of (A). For the second condition, we observe that a regular
neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{K+1}
of
G_{K+1}
can be obtained by gluing
the pair of pants delimited by
\beta
,
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
to the
regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{K}
along its boundary component

\beta
. Neither
\beta_{1}
nor
\beta_{2}
is contractible, so when we add
the disks and cylinders to
\mathcal{V}_{K+1}
to construct

\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+}
, we obtain
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
together with an
additional pair of pants (when
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are in two
different free homotopy classes) or once-holed torus (when
\beta_{1}
and

\beta_{2}
are freely homotopic to one another, and hence bound a
cylinder). In both cases,
\chi_{\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+}}=-(K+1)
by
additivity of the Euler characteristic. Since the boundary of

\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+}
is obtained by taking the boundary of

\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
and either removing the component
\beta
and adding

\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
in the pair of pants case, or just removing

\beta
in the once-holed torus case,

\ell(\partial\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+})\leq\ell(\partial\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+})-\ell(\beta)+\ell(\beta_{1})+\ell(\beta_{2}).

By hypothesis on
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
, its boundary length is
<(K+1)L
. Furthermore, because
the pair of pants delimited by
\beta
,
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
is filled by the family of
curves
\beta
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
, of total length
<\ell(\beta)+L/2
, we can pick

\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
such that

\ell(\beta)+\ell(\beta_{1})+\ell(\beta_{2})<2\ell(\beta)+L.

As a consequence,
\ell(\partial\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+})<(K+2)L
.

(b)

Or the exit points
o_{1}
and
e_{1}
belong to two different connected components

\beta_{o}
and
\beta_{e}
of
\partial\mathcal{V}_{K}
, like in

Fig.

8(b)
. Then, once again, the regular neighbourhood of
\beta_{o}
,

\beta_{e}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
is a pair of pants of boundary components
\beta_{o}^{\prime}
,

\beta_{e}^{\prime}
and
\beta
.

–

If
\beta
is contractible, then
\beta_{o}
and
\beta_{e}
bound a
cylinder
\mathcal{C}
. We modify the definition of
G_{K}
and replace it by the graph
G_{K}^{\prime}

obtained by adding the path
\alpha_{1}
and its endpoints to
G_{K}
. We notice that, when
constructing
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
, we would add the cylinder
\mathcal{C}
to

\mathcal{V}_{K}
. Now, when constructing the new
(\mathcal{V}_{K}^{\prime})^{+}
from
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{\prime}
, we
rather add the contractible boundary component delimited by
\beta
. Hence, the set

(\mathcal{V}_{K}^{\prime})^{+}
coincides with
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
, and satisfies (A).

–

Otherwise,
\beta
is not contractible. We add the edge
\alpha_{1}
and
its endpoints to
G_{K}
and obtain a graph
G_{K+1}
. The regular
neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{K+1}
is then obtained by gluing the pair of pants
delimited by
\beta_{o}
,
\beta_{e}
,
\beta
on
\mathcal{V}_{K}
, and

\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+}
is obtained from
\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+}
by the same
process since
\beta
is not contractible. Hence, the Euler characteristic
of
\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+}
is
-(K+1)
, and

\ell(\partial\mathcal{V}_{K+1}^{+})=\ell(\partial\mathcal{V}_{K}^{+})-\ell(\beta_{o})-\ell(\beta_{e})+\ell(\beta)

can be assumed to be
<(K+2)L
because we can assume that

\ell(\beta_{o})+\ell(\beta_{e})+\ell(\beta)<2\ell(\beta_{o})+2\ell(\beta_{e})+L.

In all these cases, we obtain a new family of graphs

(G_{k}^{\prime})_{0\leq k\leq K^{\prime}}
, with
K^{\prime}=K
or
K+1
, satisfying the hypotheses
(A) and (B) for
\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{j-1}
, and such that the path

\alpha_{1}
is homotopic with fixed endpoints to a path on
G_{K^{\prime}}^{\prime}
.

We apply this construction successively to
\alpha_{i}
for

i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. At the end, we obtain a family of graphs

(G_{k}^{\prime\prime})_{0\leq k\leq K^{\prime\prime}}
,
K\leq K^{\prime\prime}\leq K+n
, satisfying the hypotheses
(A) and (B) for the loops
\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{j}
, which concludes this
step of the iterative construction.
∎

This concludes the proof of
6.9
. Then,

6.8
comes as an immediate consequence of the
following lemma.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
L>0
. Let
X={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\Gamma}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\Gamma}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Gamma}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{H}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Gamma}$}}}
be a

(N,L)
-tangle-free hyperbolic surface. Let
\beta
be a geodesic path of
length
\ell
on
\mathcal{H}
. Then, there exists a neighbourhood

\mathcal{U}
of
\beta
in
\mathcal{H}
and a subgroup
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
of

\Gamma
of rank
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ell}{L}\left(\frac{\ell}{L}+N\right)\right)
such that

\forall z\in\mathcal{U},\quad\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,:\,\gamma\cdot z\in\mathcal{U}\}\subset\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}.

Remark 6.12
.

The proof actually implies that we can take
\mathcal{U}
to be equal to the

L/16
-neighbourhood of
\beta
in
\mathcal{H}
. As a consequence,

6.8
still holds if we replace
S(\gamma)
by the
surface filled by any multi-loop
(\gamma,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})
, where
(\beta_{i})_{i}
is a
family of geodesic paths of length
\leq L/16
that each intersect
\gamma
.

Proof.

Let us cover the geodesic
\beta
by
k=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ell}{L}\right)
balls of radius

\frac{L}{8}
, centered at points denoted by
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
. Then, the open
sets

\mathcal{U}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{8}}(z_{i})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{U}_{+}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{4}}(z_{i}).

are neighbourhoods of the curve
\beta
.

Figure 9
.
Illustration of the proof of
6.11
.

For each index
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, we set

\Gamma_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{+}):=\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,:\,\gamma\cdot z_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{+}\}
. We claim that

\forall z\in\mathcal{U},\quad\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,:\,\gamma\cdot z\in\mathcal{U}\}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\Gamma_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{+}).

Indeed, any element
z
of
\mathcal{U}
belongs in

\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{8}}(z_{i})
for an integer
i
. For an element

\gamma\in\Gamma
, if
\gamma\cdot z\in\mathcal{U}
, then there is an index
j

such that
\gamma\cdot z\in\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{8}}(z_{j})
. Then,

\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(z_{j},\gamma\cdot z_{i})\leq\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(z_{j},\gamma\cdot z)+\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma\cdot z,\gamma\cdot z_{i})<\frac{L}{8}+\frac{L}{8}=\frac{L}{4}

and therefore

\gamma\cdot z_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{4}}(z_{j})\subset\mathcal{U}_{+}
, which
implies that
\gamma\in\Gamma_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{+})
.

Therefore, we only need to prove that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, the
set
\Gamma_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{+})
is included in a subgroup of
\Gamma
of rank

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ell}{L}+N\right)
using the
(N,L)
-tangle-free hypothesis. This will
lead to the conclusion because
k=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ell}{L}\right)
.

For each
j\neq i
, let us pick a
\gamma_{i,j}\in\Gamma
such that

\gamma_{i,j}\cdot z_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{4}}(z_{j})
, provided such a
transformation exists. Then, if
\gamma\in\Gamma_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{+})
,

\gamma\cdot z_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{+}
so one of the following occurs:

•

either
\gamma\cdot z_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{4}}(z_{i})
, which implies that

\gamma\in\Gamma_{\frac{L}{4}}(z_{i})\subset\Gamma_{\frac{L}{2}}(z_{i})
;

•

or
\gamma\cdot z_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{\frac{L}{4}}(z_{j})
for a
j\neq i
, and hence

\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{i,j}\cdot z_{i},\gamma\cdot z_{i})\leq\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{i,j}\cdot z_{i},z_{j})+\dist_{\mathcal{H}}(z_{j},\gamma\cdot z_{i})<\frac{L}{4}+\frac{L}{4}=\frac{L}{2}

so
\gamma_{i,j}^{-1}\gamma
is an element of
\Gamma_{\frac{L}{2}}(z_{i})
.

As a consequence, every element of
\Gamma_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{+})
can be written as a
product of the
(\gamma_{i,j})_{j\neq i}
and the elements of

\Gamma_{\frac{L}{2}}(z_{i})
. The latter is included in a sub-group of
\Gamma
of
rank
\leq N
by the tangle-free hypothesis and
6.9
,
while the former contains
k-1
elements. Therefore,
\Gamma_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{+})

is included in a sub-group of rank
\leq N+k-1
, which allows us to conclude.
∎

7.
The case of the figure-eight

The aim of this section is to prove
Objective (FR)
in the case of a figure-eight
in a pair of pants, which is the local topological type represented in

Fig.

10
.

Figure 10
.
A figure-eight in a pair of pants.

The figure-eight is the simplest example of loop filling the pair of pants, which is why we address it
first. We prove the following.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\alpha
be a figure-eight filling a pair of pants
P
, and

T=[P,\alpha]_{\mathrm{loc}}
. Then, for all
k\geq 0
, the function

f_{k}^{T}
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function.

By “simplest”, we mean that it is the loop with the least self-intersections filling
P
, as
opposed to more complicated loops such as the one represented in the left part of

Fig.

3(b)
. However, when we prove
Objective (FR)
for all other loops filling

P
in
Section

8
, we will notice that it is actually the one for which the analysis
is the hardest.

7.1.
Expression of the density function as an integral

Let
F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an admissible function. We recall that, by definition, the
functions
f_{k}^{T}
will appear when computing the asymptotic expansion of the average

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\in T^{g}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg].

We saw in
5.5
that this expectation can be expressed as

(7.1)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}F(\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}))\,\phi_{g}^{P}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\,\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3},

where

•

\phi_{g}^{P}
is the sum over all realisations of the pair of pants, defined
in (
5.4
) and computed in
5.7
;

•

the length function
\mathfrak{L}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
associates to

(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
the length of the figure-eight going around the components
1
and
2

in the pair of pants of boundary components of lengths
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
. It is computed in

[
7
, equation 4.2.3]
:

(7.2)

\cosh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})}{2}\right)=2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right).

By
5.21
, the function
\phi_{g}^{P}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
has an expansion in
powers of
1/g
. The dependency of its terms with respect to
(\ell_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
is
detailed. When substituting
\phi_{g}^{P}
by its expansion in (
7.1
), we obtain
that any term of the asymptotic expansion of the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
in powers of
1/g
is a linear
combination of integrals of the form

(7.3)

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}F(\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}))\,\prod_{i=1}^{3}h_{i}(\ell_{i})\,\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}

(7.4)

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}F(\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}))\,h_{i}(\ell_{i})\,\ell_{j}\delta(\ell_{j}-\ell_{k})\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}\quad\text{for }\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}

where for all
i
, the functions
\ell_{i}\mapsto h_{i}(\ell_{i})
is of the form

(7.5)

\ell_{i}^{2k+1}\quad\text{or}\quad\ell_{i}^{2k+1}\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\quad\text{or}\quad\ell_{i}^{2k}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right).

7.2.
Level-set decomposition

Because we want to view the average
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
as the integral of
F
against a
density, we rewrite the integral (
7.3
) as

\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\left(\iint_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell}\,\prod_{i=1}^{3}h_{i}(\ell_{i})\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

Let us give precise meaning to this writing. We fix
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}>0
. Then, the application

\mathfrak{L}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}:\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow I(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\\
\ell_{3}\hskip 5.0pt\mapsto\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=2\argcosh\left(2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)\right)\end{cases}

is a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
to an interval

I(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We introduce the following notation.

Notation 7.2
.

Let
\ell>0
. For any integrable function
G:\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we
define

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell}G(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}}\frac{\mathds{1}_{I(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})}(\ell)\,G(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\mathfrak{L}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{-1}(\ell))}{\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}(\mathfrak{L}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}^{-1}(\ell))}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}.

This formula corresponds to considering the variable
\ell_{3}
as a function of the variables

\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell
, once restricted to the
2
-dimensional level-set

\{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\;:\;\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell\}
. The derivative
that appears corresponds, formally, to writing

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}=\frac{\partial\ell_{3}}{\partial\ell}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}.

It is easy to check using the local inversion theorem that one obtains the same quantity by
performing this operation on the variables
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{3}
, or
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{3}
.

We do the same for the integrals of the form (
7.4
). For instance, for

(i,j,k)=(1,2,3)
, we obtain the integral

(7.6)

\int_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{2})=\ell}h_{1}(\ell_{1})\,\ell_{2}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\cdot

7.3.
Reformulation of the question and proof in simple cases

It will be handy to observe that, for any of the three cases in (
7.5
), we can write

f_{i}(\ell_{i}):=\frac{h_{i}(\ell_{i})}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)}=p_{i}(\ell_{i})+\mathcal{O}\left((\ell_{i}+1)^{c_{i}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\right)

where
p_{i}
is a polynomial function (possibly equal to zero) and
c_{i}\geq 0
,
because

\frac{\ell_{i}}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left((\ell_{i}+1)\,e^{-\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)=\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)+e^{-\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}}.

All of the steps taken so far lead us to the following lemma.

Lemma 7.3
.

For any integer
k\geq 0
, the function
\ell\mapsto f_{k}^{T}(\ell)
is a linear combination of
functions of the form

(7.7)

\mathcal{I}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]:\ell\mapsto\iint_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell}\,\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

and

(7.8)

\begin{cases}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{1}[f_{1}]:\ell\mapsto\int_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{2})=\ell}\,f_{1}(\ell_{1})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\,\ell_{2}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\\
&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{2}[f_{2}]:\ell\mapsto\int_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{3},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell}\,f_{2}(\ell_{2})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)\,\ell_{3}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\\
&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{3}[f_{3}]:\ell\mapsto\int_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{1},\ell_{3})=\ell}\,f_{3}(\ell_{3})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)\,\ell_{1}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\end{cases}

where for any
i\in\{1,2,3\}
,
f_{i}
satisfies:

(7.9)

|f_{i}(\ell_{i})-p_{i}(\ell_{i})|\leq c_{i}(\ell_{i}+1)^{c_{i}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)

for a polynomial function
p_{i}
and a constant
c_{i}>0
.

In particular, if we prove that the integrals in (
7.7
) and
(
7.8
) are Friedman–Ramanujan for any
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}

satisfying (
7.9
), then we can conclude that
f_{k}^{T}
is too, by
linearity, hence proving
7.1
. Let us prove this in some simple cases.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
be measurable functions, each satisfying
(
7.9
).

(1)

If
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
, then

\mathcal{I}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]\in\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}
.

(2)

If
p_{3}=0
, then

\mathcal{I}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]\in\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}
.

(3)

Without any further hypothesis, the
integrals in (
7.8
) belong in

\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Before we proceed to the proof, let us observe that

(7.10)

\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\leq\ell\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{3}\leq\ell.

This can be seen on the expressions, or directly on
Fig.

10
, by
minimality of the length of a geodesic in a free homotopy class.

The proof of
7.4
for all integrals of the form
(
7.8
) are similar, and we therefore detail the computation for

\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\mathrm{tor}}
only. First, we use the level-set condition

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)=\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)\left(2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)+1\right)

to compute that

\frac{\partial\ell_{1}}{\partial\ell}=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}{2\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\leq\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)}\cdot

The hypothesis (
7.9
) on
f_{1}
implies that there exists a
constant
c>0
such that

|f_{1}(\ell_{1})|\leq c(\ell_{1}+1)^{c}.

It follows, using (
7.10
), that

\left|f_{1}(\ell_{1})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\ell_{2}\frac{\partial\ell_{1}}{\partial\ell}\right|\leq c(\ell+1)^{c+1}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)

and hence

|\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{1}[f_{1}](\ell)|\leq c(\ell+1)^{c+1}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{\ell}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\leq c(\ell+1)^{c+2}\exp\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)

which means that
\ell\mapsto\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{1}[f_{1}](\ell)
belongs in
\mathcal{R}
.

Let us now treat case when
p_{3}=0
. In that case, now using

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)=2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right),

we obtain

(7.11)

\frac{\partial\ell_{2}}{\partial\ell}=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}{2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\cdot

We then observe that our assumptions on
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
implies that
there exists a constant
c\geq 0
such that, for any
\ell_{1},\ell_{3},\ell
for
which
\ell_{2}
is well-defined,

(7.12)

\left|\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\,\frac{\partial\ell_{2}}{\partial\ell}\right|\leq c(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}+1)^{c}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right).

which, by (
7.10
), implies

\mathcal{I}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}](\ell)=\mathcal{O}\left((\ell+1)^{c}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\iint_{[0,\ell]^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left((\ell+1)^{c+2}\exp\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\right),

which is our claim.

The proof the remaining case is the same, now expressing
\ell_{3}
in terms of
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
and

\ell
. Indeed,

\frac{\partial\ell_{3}}{\partial\ell}=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)}

and hence

\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\,\frac{\partial\ell_{3}}{\partial\ell}
satisfies the bound (
7.12
) when
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

The fact that we obtain a function in
\mathcal{R}
in cases (
1
) and
(
2
) corresponds to the fact that, if

\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
, then
\tilde{f}_{1}\star\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
, as
observed in the proof of the stability of
\mathcal{F}
by convolution (
3.5
), which we invite
the reader to read at this stage. Another insight that one can gather from proof is that, if

\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
are of respective main terms
\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}
, then
the main term of
\tilde{f}_{1}\star\tilde{f}_{2}
is not only
\tilde{p}_{1}\star\tilde{p}_{2}
, but
actually contains contributions coming from the remainders terms of
\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}
.
This is the reason why
7.4
only holds if
both

f_{1}
and
f_{2}

have no polynomial term, and not when only one of them does. We do expect the contributions where

p_{1}\neq 0
whilst
p_{2}=0
to participate to the main term of
\mathcal{I}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]
.

7.4.
Change of variables

In order to study the integrals
\mathcal{I}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]
more precisely, it will be helpful to
introduce new variables, which transform the level-set integral (
7.7
)
into a convolution-like integral. We shall use the following new variables, which are represented in

Fig.

11
:

•

L_{1}
and
L_{2}
denote the lengths of the geodesic arcs based at the self-intersection
of
\alpha
, going around the first and second boundary components of the pair of pants
respectively;

•

u:=\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)
, where
\theta
denotes the outer angle of the self-intersection of

\alpha
.

We observe that, in these new coordinates, we always have

\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=L_{1}+L_{2}
, and hence the level-set
integral is an integral on

\{(L_{1},L_{2},u)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}\times(0,1)\,:\,L_{1}+L_{2}=\ell\}
, similar to a
convolution. This is evocative of the case of graphs, described in

Section

3.3
. A significant difference with the case of graphs is the
presence of an additional parameter
u\in(0,1)
that is required to describe
the geometry of the pair of pants; in the following, this quantity will mostly
behave like a free parameter in
(0,1)
that we will integrate out.

Figure 11
.
The new variables
(L_{1},L_{2},u=\cos^{2}(\theta/2))
used to describe
the geometry of the pair of pants.

The following lemma provides an expression for the lengths

\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
of the three boundary components of the pair of pants,
in terms of the new variables
(L_{1},L_{2},u)
.

Lemma 7.6
.

For any
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}>0
,

(7.14)

Proof.

Let
z
denote the intersection point of the figure eight, and
\alpha_{1}

denote the portion of
\alpha
going around the first boundary component,

b_{1}
, of
P
. We draw the common perpendicular of
\alpha_{1}
and
b_{1}
, as
well as the perpendicular of
b_{1}
passing through
z
. When cutting along

\alpha_{1}
and those two perpendiculars, we obtain a trirectangle, represented
in
Fig.

12
. By
[
7
, Theorem 2.3.1(iii)]
,

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)=\sin\left(\frac{\pi-\theta}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)=\sqrt{u}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right),

and same goes for
\ell_{2}
.

Figure 12
.
Illustration of the proof of
.

The formula for
\ell_{3}
is obtained directly from the formula for the length
of the figure eight,

\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)=2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)

together with the fact that, by (
),

2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)=2u\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)

and we have the usual trigonometric formula

2\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)=\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}-L_{2}}{2}\right).

∎

This is enough to entirely describe the change of variable

(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\rightarrow(L_{1},L_{2},u)
, which we do in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

The application
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\mapsto(L_{1},L_{2},u)
is a diffeomorphism from the set

\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}
onto the set
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}\times(0,1)
defined by

(7.15)

\mathcal{E}:=\left\{(L_{1},L_{2},u)\,:\,\begin{cases}\sqrt{u}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{i}}{2}\right)>1\qquad\text{for }i\in\{1,2\}\\
(1-u)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)>u\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}-L_{2}}{2}\right)+1\end{cases}\right\},

and we have that

\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}=-\dfrac{\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)}\,\mathrm{d}L_{1}\,\mathrm{d}L_{2}\,\mathrm{d}u.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is a straightforward consequence of
7.6
together
with the fact that
\cosh:(0,+\infty)\rightarrow(1,+\infty)
is a diffeomorphism. The only
thing we need to check is the expression for the Jacobian of the change of variable. Using

7.6
, we compute the partial derivatives of
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
expressed as
functions of
L_{1},L_{2},u
, using the slight variation of (
7.14
)

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)=\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)-2u\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right).

We obtain that we need to compute the determinant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{3}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)}\begin{vmatrix}\sqrt{u}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)&0&\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)-2u\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)\\
0&\sqrt{u}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)&\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)-2u\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)&\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)&-4\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)\end{vmatrix}.

We add
2\sqrt{u}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)
times the first column and
2\sqrt{u}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)
times
the second column to the third, which removes its negative terms. We then factor out the

\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)
in the third column, and obtain the determinant

\displaystyle\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)}{\prod_{i=1}^{3}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)}\begin{vmatrix}\sqrt{u}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)&0&1\\
0&\sqrt{u}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)&1\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)&\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)&0\end{vmatrix}=-\frac{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)}{\prod_{i=1}^{3}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)}\cdot

∎

7.5.
Technical lemmas about the change of variables

In order to prove
7.1
, which we shall do in the next section, we need a few technical
lemmas describing the change of variable
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\rightarrow(L_{1},L_{2},u)
more
precisely. For that purpose, we introduce the following notations.

Notation 7.8
.

We set
\ell_{0}:=4\argch(\sqrt{2})
. For
\ell>0
, we define

(7.16)

u_{-}(\ell):=\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{4}\right)}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{+}(\ell):=1-\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{4}\right)}

and, for any
u\in(0,1)
, we define

L_{-}^{\infty}(u):=\max\left(2\argcosh\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\right),\log\left(\frac{u}{1-u}\right)\right).

We shall refer to the following two subsets of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times(0,1)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\ell,u)\in(\ell_{0},+\infty)\times(0,1)\,:\,u_{-}(\ell)<u<u_{+}(\ell)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\infty}

\displaystyle:=\{(L,u)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times(0,1)\,:\,L>L_{-}^{\infty}(u)\}.

Let us list a few elementary properties of the quantities defined above.

Lemma 7.9
.

(1)

Let
\ell>0
. Then,
u_{-}(\ell)<u_{+}(\ell)
if and only if
\ell>\ell_{0}
.

(2)

We have for any
\ell>0
,
\lvert\log(u_{-}(\ell))\rvert=\lvert\log(1-u_{+}(\ell))\rvert\leq\ell/2
.
Furthermore, for any integer
m\geq 0
, there exists
C_{m}>0
such that, for any
\ell>0
,

(7.17)

\int_{0}^{u_{-}(\ell)}|\log u|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}u\leq C_{m}(\ell+1)^{m}e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}.

(3)

The function
u\mapsto L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
is equivalent to
-\log(u)
for

u
close to
0
and
-\log(1-u)
for
u
close to
1
.

Proof.

The first and last points are trivial, and so is the first part of (
2
). The rest of the
second point can be obtained by proving that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}|\log(u)|^{m}\,\mathrm{d}u=\int_{-\log(\epsilon)}^{+\infty}v^{m}e^{-v}\,\mathrm{d}v=\mathcal{O}\left((|\log(\epsilon)|+1)^{m}\epsilon\right)

by iterated integration by parts, and then taking
\epsilon=u_{-}(\ell)
.
∎

Let us now provide a finer description of the set
\mathcal{E}
defined in
7.7
.

Lemma 7.10
.

For any
\ell>0
, any
u\in(0,1)
, any
L\in(0,\frac{\ell}{2})
,

(L,\ell-L,u)\in\mathcal{E}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\begin{cases}(\ell,u)\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\\
L>L_{-}(\ell,u)\end{cases}

where the function
L_{-}:\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow(0,\frac{\ell}{2})
satisfies, for a
constant
C>0
,

(7.18)

\forall(\ell,u)\in\mathcal{E}_{1},\quad 0\leq L_{-}(\ell,u)-L_{-}^{\infty}(u)\leq C\ell\frac{e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}{1-u}\cdot

Note that, here, we are only describing on the part of
\mathcal{E}
for which
L_{1}<L_{2}
(which is
equivalent to
L_{1}<\ell/2
since
L_{1}+L_{2}=\ell
). Since
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
play symmetric roles, this
allows us to describe the entirety of
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

Let us consider a
\ell>0
,
0<u<1
, and
0<L<\frac{\ell}{2}
.

The first condition defining
\mathcal{E}
is
\sqrt{u}\cosh\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)>1
. The existence of number

L
in
(0,\frac{\ell}{2})
satisfying this inequality is equivalent to the following condition on
u

and
\ell
:

\sqrt{u}\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{4}\right)>1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad u>u_{-}(\ell).

Then,
L
satisfies the condition if and only if

L>L^{1}_{-}(u):=2\argcosh\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\right).

Now, because we assume that
L<\frac{\ell}{2}
, we have that
\ell-L>L
, and hence the second
condition of
\mathcal{E}
is automatically satisfied once the first is.

Let us now move on the last condition defining
\mathcal{E}
,

(1-u)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)-u\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}-L\right)>1.

The left hand side of this equation is an increasing function of
L\in(0,\frac{\ell}{2})
, and is
therefore maximal at
\ell/2
. As a consequence, there exists a value
L
in
(0,\frac{\ell}{2})

so that it exceeds
1
if and only if

(1-u)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)-u>1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad u<u_{+}(\ell).

In that case,
L
satisfies the third condition of
\mathcal{E}
if and only if

L>L^{3}_{-}(\ell,u):=\frac{\ell}{2}-\argcosh\left(\frac{1-u}{u}\cosh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)-\frac{1}{u}\right).

Finally, we observe that we need to have
u_{-}(\ell)<u_{+}(\ell)
for all three conditions of

\mathcal{E}
to be satisfied together, which we saw in
7.9
is equivalent to

\ell>\ell_{0}
. Then, we have the claimed result with

L_{-}(\ell,u):=\max\left(L^{1}_{-}(u),L^{3}_{-}(\ell,u)\right).

To prove the bound on
L_{-}-L_{-}^{\infty}
, we simply need to observe that for
x>1
,

\argcosh(x)=\log\left(x+\sqrt{x^{2}-1}\right)=\log\left(2x+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)\right)

and hence

\displaystyle L^{3}_{-}(\ell,u)=\frac{\ell}{2}-\log\left(\frac{1-u}{u}\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\right)=\log\left(\frac{u}{1-u}\right)-\log(1+A(\ell,u))

where the quantity
A(\ell,u)>-1
satisfies
A(\ell,u)=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}/(1-u)\right)
. There
exists a constant
c>0
such that, for any real number
A\geq-1/2
,
|\log(1+A)|\leq c|A|
. This is enough to conclude whenever
A(\ell,u)\geq-1/2
, since
\ell>\ell_{0}
. Otherwise,
we rather use the trivial bound

\left|L^{3}_{-}(\ell,u)-\log\left(\frac{u}{1-u}\right)\right|\leq\frac{\ell}{2}+|\log(u_{-}(\ell))|+|\log(1-u_{+}(\ell))|\leq 2\ell.

This leads to the claimed estimate, because if
A(\ell,u)<-1/2
, then
e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}/(1-u)

is bounded away from
0
.
∎

The next lemma is an expansion of the functions
\ell_{i}(L_{1},L_{2},u)
for
1\leq i\leq 3
.

Lemma 7.11
.

There exists functions
r_{0},r:\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and a constant
C>0
such
that, for any
(L_{1},L_{2},u)\in\mathcal{E}
,

\begin{cases}\ell_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},u)=L_{1}+\log(u)+r_{0}(L_{1},u)\\
\ell_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},u)=L_{2}+\log(u)+r_{0}(L_{2},u)\\
\ell_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},u)=L_{1}+L_{2}+2\log(1-u)+r(L_{1},u)+r(L_{2},u)+\mathrm{err}(L_{1},L_{2},u)\end{cases}

where:

(1)

for any
(L_{1},L_{2},u)\in\mathcal{E}
,

|\mathrm{err}(L_{1},L_{2},u)|\leq C(L_{1}+L_{2})\,e^{-\frac{\ell_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},u)}{2}};

(2)

for any
(\ell,u)\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
, any
L>L_{-}^{\infty}(\ell,u)
,

(7.19)

|r_{0}(L,u)|+|r(L,u)|\leq C\,\ell\,\frac{e^{-L}}{u(1-u)};

(3)

r_{0}
is bounded on
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\infty}
, and for any integer
k\geq 0
, any

(\ell,u)\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
,

(7.20)

\int_{L_{-}^{\infty}(u)}^{L_{-}(\ell,u)}|r(L,u)|^{k}\,\mathrm{d}L=\mathcal{O}_{k}\left(\ell^{k+1}\frac{e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}{1-u}\right)

and for any
k,j\geq 0
, any
u\in(0,1)
,

(7.21)

\int_{L_{-}^{\infty}(u)}^{+\infty}L^{j}|r(L,u)|^{k}\,\mathrm{d}L=\mathcal{O}_{k}\left((|\log(u)|+|\log(1-u)|)^{j}\right).

Proof of
7.11
.

Let us first prove the expansion for
\ell_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},u)
. First, we observe that the expression
(
) allows to write
\ell_{1}
as a function of
L_{1}
and
u
(and hence independent
of
L_{2}
), which is well-defined as soon as
L_{1}>L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
. Then, we write

\exp\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)=2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)/(1+e^{-\ell_{1}})
, which yields

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(L_{1},u)

\displaystyle=2\log\left(2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\ell_{1}}\right)=2\log\left(\sqrt{u}(e^{\frac{L_{1}}{2}}+e^{-\frac{L_{1}}{2}})\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\ell_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=L_{1}+\log(u)+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-L_{1}}+e^{-\ell_{1}}\right).

This implies the claimed estimate taking
r_{0}(L,u):=\ell_{1}(L,u)-L-\log(u)
. Indeed, this
function is defined for any
L>L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
, and is
\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-L}+e^{-\ell_{1}(L,u)}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
by
the bound above. We conclude thanks to the fact that
e^{-\ell_{1}(L,u)}=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-L}/u\right)
as soon as

L>L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
.

The formula for
\ell_{2}
is exactly the same, so we now move on to
\ell_{3}
. We shall pay
particular attention to the fact that
\ell_{3}
depends on both
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. First, we write
similarly as before

\displaystyle\ell_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},u)

\displaystyle=2\log\left(2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\ell_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-4.0pt\overset{\eqref{e:l3_calc}}{=}2\log\left((1-u)(e^{\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}+e^{-\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}})-u(e^{\frac{L_{2}-L_{1}}{2}}+e^{\frac{L_{1}-L_{2}}{2}})\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\ell_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=L_{1}+L_{2}+2\log(1-u)+R_{u}(L_{1},L_{2},u)+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\ell_{3}}\right)

where

R_{u}(L_{1},L_{2},u):=2\log\left(1+e^{-L_{1}-L_{2}}-\frac{u}{1-u}(e^{-L_{1}}+e^{-L_{2}})\right).

In order to write this term in the desired form, we rewrite the argument of the
\log
as

\left(1-\frac{u}{1-u}e^{-L_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{u}{1-u}e^{-L_{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-L_{1}-L_{2}}}{(1-u)^{2}}\right).

We observe that,
by the third condition of the definition of
\mathcal{E}
,

(7.22)

(1-u)\,e^{\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}-u(e^{\frac{L_{2}-L_{1}}{2}}+e^{\frac{L_{1}-L_{2}}{2}})>2-(1-u)>1

and hence

(7.23)

\left(1-\frac{u}{1-u}e^{-L_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{u}{1-u}e^{-L_{2}}\right)>1-\frac{u}{1-u}(e^{-L_{1}}+e^{-L_{2}})\overset{\eqref{eq:def_E_3_use}}{>}\frac{e^{-\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}}{1-u}\cdot

This implies that, as soon as
(L_{1},L_{2},u)\in\mathcal{E}
,

(7.24)

R_{u}(L_{1},L_{2},u)=r(L_{1},u)+r(L_{2},u)+\log\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}}{1-u}\right)\right)

for the function
r(L,u):=\log\left(1-\frac{u}{1-u}e^{-L}\right)
, well-defined for any

L>L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
.

We proceed as in the proof of (
7.18
) to prove the bound
(
7.19
) on
r
. Indeed,

•

if
ue^{-L}/(1-u)\leq 1/2
, then
r(L,u)=\mathcal{O}\left(ue^{-L}/(1-u)\right)
by the asymptotic behaviour
of
\log
near
1
;

•

otherwise, we rather observe that, by (
7.23
), we have

|r(L,u)|\leq\frac{\ell}{2}+|\log(1-u)|
, which is
\leq\ell
by

7.9
.(
2
).

The integral bound (
7.20
) is obtained by studying for
\epsilon>0

the integral

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}|\log(1-e^{-x})|^{k}\,\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}_{k}\left(\epsilon\,(|\log(1-e^{-\epsilon})|+1)^{k}\right)

by integration by parts, taking
\epsilon:=L_{-}(\ell,u)-\log\left(\frac{u}{1-u}\right)
, and using
the bound (
7.18
). The other integral is straightforward.

Similarly, we can prove that the logarithmic term in (
7.24
) is
\mathcal{O}\left(\ell\,e^{-\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}/(1-u)\right)
for
(L_{1},L_{2},u)\in\mathcal{E}
.
This allows us to conclude the expansion of
\ell_{3}
, because

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},u)}{2}\right)\leq(1-u)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)

and hence
e^{-\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}/(1-u)=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\frac{\ell_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},u)}{2}}\right)
.
∎

We will actually use the following corollary of
7.11
, which is obtained by
carefully taking powers and linear combinations of the statement.

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
be functions satisfying (
7.9
). Let

Q_{u}(L_{1},L_{2}):=p_{1}(L_{1}+\log(u))\,p_{2}(L_{2}+\log(u))\,p_{3}(L_{1}+L_{2}+2\log(1-u))

where
(p_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
are the respective polynomials of
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
.
There exists an integer
K
and a family of functions

\tilde{r}_{j}:\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, for
0\leq j\leq K
, such that for any

(L_{1},L_{2},u)\in\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i}(L_{1},L_{2},u))

\displaystyle=\,Q_{u}(L_{1},L_{2})+\sum_{0\leq j\leq K}\big(L_{1}^{j}\tilde{r}_{j}(L_{2},u)+L_{2}^{j}\tilde{r}_{j}(L_{1},u)\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{= \,}+\mathcal{O}\left((L_{1}+L_{2})^{K}\left(e^{-\frac{\ell_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},u)}{2}}+\frac{e^{-\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}}{u(1-u)}\right)\right)

and for any integer
0\leq j\leq K
, any
(\ell,u)\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
, any
L>L_{-}^{\infty}(\ell,u)
,

(7.25)

\tilde{r}_{j}(L,u)=\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{K}\frac{e^{-L}}{u(1-u)}\right).

Furthermore, for all
j
, there exists an integer
0\leq k\leq K
such that

\tilde{r}_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(|r|^{k}\right)
on
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\infty}
, where
r
is the function from

7.11
.

Proof.

We write

\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i}(L_{1},L_{2},u))=\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big(p_{i}(\ell_{i}(L_{1},L_{2},u))+r_{i}(\ell_{i}(L_{1},L_{2},u))\big),

replace
\ell_{i}
by its expansion from
7.11
in the polynomial terms, and
expand everything. We obtain four types of terms.

•

On the one hand, some terms are purely polynomial in
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. We group these terms
together; they form the term
Q_{u}
.

•

Then, some terms are a product of
L_{2}^{j}
for a
j\geq 0
and a function

\tilde{r}_{j}(L_{1},u)
containing no polynomial term in
L_{1}
. More precisely, contributions to
this term are products of powers of contributions

r_{1}(\ell_{1}(L_{1},u))=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\ell_{1}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-L_{1}}/u\right)
, as well as the remainder terms

r_{0}(L_{1},u)
and
r(L_{1},u)
from
7.11
. We prove that
\tilde{r}_{j}
satisfies
the claimed bounds using (
7.19
), the boundedness of
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
, and
the fact that
e^{-L}/(u(1-u))=\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
for
L>L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
.

•

There is also the term containing other crossed contributions, which is equal to

L_{1}^{j}\tilde{r}_{j}(L_{2},u)
by symmetry of the roles of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
.

•

And then, all other terms contain at least a factor
\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{K}e^{-\ell_{3}/2}\right)
, or two factors
decaying in
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
respectively, which gives us the two bounds on the error term.

∎

7.6.
Proof of
7.1

We are now finally ready to prove
7.1
. This will be achieved using a reasoning very
similar to the proof of
3.5
, where we showed that the class of functions
\mathcal{F}
is stable
by convolution.

Proof of
7.1
.

We note that the property of being Friedman–Ramanujan is an asymptotic property, and hence we can
restrict ourselves to studying the case that
\ell>\ell_{0}
. As shown in Lemmas

7.3
and
7.4
, the problem reduces to
showing that, for any
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
satisfying (
7.9
),
the function

\mathcal{I}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]:\ell\mapsto\iint_{\mathfrak{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i})\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

is a Friedman–Ramanujan function. We will now omit the mention of
(f_{i})_{i}
and denote the
integral above as
\mathcal{I}
, to simplify notations.

Let us decompose this integral in two integrals
\mathcal{I}^{1,2}
, depending on whether

\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}
or
\ell_{1}\geq\ell_{2}
. By symmetry of the roles of
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
,
if we prove the result for any
(f_{i})_{i}
for
\mathcal{I}^{1}
, the result follows for

\mathcal{I}^{2}
, and hence for
\mathcal{I}
. We can therefore assume without loss of generality
that the integral runs over the set of parameters such that
\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}
, and in
particular
\ell_{1}\leq\frac{\ell}{2}
and
\ell_{2}\geq\frac{\ell}{2}
.

Let us perform the change of variable
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\mapsto(L_{1},L_{2},u)
, and use
Lemmas
7.7
and
7.10
to write

\mathcal{I}^{1}(\ell)=\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\int_{L_{-}(\ell,u)}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i}(L,\ell-L,u))\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u.

We now use
7.12
to express the integrand, and examine the various
contributions appearing successively.

•

We first examine the term coming from the remainder decaying in
e^{-\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}}
, which is bounded by a multiple constant of

\ell^{K}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\int_{L_{-}(\ell,u)}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}e^{-\frac{\ell_{3}(L,\ell-L,u)}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u.

For this term, actually, we return to the old variables
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
, and obtain
that our contribution is bounded by

\ell^{K}\,\mathcal{I}[1,1,e^{-\frac{\cdot}{2}}](\ell)
. By

7.4
,
\mathcal{I}[1,1,e^{-\frac{\cdot}{2}}]
is a function in

\mathcal{R}
because the functions
(1,1,e^{-\frac{\cdot}{2}})
satisfy
(
7.9
) with
p_{3}=0
. Hence, this contribution to
\mathcal{I}^{1}
is
an element of
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}
.

•

The other part of the remainder of
7.12
is bounded by a multiple
constant of

\displaystyle\ell^{K}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\int_{L_{-}(\ell,u)}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}{u(1-u)}\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{K+1}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\left[|\log(u)|+|\log(1-u)|\vphantom{\int}\right]_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{K+2}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\right).

As a consequence, this contribution is also an element of
\mathcal{R}
.

•

We now observe that all of the terms
L_{1}^{j}\tilde{r}_{j}(L_{2},u)
for
0\leq j\leq K
yield
contributions in
\mathcal{R}
, because as soon as
L_{1}\leq L_{2}
,

L_{1}^{j}\tilde{r}_{j}(L_{2},u)=\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{j+K}\frac{e^{-L_{2}}}{u(1-u)}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{j+K}\frac{e^{-\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}}}{u(1-u)}\right)

which is covered by the previous case.

•

Let us now examine what we expect to be the “leading contribution”, coming from
Q_{u}
. We
need to estimate

\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\int_{L_{-}(\ell,u)}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}Q_{u}(L,\ell-L)\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u.

We integrate the polynomial, and expand the powers, to express this quantity as a linear
combination of integrals of the form

(7.26)

\ell^{j_{0}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\log(u)^{j_{1}}\log(1-u)^{j_{2}}L_{-}(\ell,u)^{j_{3}}\,\mathrm{d}u.

Let us replace
L_{-}(\ell,u)
by its approximation
L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
in this expression. We
obtain the quantity

(7.27)

\ell^{j_{0}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\log(u)^{j_{1}}\log(1-u)^{j_{2}}L_{-}^{\infty}(u)^{j_{3}}\,\mathrm{d}u.

By
7.9
(
3
), the function

(0,1)\ni u\mapsto\log(u)^{j_{1}}\log(1-u)^{j_{2}}L_{-}^{\infty}(u)^{j_{3}}
is integrable. Let

C_{\vec{\char 17\relax}}
denotes this integral. The tails of
C_{\vec{\char 17\relax}}
for
u<u_{-}(\ell)
and

u>1-u_{+}(\ell)
are
\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}}e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\right)
by

7.9
(
2
). As a consequence, for
|\vec{\char 17\relax}|:=j_{0}+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}
,

Eq.

7.27
is equal to

C_{\vec{\char 17\relax}}\,\ell^{j_{0}}\,\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{|\vec{\char 17\relax}|}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\right)

which means it is an element of
\mathcal{F}
. We are therefore left to study the error made when
replacing
L_{-}(\ell,u)
by
L_{-}^{\infty}(u)
in
Eq.

7.26
. There is nothing to do if

j_{3}=0
, and if
j_{3}\geq 1
, by (
7.18
),

|L_{-}(\ell,u)^{j_{3}}-L_{-}^{\infty}(u)^{j_{3}}|=\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{j_{3}-1}|L_{-}(\ell,u)-L_{-}^{\infty}(u)|\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{j_{3}}\frac{e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}{1-u}\right)

and hence the error term is bounded by a multiple constant of

\ell^{|\vec{\char 17\relax}|}\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\left|\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}u}{1-u}\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\ell^{|\vec{\char 17\relax}|+1}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\right).

This means that the difference between
Eq.

7.26
and
Eq.

7.27
lies in

\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}
, which allows us to conclude.

•

Last but not least, we examine the contributions of the form

\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\int_{L_{-}(\ell,u)}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}(\ell-L)^{j}\tilde{r}_{j}(L,u)\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u

for
j\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
.
We expand the exponents, so that we are left with a linear combination of integrals of the form

(7.28)

\ell^{j_{0}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\int_{L_{-}(\ell,u)}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}L^{j_{1}}\tilde{r}_{j}(L,u)\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u.

Let us replace the integration over
L\in(L_{-}(\ell,u),\frac{\ell}{2})
by an integration on a
set independent of
\ell
, the interval
(L_{-}(u),+\infty)
.

–

First, using (
7.25
), we prove that the tail
L>\frac{\ell}{2}

is an element of
\mathcal{R}
;

–

For the other tail, where
L_{-}^{\infty}(u)<L<L_{-}(\ell,u)
, we use

7.12
to bound
\tilde{r}_{j}
by a power
r^{k}
of the function
r
from

7.11
. Then, (
7.20
) allows us to prove that this
contribution is an element of
\mathcal{R}
.

As a consequence, (
7.28
) is equal, modulo elements of
\mathcal{R}
, to

\displaystyle\ell^{j_{0}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{u_{-}(\ell)}^{u_{+}(\ell)}\int_{L_{-}^{\infty}(u)}^{+\infty}L^{j_{1}}\tilde{r}_{j}(L,u)\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u.

Finally, we replace the integration on
(u_{-}(\ell),u_{+}(\ell))
by an integration on
(0,1)
. We
use (
7.21
) and (
7.17
) to deduce that the cost of this
substitution is an element of
\mathcal{R}
. As a consequence, (
7.28
) is equal
modulo
\mathcal{R}
to

\ell^{j_{0}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\int_{L_{-}^{\infty}(u)}^{+\infty}L^{j_{1}}\tilde{r}_{j}(L,u)\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}u

which is an element of
\mathcal{F}
, and we have therefore proved our claim.

∎

8.
Extension to any geodesic filling a surface of Euler characteristic
-1

We shall now extend the result of the previous section to any loop filling a
pair of pants or a once-holed torus. More precisely, we prove the following.

Theorem 8.1
.

For any
k\geq 0
, there exists
c_{k}>0
satisfying the following. For any local topological type

T
filling a surface of Euler characteristic
-1
, the function
f_{k}^{T}
is Friedman–Ramanujan in
the weak sense, and furthermore its degree, norm, exponent and constant are smaller than
c_{k}
.

Remark 8.2
.

The statement we prove in
8.1
is slightly stronger than what we need for

Objective (FR)
, because we prove a uniformity with respect to the local type. This uniformity
will be used in
Section

10
, where we prove that

\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon
typically.

The plan of the proof is the following.

•

In
Section

8.1
, we prove the result for local topological types satisfying an
additional hypothesis, “double-filling” loops.

•

In
Section

8.2
, we prove that all local topological types for which
\chi_{S}=-1

are either double-filling, a figure-eight, or elements of a class of local topological types
called “one-sided iterated eights”.

•

It then follows that the only remaining case to examine is the case of one-sided iterated
eights, which is done in
Section

8.3
.

8.1.
The case of double-filling loops

Let
S
be a filling type, and
\gamma
be a loop filling
S
, in minimal
position.

Notation 8.3
.

We say that
\beta
is a
simple portion
of
\gamma
if it is a restriction of
\gamma

which has no self-intersection and does not meet the rest of
\gamma
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a regular neighbourhood of
\gamma
. Every simple portion
\beta
is
bordered by two (non-necessarily distinct) boundary components
b_{\pm}^{\mathcal{V}}(\beta)
of
the regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}
.

Definition 8.4
.

We say that the loop
\gamma
is a
double-filling loop
if, for every
simple portion
\beta
of
\gamma
, at least one of the components

b_{\pm}^{\mathcal{V}}(\beta)
is contractible in
S
.

In order to determine if a loop is double-filling, we take a regular
neighbourhood and highlight the boundary components that are contractible, as
done in
Fig.

13
. The loop is double-filling if and only if
the whole loop is bordered by highlighted boundary components.

Figure 13
.
Three loops (in blue) filling a pair of pants. We highlighted the disks in the complement
of the regular neighbourhood of the loop. The rightmost loop is double-filling while the two
left ones are not.

Remark 8.5
.

This notion does not depend on the choice of the loop
\gamma
in a free-homotopy class. Indeed,
we saw in the proof of
4.3
that two loops homotopic in
S
can be obtained from
one another by a sequence of third Reidemeister moves. Examining
Fig.

4
, we
can see that if a loop is double-filling according to our definition, then it remains so after a
third Reidemeister move.

The double-filling hypothesis is a setting in which
8.1
is easy to prove.

Proposition 8.6
.

For any
k\geq 0
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
satisfying the following. For any local
topological type
T=[S,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, if
\chi_{S}=-1
and
\gamma
is a double-filling loop,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{|f_{k}^{T}(\ell)|}{(\ell+1)^{c_{k}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell\leq c_{k}.

In particular,
f_{k}^{T}
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense,
its degree and norm are
0
, and its constant and exponent are bounded by

c_{k}
.

The key element that allows to prove
8.6
is the following lemma.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
Y
be a bordered hyperbolic surface, and
\gamma
be a double-filling
geodesic on
Y
. Then,
\ell_{Y}(\partial Y)\leq\ell_{Y}(\gamma)
.

This is an improvement of
4.4
, which states that

\ell_{Y}(\partial Y)\leq 2\ell_{Y}(\gamma)
whenever
\gamma
fills
Y
. The double-filling
hypothesis allows us to remove the factor
2
in this length inequality, which comes from the fact
that the regular neighbourhood of
\gamma
has length approximately
2\ell_{Y}(\gamma)
. We first
prove the lemma.

Proof of
8.7
.

Let
\epsilon,\delta>0
, and
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{\delta}(\gamma)
be a
\delta
-regular
neighbourhood of
\gamma
. By definition of a filling loop, each boundary component
b
of
Y
is
homotopic to a non-contractible boundary components
b^{\mathcal{V}}
of
\mathcal{V}
. By
minimality of the length of the geodesic representative in a homotopy class,

\ell_{Y}(b)\leq\ell_{Y}(b^{\mathcal{V}})
for all
b
. Thanks to the double-filling hypothesis,
each
b^{\mathcal{V}}
borders a disjoint simple portion of the loop
\gamma
, and therefore we
can pick
\delta
to be small enough so that

\ell_{Y}(\partial Y)\leq\sum_{b}\ell_{Y}(b^{\mathcal{V}})\leq\ell_{Y}(\gamma)+\epsilon.

This implies the claim taking
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.
∎

We now proceed to the proof of
8.6
.

Proof.

Let us first consider the case of a double-filling loop
\gamma
on the pair of pants
P
, and

T:=[P,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
. Let
k\geq 0
be an integer. By the proof of
5.14
,
see
5.23
, for any admissible function
F
,

(8.1)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}f_{k}^{T}(\ell)F(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell=\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}F(\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}))\,\psi_{k}^{P}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\,\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}

where
\psi_{k}^{P}
is the
k
-th term of the asymptotic expansion of the
function
\phi_{g}^{P}
, expressed in
5.7
, and

\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the function which, to the
lengths of the three boundary components of
P
, associates the length of the
geodesic
\gamma
. We take
c>0
to be a parameter that we will adjust
later. For any
L>0
, we apply (
8.1
) to the
admissible function

\ell\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{sign}(f_{k}^{T}(\ell))}{(\ell+1)^{c}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}(\ell)

and let
L\rightarrow+\infty
. We obtain that

(8.2)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{|f_{k}^{T}(\ell)|}{(\ell+1)^{c}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}\frac{|\psi_{k}^{P}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})|}{(\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})+1)^{c}e^{\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}.

We then use the double-filling hypothesis, and
8.7
, to
deduce that

(8.3)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{|f_{k}^{T}(\ell)|}{(\ell+1)^{c}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}\frac{|\psi_{k}^{P}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})|}{(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}+1)^{c}e^{\frac{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}.

5.21
allows us to write
\psi_{k}^{P}
as a linear
combination of certain kinds of functions, and it is easy to check that we can
take a
c:=c_{k}
such that (
8.3
) is

\mathcal{O}_{k}\left(1\right)
.

If now
\gamma
is a double-filling loop on the once-holed torus
S_{1,1}
, then
(
8.1
) is replaced by

\int_{0}^{+\infty}f_{k}^{T}(\ell)F(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{V_{1,1}(x)}\int_{\mathcal{T}_{1,1}(x)}\,F(\ell_{Y}(\gamma))\,\psi_{k}^{(1,1)}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{1,1,x}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y)\,\mathrm{d}x.

We shall prove that, for any
\ell,x>0
,

(8.4)

\mathrm{Vol}_{1,1,x}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(\{Y\in\mathcal{T}_{1,1}(x)\,:\,\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\leq\ell\})\leq 4\ell^{2}.

This allows to bound the integral over
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}(x)
, and then the rest of the proof is
exactly identical to the pair of pants case above.

Let us equip
S_{1,1}
with an arbitrary hyperbolic structure, and assume that
\gamma
is the
geodesic representative for this metric. We consider a parametrisation

\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow S_{1,1}
. The loop
\gamma
is filling and therefore not simple, and
we can therefore define

t_{+}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,:\,\exists s\in[0,t)\,:\,\gamma(s)=\gamma(t)\}

and
t_{-}<t_{+}
so that
z:=\gamma(t_{-})=\gamma(t_{+})
. Then, the restriction

\gamma_{|[t_{-},t_{+}]}
is a simple loop
\alpha
on
S_{1,1}
based at
z
.

The surface
S_{1,1}\setminus\alpha
is a topological pair of pants
P
. We denote as
\alpha_{1}

and
\alpha_{2}
the two boundary components of
P
corresponding to
\alpha
, so that the path

\gamma
enters
P
through
\alpha_{1}
at the time
t_{+}
. We denote as
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
the copies
of
z
on
P
, following this numbering convention.

Let us consider a small enough
\epsilon>0
such that

\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}:=\{w\in P\,:\,0<\mathrm{dist}(w,\alpha_{2})<\epsilon\}
is a
cylinder. The loop
\gamma
fills the surface
S_{1,1}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
is an annulus
that is not homotopic to
\partial S_{1,1}
, and hence the set
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
cannot lie
entirely in the complement of
\gamma
. We deduce that there exists a restriction
\beta_{0}
of

\gamma
(or
\gamma^{-1}
) going from a point of
\alpha_{2}
to a point of
\partial P
which
cannot be homotoped to a path on
\alpha_{2}
. Because
\gamma
does not intersect

\partial S_{1,1}
, there are two possibilities for the topology of
\beta_{0}
, represented in

Fig.

14
.

(a)

The case (
1
).

(b)

Two possible scenarios in case (
2
).

Figure 14
.
Illustration of the proof of
Eq.

8.4
.

In both those cases, let us prove that can use pieces of
\gamma
to create a simple closed loop

\beta
on
S_{1,1}
that intersects
\alpha
exactly once.

(1)

If
\beta_{0}
goes from
\alpha_{2}
to
\alpha_{1}
, we
extract a simple sub-path
\beta_{1}
from it by iteratively removing the portion of
\beta_{0}

restricted to
[u_{-},u_{+}]
for any times
u_{-}<u_{+}
such that
\beta_{0}(u_{-})=\beta_{0}(u_{+})
,
until no intersections are left. We then take a sub-path
a_{2}
of
\alpha_{2}
from
z_{2}
to the
beginning of
\beta_{1}
, and a sub-path
a_{1}
of
\alpha_{1}
from the endpoint of
\beta_{1}
to

z_{1}
. The concatenation
\beta:=a_{2}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}a_{1}
satisfies the
hypotheses.

(2)

If
\beta_{0}
goes from
\alpha_{2}
to itself, then by
construction it is not homotopic with fixed endpoints to a portion of
\alpha_{2}
. We use this
information to find a topological cylinder
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
which has
\alpha_{1}
as one boundary
component, and portions of
\beta_{0}
and perhaps
\alpha_{2}
as the other. The path
\gamma

enters
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
at time
t_{+}
, and cannot be homotoped to a sub-path of
\alpha_{1}

(otherwise
S_{1,1}
would contain a geodesic bigon). As a consequence, it has to escape the
cylinder
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
by its other boundary component. We concatenate the portion of
\gamma

from
t_{+}
to its escape of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, as well as portions of
\beta_{0}
, and finally a
portion of
\alpha_{2}
from the starting point of
\beta_{0}
to
z_{2}
, to construct
\beta
.

Then, by construction,

\forall(x,Y)\in\mathcal{T}_{1,1}(x),\quad\begin{cases}\ell_{Y}(\alpha)\leq\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\\
\ell_{Y}(\beta)\leq 2\ell_{Y}(\gamma).\end{cases}

For
x>0
, we consider the Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates
(\ell_{1},\tau_{1})
on

\mathcal{T}_{1,1}(x)
so that
\ell_{1}
is equal to
\ell_{Y}(\alpha)
, and the geodesics homotopic
to
\alpha
and
\beta
are orthogonal when
\tau_{1}=0
. Then, an elementary computation yields

\cosh(\ell_{Y}(\gamma))\geq\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\beta)}{2}\right)=\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\right)\geq\cosh\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\right)

and therefore, for any
\ell>0
,

\{(\ell_{1},\tau_{1})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,\ell_{Y}(\gamma)\leq\ell\}\subset[0,\ell]\times[-2\ell,2\ell]

which is enough to conclude to (
8.4
).
∎

8.2.
Classification of filling loops that do not double-fill

Thanks to Sections
7
and

8.1
, we now know that
8.1
holds for
figure-eights and double-filling loops. We have seen, at the centre of

Fig.

13
, an example of a loop filling a pair of pants which
is neither a double-filling loop nor a figure-eight. We make the following
definition to refer to this loop, and other similar examples.

Figure 15
.
Two distinct one-sided figure eights with
j=3
iterations.

Notation 8.8
.

We call the local topological types represented in
Fig.

15

one-sided
iterated eights
, and refer to the integer
j
as their
number of iterations
. For any

j\geq 2
, we denote as
\alpha_{j}
the loop on the left-hand side of
Fig.

15
.

We prove the following.

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
S
be a filling type of Euler characteristic
-1
. Then, for any loop
\gamma
filling
S
,
one and only of the two following occurs:

•

either
\gamma
is double-filling;

•

or
S
is a pair of pants, and
\gamma
is a figure-eight or a one-sided iterated eight.

As a consequence, one-sided iterated eights are the only remaining case that we need to consider to
prove
Objective (FR)
when
\chi_{S}=-1
. We shall do so in
Section

8.3
.

Proof.

First, let
S=P
be a pair of pants, and
\gamma
be a filling loop that is
not double-filling. Let us prove that
\gamma
is a figure-eight or a
one-sided iterated eight.

Figure 16
.
Illustration of the proof in the pair of pants case.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a regular neighbourhood of
\gamma
. By definition of double-filling loopss,
there exists a simple portion of
\gamma
, denoted as
\beta
, such that

b_{\pm}^{\mathcal{V}}:=b_{\pm}^{\mathcal{V}}(\beta)
are not contractible. They therefore are
homotopic to two (non-necessarily distinct) boundary components
b_{\pm}
of
P
. Let us pick a
point
z
on
\beta
, two neighbours
z_{\pm}
of
z
on
b_{\pm}^{\mathcal{V}}
, and a simple path

c_{0}
going from
z_{+}
to
z_{-}
by crossing the regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}
transversally,
which only intersects
\gamma
at the point
z
. For
\sigma\in\{+,-\}
, since

b_{\sigma}^{\mathcal{V}}
and
b_{\sigma}
delimit an annulus, there exists a simple path
c_{\sigma}

entirely contained in this annulus going from
b_{\sigma}
to
z_{\sigma}
. Then, we let

\alpha:=c_{+}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}c_{0}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}c_{-}^{-1}
, as represented in the left part of

Fig.

16
. By construction, the path
\alpha
is simple, and only intersects

\gamma
once, at
z
. Let us cut the pair of pants
P
along the simple path
\alpha
going from

b_{+}
to
b_{-}
.

If
b_{+}=b_{-}
, then the path
\alpha
would separate the pair of pants
P
it into two connected
components, while it intersects
\gamma
only once. This is impossible because the loop
\gamma

is closed. As a consequence,
b_{+}\neq b_{-}
, and the result we obtain by cutting
P
along

\alpha
is therefore an annulus
\mathcal{A}
, as represented on the middle part of

Fig.

16
.

The point
z
corresponds to two marked points
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
on one boundary component of the
annulus. Because
\gamma
only intersects
\alpha
once, at
z
, the path
\gamma
corresponds to
a path
\gamma^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{A}
, going from
z_{1}
to
z_{2}
. The fundamental group of an annulus
is
\mathbb{Z}
, and therefore
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic with fixed endpoint to

t_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}b_{0}^{j}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}t_{2}^{-1}
for a
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
b_{0}
is the boundary
component of
\mathcal{A}
on which
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
do not lie, corresponding to the third
boundary component of the pair of pants, and
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
are paths from
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
to a
shared fixed point of
b_{0}
(see the right-hand side of
Fig.

16
). It follows
that
\gamma
is a figure-eight or a one-sided iterated eight.

Figure 17
.
Illustration of the proof in the once-holed torus case.

Now, let us assume that
S
is the once-holed torus
S_{1,1}
, and let
\gamma
be a loop on

S_{1,1}
that is not double-filling. We prove that
\gamma
does not fill
S_{1,1}
. Indeed, let
us construct
b_{-}
,
b_{+}
,
\alpha
be as above. The once-holed torus
S_{1,1}
only has one
boundary component, i.e.
b_{-}=b_{+}=:b
. The surface
S_{1,1}\setminus\alpha
has Euler
characteristic
0
and cannot be disconnected, since
\gamma
is a closed loop intersecting

\alpha
exactly once. As a consequence, it is an annulus. Let
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
denote the two
copies of
z
, that lie on the two distinct boundary components of the annulus. The fundamental
group of an annulus is
\mathbb{Z}
, so the path
\gamma
on the annulus can be written as

t_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}b_{0}^{j}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}t_{2}^{-1}
for a
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
b_{0}
is the core of
the annulus and
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
are two paths connecting
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
to a shared point of
b_{0}
,
as represented on
Fig.

17
. This path admits a simple representative in
the homotopy class with fixed endpoints, which corresponds to a simple loop on
S_{1,1}
homotopic
to
\gamma
. By minimality of the intersection number for the geodesic representation, this
implies that
\gamma
is simple. In particular,
\gamma
does not fill
S_{1,1}
, which was our
claim.

Because figure-eights and one-sided iterated eights are not double-filling, it
is clear the two cases are mutually exclusive.
∎

8.3.
Proof of the conjecture for one-sided iterated eights

Let us now proceed to the last step towards a proof of
8.1
, which consists in
proving it for any one-sided iterated eight.
For any
j\geq 2
, there are exactly two distinct local types of one-sided iterated eights with
j

iterations, represented on
Fig.

15
. Because these two types only differ by
orientation, we shall only study the local type
[P,\alpha_{j}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
. Let us prove the following
statement.

Proposition 8.10
.

For any
k\geq 0
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
satisfying the
following. For any
j\geq 2
, the function
f_{k}^{[P,\alpha_{j}]_{\mathrm{loc}}}
is
Friedman–Ramanujan, and its degree, norm and constant are smaller than
c_{k}
.

The proof is extremely similar to the case of the figure eight, done in detail
in
Section

7
, and we shall therefore only sketch it.
The first step we need to take is to compute the length-formula relating the
length of the geodesic
\alpha_{j}
to the three boundary lengths of the pair of pants,
generalising
Eq.

7.2
for
j\geq 1
. This leads to the
following.

Lemma 8.11
.

The expression for
(f_{k}^{[P,\alpha_{1}]_{\mathrm{loc}}})_{k\geq 0}
from

7.3
, true for the figure-eight, can be extended to any local
topology
[P,\alpha_{j}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
for
j\geq 2
by replacing the length-function
\mathcal{L}
in the
level-set integrals by the new length-function
\mathcal{L}_{j}
satisfying

(8.5)

\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{j}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})}{2}\right)=\frac{\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{(j+1)\ell_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\cdot

The family of functions
(f_{i})_{i}
and the coefficients that appear in this
expansion does not depend on the parameter
j
.

Proof.

We simply follow the exact same steps as in Sections

7.1
to
7.6
. The only
difference here is that the expression of the length,
(
7.2
) in the case of the figure eight,
and (
8.5
) here. We prove that, for any
j\geq 2
,

(8.6)

\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{j}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})}{2}\right)=2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{j-1}(\ell_{3},\ell_{2},\ell_{1})}{2}\right).

We then obtain (
8.5
) by a straightforward induction.

The proof of
Eq.

8.6
is a geometric argument, that uses the interpretation of the
unit tangent bundle of the pair of pants
P
as a quotient of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and the
formula
\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma)}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}|{\rm tr}(\gamma)|
true for any hyperbolic element

\gamma
of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Because these tools only appear in this proof, we will not
provide more introductory detail, and invite the reader to refer to
[
19
]
for more
details.

Figure 18
.
The geometric construction for the proof of
Eq.

8.6
.

The following construction is illustrated on
Fig.

18
. Let
\beta_{j-1}
denote
the one-sided iterated eight obtained by going once around
\gamma_{3}
and
j-1
times
around
\gamma_{2}
, which has length
\mathcal{L}_{j-1}(\ell_{3},\ell_{2},\ell_{1})
. Let
p
denote
the common perpendicular of
\beta_{j-1}
with itself represented in
Fig.

18
–
we shall also use the notation
p
for its length. The endpoints of
p
delimits two
paths
\beta_{j-1}^{1}
and
\beta_{j-1}^{2}
obtained from
\beta_{j-1}
, of respective lengths
denoted by
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
. Then, the one-sided iterated eight
\alpha_{j}
, of length

\mathcal{L}_{j}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
, is freely homotopic to

\beta_{j-1}^{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\beta_{j-1}^{2}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p
. Transporting the vector

v
represented on
Fig.

18
along this trajectory using elements of

\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we obtain that

(8.7)

\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{j}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,|{\rm tr}(a_{t_{1}}w_{p}a_{t_{2}}w_{-p})|

where

a_{t}:=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\frac{t}{2}}&0\\
0&e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad w_{p}:=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)&\sinh\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\\
\sinh\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)&\cosh\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\end{pmatrix}

respectively correspond to moving the vector
v
in a straight direction for a
length
t
, or along the orthogonal geodesic in the right direction for the
(algebraic) length
p
. We rewrite (
8.7
) as

(8.8)

\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{j}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,|{\rm tr}(A_{1}A_{2})|

where

(8.9)

A_{1}:=a_{t_{1}}w_{p}R,\quad A_{2}:=R^{-1}a_{t_{2}}w_{-p},\quad\text{with}\quad R:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

In
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have that

(8.10)

{\rm tr}(A_{1}A_{2})={\rm tr}(A_{1})\,{\rm tr}(A_{2})+{\rm tr}(-A_{1}A_{2}^{-1}).

We observe by a direct computation that for
i=1,2
,

{\rm tr}(A_{i})=2\sinh\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{t_{i}}{2}\right)>0
, and the path described by

A_{i}
is freely homotopic to the closed geodesic
\gamma_{1}
for
i=1

and
\gamma_{2}^{j}
for
i=2
. Hence,

(8.11)

{\rm tr}(A_{1})=2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm tr}(A_{2})=2\cosh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right).

We use that
R^{-1}=-R
,
a_{t_{2}}^{-1}=a_{-t_{2}}
,
w_{p}^{-1}=w_{-p}
and

R\,w_{p}a_{-t_{2}}\,R^{-1}=w_{-p}a_{t_{2}}
to obtain

(8.12)

{\rm tr}(-A_{1}A_{2}^{-1})={\rm tr}(a_{t_{1}}w_{p}R\,w_{p}a_{-t_{2}}R^{-1})={\rm tr}(a_{t_{1}}a_{t_{2}})=2\cosh\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{j-1}(\ell_{3},\ell_{2},\ell_{1})}{2}\right).

The conclusion then follows from

Eqs.

8.7
,
8.10
,
8.11
and
8.12
.
∎

In the case of the pair of pants,
7.4
allowed us to
deal with a few elementary cases. We easily adapt it to this new setting, and
prove the following.

Lemma 8.12
.

For any
j\geq 2
, the integrals of the form (
7.8
) are always
elements of
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}
, and so is the integral (
7.7
) as soon
as
p_{1}=0
or
p_{3}=0
. The estimate on the remainders can be made uniformly in
j
.

Proof.

We only sketch the proof for
p_{1}=0
, because this case is actually new
compared to before (in the case of the eight, we needed to assume

p_{1}=p_{2}=0
). The difference is the fact that, now,

\frac{\partial\ell_{3}}{\partial\ell}=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)}

and hence the integral we need to bound is

\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\iint\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i})\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\frac{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\mathds{1}_{I(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}

which, if
p_{1}=0
, is clearly an element of
\mathcal{R}
, uniformly in
j
,
because for any
j\geq 2
,

\frac{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\leq\frac{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh(\ell_{2})}=\mathcal{O}\left(1\right).

∎

Figure 19
.
The coordinates for the one-sided iterated eight.

For the rest of the proof, we need to pick new variables, suited to the
integration on the new level-set

\{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}\,:\,\mathcal{L}_{j}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell\}
. We choose the natural adaptation of
the case of the figure-eight, illustrated in
Fig.

19
. More
precisely,
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
denote the lengths of the two portions of
\alpha_{j}

going around
\gamma_{1}
(once) and
\gamma_{2}
(
j
times) respectively, so that

\ell_{P}(\alpha_{j})=L_{1}+L_{2}
. We once again let
u:=\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)
,
where
\theta
is the angle represented on
Fig.

19
. We
then prove the following formula for the change of variables, generalising

7.6
for
j\geq 1
.

Lemma 8.13
.

For any
j\geq 1
, any
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}>0
,
