{}\begin{cases}&\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)=\sqrt{u}\,\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\\
&\cosh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)=\sqrt{u}\,\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)\\
&\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)=a_{j}(\ell_{2})\left[(1-b_{j}(\ell_{2})\,u)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)-b_{j}(\ell_{2})\,u\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}-L_{2}}{2}\right)\right]\\
\end{cases}

where

a_{j}(\ell_{2}):=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{j}(\ell_{2}):=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{(j+1)\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{2\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\cdot

We then proceed to compute the expression of the Jacobian of the change of
variables.

Lemma 8.14
.

For any
j\geq 1
, the change of variable

(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})\rightarrow(L_{1},L_{2},u)
is a diffeomorphism from

\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}
to an open set
\mathcal{E}_{j}
of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}\times(0,1)
, and
its Jacobian can be written as

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{3}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{i}=-\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)\frac{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{j\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\,\mathrm{d}L_{1}\,\mathrm{d}L_{2}\,\mathrm{d}u.

Proof.

In order to compute the Jacobian, we shall use the expression of
\ell_{3}

derived in
8.12
:

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)-\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{(j+1)\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{expr}(L_{1},L_{2},\ell_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},u),\ell_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},u)).

We observe that, by the chain rule, we can write the derivative of the
previous equation according to
L_{1}
as:

\frac{1}{2}\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)\frac{\partial\ell_{3}}{\partial L_{1}}=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{2\sinh\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)+\frac{\partial\ell_{1}}{\partial L_{1}}\partial_{3}\mathrm{expr}+\frac{\partial\ell_{2}}{\partial L_{1}}\partial_{4}\mathrm{expr}

and similar expressions are valid for the derivation with respect to
L_{2}
and
u
. The advantage
of these expressions is that it makes it clear that, when we compute the Jacobian, we can remove
from the column of the variable
\ell_{3}
the crossed terms in which the derivatives

\partial_{3}\mathrm{expr}
and
\partial_{4}\mathrm{expr}
appear, by subtracting multiples of the
columns from the variables
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
. We can therefore reduce ourselves to computing
the determinant

\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{1}}{2}\right)j\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{3}}{2}\right)}\begin{vmatrix}\sqrt{u}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)&0&\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)\\
0&\sqrt{u}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)&\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}+L_{2}}{2}\right)\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)&\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\cosh\left(\frac{L_{2}}{2}\right)&0\end{vmatrix}

which yields the claimed expression.
∎

We will then be able to conclude using the following.

Proposition 8.15
.

Let
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}
be functions satisfying
(
7.9
). For any
j\geq 2
, the function

(8.13)

\ell\mapsto\iint_{L_{1}+L_{2}=\ell}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{i}(\ell_{i})\right)\frac{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}{j\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{j\ell_{2}}{2}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{j}}(L_{1},L_{2},u)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}L_{1}\,\mathrm{d}L_{2}\,\mathrm{d}u}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

is equal to a polynomial function up to an error of size

\mathcal{O}\left((\ell+1)^{c}e^{-\ell/2}\right)
. Furthermore, the degree and coefficients of the
polynomial, as well as
c
and the implied constant, can be bounded uniformly
in
j
.

The proof of
8.15
is a straightforward adaptation of the
proof in the case of the figure-eight, thanks to the similarity of the formulas
for the changes of variables. Actually, it is slightly more elementary thanks to
the additional decay in
\ell_{2}
in
Eq.

8.13
, which removes the
polynomial behaviour of
\ell_{2}
and hence allows to consider the dependency in

\ell_{2}
as a “remainder term” directly.

9.
The second-order term is not a Friedman–Ramanujan function

In this section, we shall prove the following result, the hyperbolic surface
counterpart of Theorem 2.12 from
[
10
]
for random regular graphs.

Theorem 9.1
.

The function
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is not a Friedman–Ramanujan function in
the weak sense.

We shall prove this by contradiction, grouping the following two observations.

•

On the one hand, in
Section

9.1
, we prove that

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)
is not very small as
g\rightarrow+\infty
(for a fixed
a
). Indeed,
we know that this probability goes to
0
as soon as
a<\frac{3}{16}
by

[
45
,
21
]
, but we show that it does so at the speed
1/g
as

g\rightarrow+\infty
only, and no faster.

•

On the other hand, in
Section

9.2
, we show that, if

\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
was Friedman–Ramanujan in the weak sense, then we would be able
to prove that
\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)
goes to
0
at a rate
1/g^{1+\delta}
for a
\delta>0
.

9.1
could appear to be a contradiction, because we have shown that

f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}
is the sum of
f_{1}^{T}
for all local types
T
that are simple or filling a
surface of Euler characteristic
-1
, and
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{T}(\ell)
is Friedman–Ramanujan in
the weak sense for all those types by
3.3
and
8.1
. However, the
class
\mathcal{F}_{w}
is not stable by countable summation, so there is no contradiction.

The reader is invited to read this section thinking of the fact that the issue causing that

\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)\notin\mathcal{F}_{w}
is the possible existence of
tangles
, as
introduced in
[
32
,
21
]
for hyperbolic surfaces, and

[
10
,
4
]
for random regular graphs. We shall see that tangles are small
pairs of pants or once-holed tori, which contain “too many” closed geodesics, and cause the first
non-zero eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
to be small.

9.1.
Estimate of the probability of having a small eigenvalue

Let us prove the following result on the speed of convergence of

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)
as
g\rightarrow+\infty
.

Theorem 9.2
.

There exists
a_{0},c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that, for any
a\leq a_{0}
, any large enough
g
,

(9.1)

c_{1}\frac{a^{2}}{g}\leq\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)\leq c_{2}\,\frac{a}{g}\cdot

The proof of the upper and lower bounds are different, and will be treated separately.

9.1.1.
The lower bound

In order to prove the lower bound, we shall prove that, if a surface contains a
once-holed torus with a short boundary, then it has a small eigenvalue.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
X
be a compact hyperbolic surface of genus
g\geq 2
. We assume
that
X
contains an embedded once-holed torus
\mathcal{T}
with geodesic boundary, and
that
\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})\leq 1
. Then,
\lambda_{1}(X)\leq\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})
.

Proof.

The min-max principle allows to bound eigenvalues in terms of well-chosen
Rayleigh quotients. A classic application, that can be found in
[
7
, Theorem
8.2.1]
for instance, states that, if
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in H^{1}(X)

are
L^{2}
-orthogonal, then

(9.2)

\lambda_{1}(X)\leq\max\left\{\frac{\int_{X}\|\grad\phi_{1}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{X}|\phi_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu},\frac{\int_{X}\|\grad\phi_{2}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{X}|\phi_{2}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\},

where
\grad
and
\,\mathrm{d}\mu
are the gradient and volume form associated to the metric on
X
.

By the collar lemma
[
7
, Theorem 4.1.1]
, the neighbourhood of width

\arcsinh\left(\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})}{2}\right)^{-1}\right)

of
\partial T
is isometric to a cylinder. As soon
as
\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})\leq 1
, this width is larger than
\arcsinh(1/\sinh(1/2))>1
. We can
therefore define two elements of
H^{1}(X)
by

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(z):=\min(1,\mathrm{dist}(z,X\setminus\mathcal{T}))\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{2}(z):=\min(1,\mathrm{dist}(z,\mathcal{T})).

These functions are
L^{2}
-orthogonal because they have disjoint support, and we
can therefore apply the min-max principle to them. Let us estimate the Rayleigh
quotients appearing in
Eq.

9.2
.

First, the norm of the gradient of
\phi_{1}
is equal to
1
on the set

\mathcal{T}^{-}:=\{z\in\mathcal{T}\,:\,\mathrm{dist}(z,X\setminus\mathcal{T})<1\},

and
0
outside of it. Therefore,

\int_{X}\|\grad\phi_{1}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\area(\mathcal{T}^{-})=\int_{0}^{\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})}\int_{0}^{1}\cosh\rho\,\mathrm{d}\rho\,\mathrm{d}t=\sinh(1)\;\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})

where the area is computed using Fermi coordinates (see
[
7
, Section 1.1]
).
We then observe that
\phi_{1}
is identically equal to
1
in
\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}^{-}
, and hence

\int_{X}|\phi_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\geq\area(\mathcal{T})-\area(\mathcal{T}^{-})\geq 2\pi-\sinh(1)\,\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})\geq\pi

provided that
\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})\leq\pi/\sinh 1
, which is the case by hypothesis.

The function
\phi_{2}
satisfies the same bounds.
Therefore,
Eq.

9.2
implies that

\lambda_{1}(X)\leq\sinh(1)\,\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})/\pi\leq\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})
, which is our
claim.
∎

We then estimate the probability for a surface to contain a small once-holed torus.

Lemma 9.4
.

There exists
a_{0},c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that, for any
a\leq a_{0}
, any large enough
g
,

(9.3)

c_{1}\,\frac{a^{2}}{g}\leq\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\exists\text{ once-holed torus }\mathcal{T}\subset X\,:\,\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})\leq a\right)\leq c_{2}\,\frac{a^{2}}{g}\cdot

The proof of the lower bound of
9.2
then directly follows,
because for any small enough
a
and large enough
g
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)\underset{\text{Lemma }\ref{lem:minmax}}{\geq}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\exists\text{ once-holed torus }\mathcal{T}\subset X\,:\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})\leq a\right)\underset{\text{Lemma }\ref{lem:once_holed}}{\geq}c_{1}\frac{a^{2}}{g}\cdot

We actually only need the lower bound part of
9.4
to conclude to the lower bound
of
9.2
, but it is the hard part of the statement.

The proof of
9.4
is inspired by Mirzakhani’s proof of the fact that

c_{1}\,a^{2}\leq\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\exists\text{ closed geodesic }\gamma\text{ on }X\,:\,\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq a\right)\leq c_{2}\,a^{2},

done in
[
27
, Theorem 4.2]
. We adapt it to count small once-holed tori rather than small
closed geodesics.

Proof.

For
a>0
, let
\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}(X)
denote the number of once-holed tori
\mathcal{T}
with geodesic boundary
embedded in
X
such that
\ell_{X}(\partial\mathcal{T})\leq a
. Let us estimate
\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq 1\right)
.

Step 1: expectation estimate.
It is easy to compute the expectation of
the random variable
\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}
, because it is a geometric function, and we can
therefore apply Mirzakhani’s integration formula:

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\big]=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{a}V_{1,1}(x)\,V_{g-1,1}(x)\,x\,\mathrm{d}x.

We know by
[
33
]
that
V_{1,1}(x)=\pi^{2}/12+x^{2}/48
. We then replace
V_{g-1,1}(x)

by its first-order approximation, see (
2.8
), and obtain that

(9.4)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\big]=\frac{V_{g-1,1}}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{a}\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}+\frac{x^{2}}{12}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{V_{g-1,1}}{gV_{g}}\int_{0}^{a}(1+x)^{4}\,e^{\frac{x}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right).

Since
1/2<\pi^{2}/12<1
, we can take
a_{0}
to be small enough so that

(9.5)

\forall x\in[0,a_{0}],\quad\frac{x}{2}\leq\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}+\frac{x^{2}}{12}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\leq x.

By equations (
2.4
) and (
2.5
), the
volume ratio is

(9.6)

\frac{V_{g-1,1}}{V_{g}}=\frac{V_{g-1,1}}{V_{g-1,2}}\,\frac{V_{g-1,2}}{V_{g}}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}g}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{g^{2}}\right).

Equations (
9.4
) to (
9.6
) together imply the
existence of constants
c_{1},c_{2}
(depending on
a_{0}
) such that, for any
a<a_{0}
,

(9.7)

c_{1}\frac{a^{2}}{g}\leq\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\big]\leq c_{2}\frac{a^{2}}{g}\cdot

Step 2: from the expectation to the probability.

By Markov’s inequality, we can directly obtain the upper bound part of the claim:

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq 1\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\big]\leq c_{2}\,\frac{a^{2}}{g}\cdot

The other side of the inequality is harder to obtain because there is no lower bound on

\mathbb{P}(N\geq 1)
in term of
\mathbb{E}[N]
in general. We shall express the
expectation of
\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}
as

(9.8)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq k\right)=\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq 1\right)+\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq k\right).

Thanks to this expression, we observe that in order to prove that

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq 1\right)
is bounded below by
c_{1}^{\prime}a^{2}/g
, it is sufficient to prove
that
\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq k\right)
is negligible compared to

a^{2}/g
. This reduces the problem to studying the probability for a typical
surface to contain multiple small once-hole tori.

Let
X
be a compact hyperbolic surface of genus
g
. Let us describe the topology of families of
embedded once-holed tori in the surface
X
. In order to make the discussion simpler, let us
assume that
a_{0}\leq 2\arcsinh 1
. Then, by the collar lemma
[
7
, Theorem 4.1.6]
,
all closed geodesics shorter than
a_{0}
on
X
are pairwise disjoint. As a consequence, if
X

contains
k
once-holed tori
\mathcal{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{T}_{k}
of boundary lengths
\leq a\leq a_{0}
, then
they are all disjoint (here, we assume that
g>2
, so that no two embedded once-holed tori can
share a boundary component). Let
C
denote the surface obtained by removing all of these
once-holed tori from
X
. Since the once-holed tori only have one boundary component and the
surface
X
is connected,
C
is also connected. It has
k
boundary components (corresponding to
the
k
once-holed tori). By additivity of the Euler characteristic, the genus of
C
is
g-k
,
and in particular
k\leq g
.

Figure 20
.
The multi-curve
\Gamma_{k}
that separates
k
once-holed tori off a
surface of genus
g
.

Therefore, for any
1\leq k\leq g
, there is exactly one topological type of
family of
k
once-holed tori in a surface of genus
g
(and none for

k>g
). Let us take a multi-curve
\Gamma_{k}
of this topological type on the
base surface
S_{g}
, as represented in
Fig.

20
. We consider the
function

F_{a,k}:\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})&\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathds{1}_{[0,a]}(x_{i}).\end{cases}

Then, the geometric function
X\mapsto F_{a,k}^{\Gamma_{k}}(X)
defined by (
2.2
)
exactly counts the number of undordered families of
k
embedded once-holed tori with boundary
length
\leq a
. Hence,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq k\right)=\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(F_{a,k}^{\Gamma_{k}}\geq 1\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Big[F_{a,k}^{\Gamma_{k}}\Big]

by Markov’s inequality. We compute the expectation of this geometric function
using Mirzakhani’s integration formula:

(9.9)

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq k\right)\leq\frac{1}{k!}\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{[0,a]^{k}}V_{g-k,k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{k}V_{1,1}(x_{i})\,x_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}.

We use once again
[
33
]
to express the volume
V_{1,1}(x_{i})
. By

Eq.

2.7
and
[
27
, Lemma 3.2]
, there exists a constant
C>0

independent of
k
such that

\frac{V_{g-k,k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})}{V_{g}}\leq\frac{C^{k}}{g^{k}}\exp\left(\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{k}}{2}\right).

Then,
Eq.

9.9
becomes

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq k\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{C^{k}}{k!}\frac{1}{g^{k}}\left[\int_{0}^{a}\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}+\frac{x^{2}}{48}\right)x\exp\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\right]^{k}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{C^{k}}{k!}\frac{a^{2k}}{g^{k}}\right),

provided we picked
a_{0}
to be small enough.
We deduce from the previous inequality that

(9.10)

\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\mathcal{T}}\geq k\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}\frac{C^{k}}{k!}\frac{a^{2k}}{g^{k}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{a^{4}}{g^{2}}\,e^{\frac{Ca^{2}}{g}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{a^{4}}{g^{2}}\right)

which is negligible compared to
\frac{a^{2}}{g}
for small enough
a
and large
enough
g
. Our claim then follows directly by putting together

Eqs.

9.7
,
9.8
and
9.10
.
∎

9.1.2.
The upper bound

Let us now prove the upper bound part of
9.2
. It cannot be
done the same way as the lower bound. Indeed, we have used the fact that, by the
min-max principle, the presence of a small once-holed torus implies the presence
of a small eigenvalue. But the converse is not true, and the existence of a
small eigenvalue does not imply that the surface contains a small once-holed
torus.
We shall therefore rather rely on another geometric quantity, the Cheeger
constant
h(X)
, defined by

h(X):=\inf_{A\sqcup B=X}\left\{\frac{\ell(\partial A)}{\min(\area(A),\area(B))}\right\}

where the infimum is taken over all partitions
A\sqcup B
of
X
into two
smooth connected components. Cheeger’s inequality
[
9
]
states that

(9.11)

\lambda_{1}(X)\geq\frac{h(X)^{2}}{4}

and in particular, if
\lambda_{1}(X)
is small, then
h(X)
is too (the converse
is also true by Buser’s inequality
[
6
]
).

The Cheeger constant
h(X)
is a priori difficult to estimate for random Weil–Petersson surfaces,
because it does not depend only on geometric functions. In
[
27
, Section 4.5]
,
Mirzakhani defined a
modified Cheeger constant

H(X)
. It is defined the same way as
h(X)

is, except the infimum ranges over all partitions
A\sqcup B
of
X
into two connected components
such that
\partial A
is a
union of disjoint simple geodesics
. Mirzakhani proved in

[
27
, Proposition 4.6]
that the two Cheeger constants satisfy the inequality

(9.12)

\frac{H(X)}{1+H(X)}\leq h(X)\leq H(X)

where the upper bound is trivial. In particular,
h(X)
is small if and only if

H(X)
is.

We shall use the following probabilistic estimate on
H(X)
.

Lemma 9.5
.

There exists
c,a_{0}>0
such that, for any
a\leq a_{0}
and any large enough
g
,

(9.13)

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H(X)\leq a\right)\leq c\frac{a^{2}}{g}\cdot

The upper bound of
9.2
then follows, because for any small
enough
a
and large enough
g
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)\underset{\eqref{eq:cheeger_ineq}}{\leq}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(h(X)\leq 2\sqrt{a}\right)\underset{\eqref{eq:mirz_cheeger}}{\leq}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H(X)\leq\frac{2\sqrt{a}}{1-2\sqrt{a}}\right)\underset{\eqref{eq:mirz_prob_cheeger}}{\leq}c_{2}\,\frac{a}{g}\cdot

9.5
comes as a consequence of the proof of
[
27
, Theorem
4.8]
, also it is not stated as such. For the sake of self-containment, we repeat the
argument here.

Proof.

For a surface
X
, let
\Gamma
be a multi-curve candidate to realising the
modified Cheeger constant
H(X)
. The topological type of
\Gamma
is entirely
determined by its number of components, denoted by
k
, and the genera
g_{1}

and
g_{2}
of the two connected components of
X\setminus\Gamma
. We pick the
numbering so that
g_{1}\leq g_{2}
, and note that by additivity of the Euler
characteristic, we always have
g_{1}+g_{2}+k=g+1
. For such a
g_{1}
and

k
, we fix one multi-curve
\Gamma_{g_{1},k}
on the base surface
S_{g}
of this
topology. Then,

(9.14)

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H(X)\leq a\right)\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}+g_{2}+k=g+1\\
0\leq g_{1}\leq g_{2}\\
g_{1}+k>1\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H_{g_{1},k}(X)\leq a\right),

where for any
g_{1},k
, the function
H_{g_{1},k}
is defined by

(9.15)

H_{g_{1},k}(X):=\frac{\min\left\{\ell_{X}(\Gamma)\,:\,\Gamma\in\mathrm{Orb}_{g}(\Gamma_{g_{1},k})\right\}}{2\pi(2g_{1}-2+k)}\cdot

By Markov’s inequality and Mirzakhani’s integration formula,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H_{g_{1},k}(X)\leq a\right)\leq\frac{1}{V_{g}}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{k}}V_{g_{1},k}(\mathbf{x})V_{g_{2},k}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathds{1}_{[0,L_{g_{1},k}(a)]}(x_{1}+\ldots+x_{k})\,\prod_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}

for
L_{g_{1},k}(a):=2\pi a(2g_{1}-2+k)
. This can be bounded using

Eq.

2.7
and the fact that

\forall L>0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{k}}\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}(x_{1}+\ldots+x_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\leq\frac{L^{2k}}{(2k)!}

and we obtain that

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H_{g_{1},k}(X)\leq a\right)\leq\frac{1}{V_{g}}\frac{L_{g_{1},k}(a)^{2k}e^{L_{g_{1},k}(a)}}{k!(2k)!}V_{g_{1},k}V_{g_{2},k}\cdot

In order to sum over all values of
k
, we now set

g_{i}^{\prime}=g_{i}+\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor
and

n=k-2\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor\in\{0,1\}
. We also assume that

2\pi a_{0}\leq 1
. By
[
27
, Lemma 3.2 (3)]
,
V_{g_{1},k}V_{g_{2},k}=\mathcal{O}\left(V_{g_{1}^{\prime},n}V_{g_{2}^{\prime},n}\right)
and hence

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H_{g_{1},k}(X)\leq a\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{a^{2}}{V_{g}}\frac{(2g_{1}^{\prime}-2+n)^{2k}}{k!(2k)!}\,e^{2\pi a(2g_{1}^{\prime}-2+n)}\,V_{g_{1}^{\prime},n}V_{g_{2}^{\prime},n}\right)

with a constant independent of
k
(note that we have kept a term
a^{2}
in this estimate,
granted by the fact that
k\geq 1
; this is the only difference with Mirzakhani’s proof). We
have that, for any
x
,

\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{x^{2k}}{k!(2k)!}=\mathcal{O}\left(\exp\left(\frac{3}{2}x^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)\right)

and hence,
Eq.

9.14
yields

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(H(X)\leq a\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{a^{2}}{V_{g}}\sum_{n=0}^{1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}^{\prime}\leq g_{2}^{\prime}\\
g^{\prime}_{1}+g^{\prime}_{2}=g+1-n\end{subarray}}e^{2\pi a(2g_{1}^{\prime}-2+n)+\frac{3}{2}(2g_{1}^{\prime}-2+n)^{\frac{2}{3}}}V_{g_{1}^{\prime},n}V_{g_{2}^{\prime},n}\right).

In
[
27
, Corollary 3.7]
, Mirzakhani proved that, provided that

4\pi a_{0}<2\log(2)
, the sum over all values of
g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{O}\left(V_{g}/g\right)

for
n=0
and
n=1
, which allows us to conclude.
∎

9.2.
Contradiction if
\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is Friedman–Ramanujan

Let us now prove that, if the function
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
was
Friedman–Ramanujan in the weak sense, then we would be able to prove spectral estimates that are
too good to be true.

Lemma 9.6
.

If
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is Friedman–Ramanujan in the weak sense, then for
any small enough
\delta>0
, any large enough
g
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{5}{72}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{\delta}\left(g^{-\frac{5}{4}}\right).

Proof.

Let us assume that
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function
in the weak sense, and denote as
d
its degree. We shall use the trace method developed in

Section

3.4
, with the parameters:

A=6,\quad\alpha=\frac{5}{12}<\frac{1}{2},\quad\epsilon=\frac{1}{144},\quad m=\max(d,0)+1.

We observe that
1/4-\alpha^{2}-\epsilon=5/72
and

(\alpha+\epsilon)A>\alpha A=5/2
, and therefore

3.14
implies that

(9.16)

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{5}{72}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{\delta}\left(g^{-\frac{5}{2}}\left\langle\ell e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\right\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}+g^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right)\cdot

We then use the density-writing of the average
\langle\cdot\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
, to write

(9.17)

\left\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\right\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\frac{\ell V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)}{V_{g}}\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell

because
\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}
is identically equal to zero outside of
[0,L]
. By our asymptotic expansion
result for the sum over all geodesics,
6.3
, at the second order (
K=1
),
using a fixed
\epsilon^{\prime}<1/24
,

(9.18)

\frac{\ell\,V_{g}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)}{V_{g}}\mathds{1}_{[0,L]}(\ell)=F_{g,1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)+\mathcal{O}^{w}\left(\frac{\ell\,e^{(1+\epsilon^{\prime})\ell}}{g^{2}}\right)=F_{g,1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)+\mathcal{O}^{w}\left(\frac{e^{\frac{25\ell}{24}}}{g^{2}}\right)

for

F_{g,1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell):=\ell f_{0}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)+g^{-1}\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
. When we inject (
9.18
) into
(
9.17
), we obtain

(9.19)

\left\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\right\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\int_{0}^{L}F_{g,1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell+\mathcal{O}\left(g^{\frac{5}{4}}\right)

because the remainder is

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}\|_{\infty}}{g^{2}}e^{\frac{25L}{24}-\frac{L}{2}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{\frac{13L}{24}}}{g^{2}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(g^{\frac{13}{4}-2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(g^{\frac{5}{4}}\right).

We know that
\ell f_{0}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)=4\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)
is a Friedman–Ramanujan
function, and therefore our hypothesis implies that
F_{g,1}^{\mathrm{all}}
is an element of

\mathcal{F}_{w}
. Its degree is at most
\max(d,0)
, its norm, exponent and constant can be bounded by a
uniform constant
c
. As a
consequence, by
3.16
, for
m=\max(d,0)+1
,

\int_{0}^{L}F_{g,1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell=\mathcal{O}\left(L^{c}\right).

Together with
Eq.

9.19
, this implies that

\left\langle\ell\,e^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}(\ell)\right\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}=\mathcal{O}\left(g^{\frac{5}{4}}\right)
. The conclusion then follows directly from

Eq.

9.16
.
∎

We conclude with the proof of
9.1
.

Proof.

Let us assume by contradiction that
\ell\mapsto\ell f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}(\ell)
is a
Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense. By
9.6
, for any small
enough
\delta>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{5}{72}\right)\leq C_{\delta}g^{-\frac{5}{4}}.

This is in particular also true if we replace
5/72
by
a:=\min(a_{0},5/72)
, where

a_{0}
is the constant from
9.2
. Applying this result yields

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)=\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)-\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}<\delta\right)\geq c_{1}\frac{a^{2}}{g}-c_{2}\frac{\delta}{g}

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
. We take
\delta
to be a fixed number smaller than

a^{2}c_{1}/(2c_{2})
. Then, we obtain that, for any large enough
g
,

c_{1}\frac{a^{2}}{2}\leq g\,\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq a\right)\leq C_{\delta}g^{-\frac{1}{4}}

which is a contradiction as
g\rightarrow+\infty
.
∎

10.
Typical surfaces have a spectral gap at least
\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon

We conclude this article by proving the following result, which improves the best known spectral gap
result for random compact hyperbolic surfaces of high genus sampled using the Weil–Petersson
probability measure.

Theorem 10.1
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon\right)\underset{g\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}1.

The main challenge in this proof is that we need to make a probabilistic
hypothesis in order to remove tangles, which requires to adapt the methods
developed in this article to more general averages that include an indicator
function. This work is done in Sections
10.1
to

10.3
. We then conclude to the proof of
10.1
in

Section

10.4
.

10.1.
The tangle-free hypothesis and its geometric implications

In order to overcome the difficulties exposed in
Section

9
, we need to make a
probabilistic hypothesis, which allows us to remove some “bad” surfaces containing many
geodesics. Those surfaces are the reason why the second-order term
f_{1}^{\mathrm{all}}
does not
satisfy
Objective (FR)
, and stand in the way to prove
10.1
. Let us introduce a set of
“good surfaces”.

Notation 10.2
.

Let
0<\kappa<1
. Let us consider the counting functions

\displaystyle N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}(X)

\displaystyle=\#\{\beta\in\mathcal{P}(X)\,:\,\ell_{X}(\beta)\leq\kappa\}

\displaystyle N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)

\displaystyle=\#\{Y\text{ embedded in }X\,:\,\chi_{Y}=-1,\ell_{X}^{\mathrm{max}}(\partial Y)\leq\kappa\log(g)\},

where:

•

any embedded subsurface
Y
is assumed to have geodesic boundary;

•

\ell_{X}^{\mathrm{max}}(\partial Y)
denotes the length of the longest boundary component of

Y
;

•

in
N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}
, amongst pair of pants, we only count those with three boundary components
forming a multi-curve, i.e. we exclude the possibility that two components are glued into a
once-holed torus (because this once-holed torus is already counted once in
N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}
).

We denote as
\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
the set of hyperbolic surfaces of genus
g
such
that
N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}=0
. We call
\kappa
-
short loops
the closed
geodesics counted by
N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}
, and
\kappa
-
tangles
the embedded
surfaces counted by
N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}
.

The same set of good surfaces appears in
[
21
]
, and making the probabilistic
assumption that
X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
is a crucial step of Lipnowski–Wright’s proof of the

3/16-\epsilon
spectral gap result. The probability of
\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
can be estimated
directly using
[
27
, Theorem 4.2]
and
[
32
, Theorem 5]
.

Lemma 10.3
.

For any small enough
\kappa>0
and any large enough
g
,

1-\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa^{2}+g^{\frac{3}{2}\kappa-1}\right).

We now prove that, under the probability assumption
X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
, the number of
geodesics of length
\leq L:=A\log(g)
filling any pair of pants or once-holed torus is radically
reduced, from being exponential in
L
to polynomial in
L
.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
0<\kappa<1
,
A\geq 1
. For any large enough
g
, there exists a set

\mathrm{Loc}_{g}^{\kappa,A}
of local topological types, of cardinal

(10.1)

\#\mathrm{Loc}_{g}^{\kappa,A}=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa,A}\left((\log g)^{c_{\kappa,A}}\right)

for a constant
c_{\kappa,A}>0
depending only on
\kappa
and
A
, such that for any

X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
, any primitive closed geodesic of length
\leq A\log g
filling a
surface of Euler characteristic
-1
is locally equivalent to a local type in

\mathrm{Loc}_{g}^{\kappa,A}
.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
, and let
\gamma
be a primitive closed geodesic on
X

of length
\leq A\log(g)
filling a pair of pants
P
. Let
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}

denote the three boundary components of
P
, ordered in non-decreasing length. By
definition of
\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
,

\ell_{X}(b_{1}),\ell_{X}(b_{2})\geq\kappa\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\ell_{X}(b_{3})\geq\kappa\log(g).

Let
\alpha
denote the simple orthogeodesic of
P
from
b_{3}
to itself. We fix a base point

x_{0}
on
\alpha
, and
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
two simple paths based at
x_{0}
, rotating around
b_{1}
and

b_{2}
respectively, so that
\pi_{1}(P)
is the free group generated by
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
(see

Fig.

21
).

Figure 21
.
Illustration of the proof of
10.4
for pair of
pants.

The geodesic
\gamma
fills
P
, and is in particular not simple. As a consequence, it must
intersect
\alpha
(because
P\setminus\alpha
is a union of two cylinders). All of its
intersections are transversal, because
\gamma
cannot coincide with
\alpha
on an interval. The
number of these intersections must be even because
\alpha
separates
P
into two connected
components. We can therefore write
\gamma
as the concatenation

\gamma_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\gamma_{2}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\gamma_{2k}
, where for every

i
,
\gamma_{i}
is a geodesic path intersecting
\alpha
exactly at its endpoints, lying entirely
on the connected component of
P\setminus\alpha
containing
b_{\bar{\imath}}
for

\bar{\imath}\in\{1,2\}
of the same parity as
i
.

The path
\alpha
cuts
b_{3}
into two geodesic arcs, of respective lengths
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
. For
all
i
, the geodesic path
\gamma_{i}
is a path from
\alpha
to itself on the connected
component
C_{\bar{\imath}}
of
P
containing
b_{\bar{\imath}}
, that cannot be homotoped to a
sub-path of
\alpha
(by minimality of the length of
\gamma
in its homotopy class). As a
consequence, its length is greater than the length
t_{\bar{\imath}}
of the path minimising this
property. We deduce that

A\log(g)\geq\ell_{X}(\gamma)=\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\ell(\gamma_{i})\geq k(t_{1}+t_{2})\geq k\,\kappa\log(g),

which implies that
k\leq A/\kappa
.

For all
i
,
\gamma_{i}
is homotopic with endpoints gliding along
\alpha
to

a_{\bar{\imath}}^{m_{i}}
for an integer
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. The component
C_{\bar{\imath}}
is
isometric to part of a hyperbolic cylinder of core of length
\geq\kappa
. Projecting the
geodesic path
\gamma_{i}
on the core
b_{\bar{\imath}}
following Fermi coordinates decreases its
length, and in doing so we obtain a path on
b_{\bar{\imath}}
rotating at least
|m_{i}|-1

times around it. It follows that

A\log(g)\geq\ell(\gamma_{i})\geq(|m_{i}|-1)\kappa,

which implies that

|m_{i}|\leq M:=1+\lceil\frac{A}{\kappa}\log g\rceil
.

To conclude, we have proven that
\gamma
is freely homotopic to

a_{1}^{m_{1}}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}a_{2}^{m_{2}}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}a_{1}^{m_{2k-1}}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}a_{2}^{m_{2k}}
for
k:=\lceil A/\kappa\rceil
and a family of integers
(m_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}

such that
|m_{i}|\leq M
for all
i
. We put all of those local topologies in the set

\mathrm{Loc}_{g}^{\kappa,A}
, and note that the number of such configurations is bounded by

(2M+1)^{2k}=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa,A}\left((\log g)^{\lceil 2A/\kappa\rceil}\right).

The proof in the case of a once-holed torus is similar.
∎

10.2.
Inclusion-exclusion

In order to be able to “make the assumption that

X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
”, we need to be able to compute expectations of
functions in which the indicator function

\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}=\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}\times\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}=0}
appears. A good
way to achieve this is to write this indicator function in terms of counting
functions, using an inclusion-exclusion (such an argument also appears in
Friedman’s proof of Alon’s conjecture
[
10
]
). The hope is that
these counting functions can then be expressed in terms of geometric functions
and integrated using Mirzakhani’s integration formula.

The indicator function
\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}
can be rewritten in the following way, as
done by Mirzakhani
[
27
]
and
Lipnowski–Wright
[
21
]
.

Notation 10.5
.

For
j\geq 1
, we define

N_{\kappa,j}^{\mathrm{inj}}(X)=\#\{\text{unordered families }\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{j}\}\text{ of distinct }\kappa\text{-short-loops on }X\}.

Lemma 10.6
.

For any small enough
\kappa>0
, for any
j\geq 1
, all families of geodesics
counted by
N_{\kappa,j}^{\mathrm{inj}}
are made of simple and disjoint geodesics, and

(10.2)

\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}=1-\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}(-1)^{j+1}N_{\kappa,j}^{\mathrm{inj}}=\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}(-1)^{j}N_{\kappa,j}^{\mathrm{inj}}

with the convention that
N_{\kappa,0}^{\mathrm{inj}}=1
.

Proof.

We simply observe that, by the binomial theorem,

\forall n\geq 0,\quad\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}\binom{n}{j}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }n=0\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The fact that the geodesics are simple and disjoint for small enough
\kappa

is a classic application of the Collar Lemma
[
7
, Theorem 4.1.6]
.
∎

However, there is no reason to believe that all embedded surfaces contributing to
N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}
are
disjoint. For this reason, it is a priori better to treat the indicator function of
N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}=0

differently, to avoid a tedious enumeration of cases. Luckily, the following easy
inclusion-exclusion is enough for proving
10.1
.

Notation 10.7
.

We define

N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}:=\#\{\text{unordered pairs }\{Y,Y^{\prime}\}\text{ of distinct }\kappa\text{-tangles on }X\}.

Lemma 10.8
.

For any
\kappa>0
and
g\geq 2
,

\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}=0}=1-N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}+\mathcal{O}\left(N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}\right).

Proof.

The bound is a direct consequence of the inequality

0\leq N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}-\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}\geq 1}\leq N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}
, which is trivially true
when
X
contains
0
or
1
tangles, and otherwise true because

\forall n\geq 2,\quad 0\leq n-1\leq\frac{n(n-1)}{2}=\binom{n}{2}.

∎

10.3.
Expression of the terms arising in the inclusion-exclusion

The aim of this section is to express sums of the form

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}(X)\Bigg],

where the sum runs over all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
or
\gamma\in T^{g}
for a local type
T
,
under a manageable form, using the formulas for
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}
from

Section

10.2
.

We proceed in three steps, presented in the following three paragraphs:

•

first, we prove an upper bound in all expectations involving
N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}
, which will allow us
to neglect such terms in the proof of
10.1
;

•

then, we estimate

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\sum_{\gamma\in T^{g}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}(X)\right]
, up to
reasonable errors, for the local type “simple” and any local type filling a
pair of pants;

•

finally, we estimate

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\sum_{\gamma\in T^{g}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}(X)N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)\right]
, up
to reasonable errors, for the same local types as above.

This will be enough because

(10.3)

\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}=\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}-\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}+\mathcal{O}\left(N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}\right).

10.3.1.
Expectation against
N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}

The following lemma will allow us to discard any expectation in which
N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}
appears when
proving the
2/9-\epsilon
spectral gap result. This is the reason why the writing in

10.8
is enough for our purposes. We expect a more careful inclusion-exclusion to
be necessary to reach the optimal spectral gap
1/4-\epsilon
.

Lemma 10.9
.

For any
0<\kappa<1
, any large enough
g
, any admissible function
F
, if
L:=6\log(g)
,
then

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)\Bigg]=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(\frac{\|F(\ell)\exp(\ell)\|_{\infty}}{g^{2-19\kappa}}\right).

Proof.

First, we notice that, using the triangle inequality, we can consider the case
F(x)=e^{-x}

only. Let
\gamma
be a closed geodesic on
X
of length
\leq L
, and
Y
,
Y^{\prime}
be two distinct
tangles on
X
. Let
S:=S_{\gamma}
be the surface filled by
\gamma
. We define
S^{\prime}
to be the
surface obtained by adding any disk in the complement of the union
S\cup Y\cup Y^{\prime}
. The surface

S^{\prime}
is not necessarily connected, and has at most three connected components. Because the
boundary of
S^{\prime}
can be obtained from the boundary of
S
together with
\partial Y
and

\partial Y^{\prime}
, we have

(10.4)

\ell_{X}(\partial S^{\prime})\leq\ell_{X}(\partial S)+6\kappa\log(g)\leq 2\ell_{X}(\gamma)+6\log(g)\leq 18\log(g)=3L.

We observe that the surface
S^{\prime}
contains the surface
Y
of Euler
characteristic
-1
. Furthermore,
S^{\prime}\neq Y
, because
S^{\prime}
contains
Y^{\prime}
and

Y\neq Y^{\prime}
. Since
S^{\prime}\setminus Y
cannot be reduced to one cylinder (by
definition of the notion of tangle),
r:=-\chi_{S^{\prime}}\geq 2
.

As in the proof of
6.3
, we use the generalised
tangle-free hypothesis developed in
Section

6.3
to bound
the number of possibilities for the Euler characteristic
-r
. Indeed, for an
integer
N\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{B}_{g}^{(N)}\subset\mathcal{M}_{g}
denote
the set of
(N,\log(g))
-tangle-free surfaces. Then, we have the following bounds.

•

By
6.7
,
1-\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\mathcal{B}_{g}^{(N)}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{N}\left(1/g^{N/2-1}\right)
.

•

By the naive geodesic-counting result,
2.1
, for any

X\in\mathcal{M}_{g}
,

–

N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)=\mathcal{O}\left(g^{6(\kappa+1)}\right)
because the pair of tangle is determined by at most
6

geodesics of lengths
\leq\kappa\log(g)
;

–

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma:\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}=\mathcal{O}\left(Lg\right)
.

As a consequence, taking
N:=34
, for any
\kappa<1
,

(10.5)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\,N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)(1-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{g}^{(N)}}(X))\Bigg]=\mathcal{O}_{N}\left(\frac{Lg^{7+6\kappa}}{g^{N/2-1}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{g^{2}}\right).

We now apply
6.12
to obtain that, as soon as

\kappa<1/16
, there exists a universal constant
M
such that, for any

\gamma,Y,Y^{\prime}
with a non-zero contribution to the sum

(10.6)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\,N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{g}^{(N)}}(X)\Bigg],

the Euler characteristic of the connected component of
S^{\prime}
containing
\gamma
is smaller
than
M
in absolute value. Because the other connected components of
S^{\prime}
are tangles, of Euler
characteristic
-1
, this implies that
r\leq M+2
. We can therefore reduce the sum
(
10.6
) to only contributions such that
2\leq r\leq M+2
. Then, by
positivity, we can remove the indicator function
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{g}^{(N)}}\leq 1
, and hence obtain

(10.7)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\,N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{g}^{(N)}}\Bigg]\leq\sum_{r=2}^{M+2}\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\gamma,Y,Y^{\prime})\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\\
\chi_{S^{\prime}}=-r\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\Bigg]

where the sum runs over all families
(\gamma,Y,Y^{\prime})
such that
\gamma
is a primitive closed
geodesic on
X
of length
\leq 6\log(g)
,
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are disjoint tangles on
X
, and

\chi_{S^{\prime}}=-r
.

Let us fix a filling type
S^{\prime}
of Euler characteristic
2\leq r\leq M+2
,
with at most three connected components, and a connected subsurface

S\subset S^{\prime}
(we allow that
S
is a connected component of
S^{\prime}
, or shares
some of its boundary components). By (
10.4
), the quantity we
need to bound is smaller than

(10.8)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Z,Z^{\prime})\text{ on }S_{g}\\
\text{ homeo to }(S,S^{\prime})\\
\ell_{X}(\partial Z),\ell_{X}(\partial Z^{\prime})\leq 3L\end{subarray}}N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}(Z^{\prime})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ filling }Z\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}\mathds{1}_{\ell_{X}(\partial Z^{\prime})\leq\ell_{X}(\partial Z)+6\kappa\log(g)}\Bigg].

We once again use
2.1
to bound the number of pairs of tangles, and
obtain that for any
Z^{\prime}
as above,

N_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{tang}}(Z^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}\left((re^{\kappa\log(g)})^{6}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(g^{6\kappa}\right)
. We use

2.3
with
\eta=\kappa
to bound the number of possibilities for the loop

\gamma
filling
Z
, and obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ filling }Z\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}e^{-\ell_{X}(\gamma)}=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(Le^{-\frac{1-\kappa}{2}\ell_{X}(\partial Z)}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(g^{12\kappa}\exp\left(-\frac{\ell_{X}(\partial Z^{\prime})}{2}\right)\right)

because, by the hypotheses on the lengths of
\partial Z
and
\partial Z^{\prime}
in
(
10.8
),

\frac{1-\kappa}{2}\ell_{X}(\partial Z)\geq\frac{1-\kappa}{2}\ell_{X}(\partial Z^{\prime})-3\kappa(1-\kappa)\log(g)>\frac{\ell_{X}(\partial Z^{\prime})}{2}-12\kappa\log(g).

Therefore,

\eqref{eq:proof_Ntang_2_enumeration}=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\Bigg(g^{18\kappa}\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Z,Z^{\prime})\text{ on }S_{g}\\
\text{ homeo to
}(S^{\prime}S^{\prime})\\
\ell_{X}(\partial Z),\ell_{X}(\partial Z^{\prime})\leq 3L\end{subarray}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ell_{X}(\partial Z^{\prime})}{2}\right)\Bigg]\Bigg).

We use Mirzakhani’s integration formula to compute this expectation, and
obtain that we need to bound

(10.9)

g^{18\kappa}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1},\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}\leq 3L\end{subarray}}\phi_{g}^{S^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\frac{V_{S}(\mathbf{y})V_{S^{\prime}\setminus S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{V_{S^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})}\exp\left(-\frac{\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

where

•

\mathbf{x}
denotes the length-vector of
\partial Z^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y}
that of
\partial Z
;

•

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1}
is the
\ell^{1}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
for
p\geq 1
;

•

\phi_{g}^{S^{\prime}}
is sum over all realisations of
S^{\prime}
in
S_{g}
as introduced in
(
5.4
) for connected filling types, and generalised in

Section

5.5
to more general filling types;

•

V_{S}(\mathbf{y})
,
V_{S^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})
and
V_{S^{\prime}\setminus S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
respectively stand for the product
of the Weil–Petersson volumes of the components of
S
,
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}\setminus S
.

We bound the Weil–Petersson volumes
V_{S}(\mathbf{y})
and
V_{S^{\prime}\setminus S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
by the polynomial
bound (
2.6
), and note that their values for
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
and

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{0}
are
\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
because
r\leq M+2
. We naturally extend the bound on
\phi_{g}^{S^{\prime}}

from
5.21
with
K=r=|\chi_{S^{\prime}}|
to the case of disconnected
filling types. Altogether, this allows us to deduce that (
10.9
) is

\mathcal{O}\left(g^{18\kappa-r}(3L)^{c(r)}\right)
for a constant
c(r)
depending only on
r
.

Summing these inequalities for
2\leq r\leq M+2
, we obtain that (
10.6
) is

\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(g^{19\kappa-2}\right)
, which together with (
10.5
) leads to
our claim.
∎

10.3.2.
Expectation against
\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}

For a local topological type
T
,
g\geq 2
, an admissible function
F
and a set

\mathcal{A}_{g}\subset\mathcal{M}_{g}
, we denote

\langle F\,|\,X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}\rangle_{g}^{{T}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\in T^{g}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}}(X)\Bigg]

and we naturally define
\langle F\,|\,X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}
the same way. The aim of this section
is to provide expressions for these averages for

\mathcal{A}_{g}=\{X\in\mathcal{M}_{g}\,:\,N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}(X)=0\}
, in particular when
T
is the local
type “simple” or a local type filling a pair of pants. This requires some of the notations
introduced in
Section

5.5
, generalising the notion of local topological type and
average over a local type to situations where the surface is not connected, or filled by several
loops. We prove the following.

Lemma 10.10
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that, for any small enough
\kappa>0
, any large enough
g
,

L:=6\log(g)
, any admissible function
F
of support in
[0,L]
,

(1)

for the local topological type “simple”,

(10.10)

\begin{split}\langle F\,|\,N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}=&\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\Bigg(\langle F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}^{j+1}}}-\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s}}}\langle(F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}),\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j}\rangle_{g}^{{T\times\mathrm{s}^{j}}}\Bigg)\\
&+\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(\|F\|_{\infty}+\frac{\|F(\ell)\,e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}}{g^{2-c\kappa}}\right)\end{split}

where for any
j\geq 0
,

•

\mathrm{s}^{j}=(\mathrm{s},\ldots,\mathrm{s})
is the local topology with
j
copies
of the type “simple”;

•

\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s}}
is the set of local topological types of pairs of
simple loops filling
S_{1,1}
.

(2)

for any local type
T
filling a pair of pants,

(10.11)

\langle F\,|\,N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\langle F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j}\rangle_{g}^{{T\times\mathrm{s}^{j}}}+\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(\|F\|_{\infty}+\frac{\|F(\ell)\,e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}}{g^{2-c\kappa}}\right).

Proof.

First, we observe that by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left|\langle F\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}\,|\,N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{all}}}\right|\leq\|F\|_{\infty}\,\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left[\#\{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}\,:\,\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq\kappa\}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa^{2}\|F\|_{\infty}\right)

by
[
27
, Section 4.2]
. We can therefore restrict both sums we wish to study to
geodesics
\gamma
of length
>\kappa
. We then apply
10.6
, which tells us
that for small enough
\kappa
, all geodesics of length
\leq\kappa
are disjoint, and that we
can write

\langle F\mathds{1}_{(\kappa,L]}\,|\,N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}}}=\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}(-1)^{j}\,\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ simple}\\
\kappa<\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,N_{\kappa,j}^{\mathrm{inj}}(X)\Bigg].

For any
j\geq 0
, we pick a numbering of the
j
-tuples counted in

N_{\kappa,j}^{\mathrm{inj}}
, which allows us to rewrite the sum above as

(10.12)

\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\,\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ simple}\\
\kappa<\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{j})\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\prod_{i=1}^{j}\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}(\ell_{X}(\beta_{i}))\Bigg].

We notice that the hypothesis
\ell_{X}(\gamma)>\kappa
implies that
\gamma
is distinct from

\beta_{i}^{\pm 1}
for all
i
. As in the proof of
10.9
, we regroup the
terms of this sum according to the topology of the surface
S^{\prime}
filled by the multi-loop

(\gamma,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{j})
. This allows us to prove, using the same strategy, that there
exists a constant
c>0
such that the restriction of
Eq.

10.12
to multi-loops such
that
|\chi_{S^{\prime}}|\geq 2
is
\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(\|F(\ell)\,e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}/g^{2-c\kappa}\right)
.
Hence, we only need to enumerate local topologies for which
|\chi_{S^{\prime}}|\leq 1
.

The only local topology for which
\chi_{S^{\prime}}=0
is the product
\mathrm{s}^{j+1}
of
j+1
copies of
the local type “simple”. By definition of the average
\langle\cdot\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}^{j+1}}}
, these
contributions can be rewritten as

\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\langle F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]},\ldots,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}^{j+1}}}.

This is the first term on the right hand side of
Eq.

10.10
.

Now, we carefully enumerate the local topologies for which
\chi_{S^{\prime}}=-1
. We recall that, by
hypothesis on
\kappa
, for any multi-loop
(\gamma,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{j})
contributing to
the sum, all the
\beta_{j}
are simple and disjoint. Furthermore,
\gamma
is simple, and distinct
from any
\beta_{i}^{\pm 1}
(but not necessarily disjoint). The only way to obtain a local type of
Euler characteristic
-1
then is to take a type
T\times\mathrm{s}^{j-k}
, where

T=[S_{1,1},(\gamma,\beta_{i_{1}},\ldots,\beta_{i_{k}})]_{\mathrm{loc}}
or

[S_{0,3},(\gamma,\beta_{i_{1}},\ldots,\beta_{i_{k}})]_{\mathrm{loc}}
for a
k\leq j
and a multi-loop

(\gamma,\beta_{i_{1}},\ldots,\beta_{i_{k}})
filling
S_{1,1}
or
S_{0,3}
.

•

Any simple loop on the pair of pants
S_{0,3}
is homotopic to one of its boundary
components. It is therefore impossible to fill a pair of pants with a multi-loop containing only
simple loops, so the case
S_{0,3}
cannot happen.

•

If
T=[S_{1,1},(\gamma,\beta_{i_{1}},\ldots,\beta_{i_{k}})]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, in order for the
multi-loop to fill the once-holed torus, we need
k\geq 1
, because
\gamma
is simple. But a
once-holed torus cannot contain two disjoint essential curves, so
k=1
. After the change of
variables
j\rightarrow j-1
, counting the number of possibilities for the choice of the index

i_{1}
, this yields the second term on the right hand side of
Eq.

10.10
.

This allows us to conclude. The proof in the case of a local topology
T
filling a pair of pants

P
is the same, with less cases because the only thing we can add to
P
to form a
S^{\prime}
of Euler
characteristic
-1
is cylinders.
∎

10.3.3.
Expectation against
\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}}\times N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}

Let us conclude this section with the last cases that need to be described to compute averages of
the form
\langle F\,|\,X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\rangle_{g}^{{T}}
, in light of
Eq.

10.3
.

Lemma 10.11
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that, for any small enough
\kappa>0
,
any large enough
g
,
L:=6\log(g)
, any admissible function
F
of support in
[0,L]
,

(10.13)

\begin{split}&\quad\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\text{ simple}\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}(X)\,N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)\Bigg]\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\Bigg(\langle F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j},\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}^{3}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}^{j+1}\times T_{0,3}^{b}}}+\langle F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j},\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}\rangle_{g}^{{\mathrm{s}^{j+1}\times T_{1,1}^{b}}}\Bigg)\\
&+\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\Bigg(\langle(F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}),\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j}\rangle_{g}^{{T_{1,1}^{1,b}\times\mathrm{s}^{j}}}-\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s},b}}\langle(F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]},\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}),\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j}\rangle_{g}^{{T\times\mathrm{s}^{j}}}\Bigg)\\
&+\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(\|F\|_{\infty}+\frac{\|F(\ell)e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}}{g^{2-c\kappa}}\right)\end{split}

and for any local topology
T
filling a pair of pants,

(10.14)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in T^{g}\\
\ell_{X}(\gamma)\leq L\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\,\mathds{1}_{N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{inj}}=0}(X)\,N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}(X)\Bigg]\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\langle(F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}^{3}),\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j}\rangle_{g}^{{T^{b}\times\mathrm{s}^{j}}}+\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(\|F\|_{\infty}+\frac{\|F(\ell)e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}}{g^{2-c\kappa}}\right)\end{split}

where

•

T_{0,3}^{b}
and
T_{1,1}^{b}
are the local types of respective filling types
S
for

S=S_{0,3}
and
S_{1,1}
, with
\gamma=\partial S
(and, in the average for
S=S_{0,3}
,
excluding the possibility of gluing two distinct boundary components of
S_{0,3}
together);

•

T_{1,1}^{1,b}
is the local type

[S_{1,1},(\beta,\partial S_{1,1})]_{\mathrm{loc}}
where
\beta
is the essential
simple closed loop on the once-holed torus
S_{1,1}
;

•

\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s},b}
is the set of local types

[S_{1,1},(\gamma,\beta,\partial S_{1,1})]_{\mathrm{loc}}
where
(\gamma,\beta)

are two simple loops on
S_{1,1}
;

•

if
T=[S_{0,3},\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, then
T^{b}
is the local topology

[S_{0,3},(\gamma,\partial S_{0,3})]_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Proof.

The proof runs along the same lines as the proof of
10.10
, with the difference
that we now enumerate possibilities for the loop
\gamma
, the multi-curve

(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{j})
and the tangle
S_{0,3}
or
S_{1,1}
. Note that possibilities are
quite limited, because the Euler characteristic of the tangle is
-1
and we can exclude all
situations for which the total Euler characteristic is
<-1
. For instance, in the case of
(
10.14
), the only term corresponds to the case where the tangle is

S_{\gamma}
.
∎

10.3.4.
Writing of these expectations as Friedman–Ramanujan functions

We are now able to prove the following proposition. This will allow us to apply the properties of
Friedman–Ramanujan functions, and in particular the cancellation argument presented in

Section

3.4
, in the context of the inclusion-exclusion.

Proposition 10.12
.

There exists a constant
c>0
satisfying the following. For any small enough
\kappa>0
, any
large enough
g
, any local topology that
T
is “simple” or fills a pair of pants, there exists
a density function
A_{T,g}^{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that, for any admissible
function
F
of support included in
[0,L]
with
L:=6\log(g)
,

\displaystyle\langle F\,|\,X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\rangle_{g}^{{T}}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\,A_{T,g}^{\kappa}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell+\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(\frac{\|F(\ell)\,e^{\ell}\|_{\infty}}{g^{2-c\kappa}}+g^{c\kappa}\|F(\ell)e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\|_{\infty}\right).

Furthermore, the function
\ell\mapsto\ell A_{T,g}^{\kappa}(\ell)
is Friedman–Ramanujan in the
weak sense, of degree and exponent
\leq c
, and norm and constant
\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(g^{c\kappa}\right)
.

We will use the following lemma to prove
10.12
.

Lemma 10.13
.

For any integer
j\geq 0
, any large enough
g
,

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{i}x_{i}\,V_{g-2-j,2j+3}(\mathbf{x})}{V_{g}}

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{g-2-j,2j+3}}{V_{g}}\prod_{i}2\sinh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1}{g^{2}}\prod_{i}x_{i}e^{\frac{x_{i}}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{i}x_{i}\,V_{g-1-j,2j+1}(\mathbf{x})}{V_{g}}

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{g-1-j,2j+1}}{V_{g}}\prod_{i}2\sinh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1}{g^{2}}\prod_{i}x_{i}e^{\frac{x_{i}}{2}}\right)

and the ratios
V_{g-2-j,2j+3}/V_{g}
and
V_{g-1-j,2j+1}/V_{g}
are
\mathcal{O}\left(1/g\right)
. Furthermore,

\frac{\prod_{i}x_{i}\,V_{g-j,2j}(\mathbf{x})}{V_{g}}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}}c^{(g,j)}_{\mathbf{m}}\prod_{i}f_{m_{i}}(x_{i})+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{(j+1)^{4}(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1)^{3}}{g^{2}}\prod_{i}x_{i}e^{\frac{x_{i}}{2}}\right)

where the sum runs over all families
(m_{i})_{i}
of integers in
\{1,\ldots,4\}
, the
coefficients
c^{(g,j)}_{\mathbf{m}}
are bounded uniformly in
g
and
j
, and

(10.15)

f_{1}(x)=2\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\quad f_{2}(x)=2x^{2}\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\quad f_{3}(x)=x\cosh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\quad f_{4}(x)=x.

Proof.

The proof is immediate for
V_{g-2-j,2j+3}(\mathbf{x})
and
V_{g-1-j,2j+1}(\mathbf{x})
, as it is a simple
consequence of the first-order expansion (
2.8
) together with Weil–Petersson
volume comparisons from
[
27
]
. Indeed, by
[
27
, Lemma 3.2]
, there
exists a constant
c
such that, for any
g,n,j
,
V_{g-j,n+2j}\leq cV_{g,n}
. Therefore,

V_{g-2-j,2j+3}/V_{g}=\mathcal{O}\left(V_{g-2,3}/V_{g}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(1/g\right)
, and same goes for
V_{g-1-j,2j+1}
.

However, for
V_{g-j,2j}(\mathbf{x})
, we need to use the explicit second-order expansion from

[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
, stating that for
g,n\geq 1
,

(10.16)

\begin{split}&\prod_{i}x_{i}V_{g,n}(\mathbf{x})\\
&=8\;V_{g-1,n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[f_{3}(x_{i})+f_{4}(x_{i})-\frac{f_{2}(x_{i})}{16}-2f_{1}(x_{i})\right]\prod_{k\neq i}f_{1}(x_{k})\\
&-4V_{g,n-1}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left[f_{3}(x_{i})f_{3}(x_{j})+f_{4}(x_{i})f_{4}(x_{j})-2\,f_{1}(x_{i})f_{1}(x_{j})\right]\prod_{k\notin\{i,j\}}f_{1}(x_{k})\\
&+V_{g,n}\prod_{1\leq k\leq n}f_{1}(x_{k})+\mathcal{O}_{n}\left(V_{g,n}\frac{(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1)^{3}\prod_{i}x_{i}}{(g+1)^{2}}\;\exp\left(\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}}{2}\right)\right)\end{split}

with the notations of (
10.15
). As above, we use
[
27
, Lemma
3.2]
to compare all coefficients appearing in the expansion of

\prod_{i}x_{i}V_{g-j,2j}(\mathbf{x})/V_{g}
to the ones for which
j=0
or
1
, and hence obtain a uniform
bound on the various volume ratios.

We further check the dependency of the implied constant that appears when applying
(
10.16
) in terms of
j
, following the proof of
[
2
, Theorem
3.1]
, and using the method above to bring any volume appearing down to the case

j=0
or
1
. More precisely, with the numbering of
[
2
]
: in each step of this
second-order expansion, we gain exactly two powers of
j+1
. Indeed, during the first order, we
gain one power when summing the different terms in equation (22), and another using Lemma 2.9. The
same two steps in the second-order expansion yield two other terms
j+1
, which altogether lead
to the term
(j+1)^{4}
.
∎

Let us now prove
10.12
.

Proof.

Let
T=[P,\gamma]_{\mathrm{loc}}
for a loop
\gamma
filling the pair of pants
P
.
For
j\geq 0
, by
5.5
, the average

(-1)^{j}\langle F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}^{j}\rangle_{g}^{{T\times\mathrm{s}^{j}}}/j!
present in
(
10.11
) can be written

\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}}\int_{[0,\kappa]^{j}}F(\mathfrak{L}_{\gamma}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}))\,\phi_{g}^{S_{0,3}\times S_{0,2}^{j}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

The filling type
S_{0,3}\times S_{0,2}^{j}
has Euler characteristic
-1
, so when we replace the
realisation function above by its main term, we obtain an approximation of

\langle F,\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}\rangle_{g}^{{T\times\mathrm{s}^{j}}}
at precision
1/g^{2}
. First, we compute the term
of the connected realisation, which is the case when we glue a surface of signature
(g-2-j,2j+3)

to the pair of pants
S_{0,3}
and the
j
copies of the cylinder
S_{0,2}
. This yields a density
function

\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\frac{1}{m(T)}\int_{\star}\int_{[0,\kappa]^{j}}\frac{V_{g-2-j,2j+3}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},x_{1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{j},x_{j})}{V_{g}}\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{j}x_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}

where the integral
\int_{\star}
runs on the level-set

\{\mathfrak{L}_{\gamma}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell\}
. By
10.13
, we only
need to compute

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\frac{1}{m(T)}\frac{V_{g-2-j,2j+3}}{V_{g}}\int_{\star}\int_{[0,\kappa]^{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}2\sinh\left(\frac{\ell_{i}}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{4}{x_{i}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{j}I(\kappa)^{j}}{j!}\frac{1}{m(T)}\frac{V_{g-2-j,2j+3}}{V_{g}}\int_{\star}\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{1}(\ell_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where
I(\kappa):=\int_{0}^{\kappa}4\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x/x=\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa\right)
for
\kappa\leq 1
. We
sum the terms for the different values of
j
to obtain the corresponding density (the sum
converges because
V_{g-2-j,2j+3}/V_{g}=\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
by
10.13
). We can similarly
write the terms arising from the other realisations of minimal rank, which are obtained by adding
some Dirac distributions. This yields additional terms, multiple of

\iint_{\{\mathfrak{L}_{\gamma}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell\}}f_{1}(\ell_{i})\,\ell_{j}\delta(\ell_{j}-\ell_{k})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\quad\text{for }\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}

but also, when we glue one or two cylinders from the types
\mathrm{s}^{j}
to
the pair of pants
P
,

\int_{\{\mathfrak{L}_{\gamma}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=\ell\}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}f_{1}(\ell_{i})\prod_{i\in I}\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}(\ell_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{3}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\quad\text{for }I\subset\{1,2,3\},

or even a mixture of both, for instance if we glue two components of
P
to the same cylinder of

\mathrm{s}^{j}
. We notice that
f_{1}
and
f_{1}\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}
both satisfy

(10.17)

\frac{f(\ell)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}=p(\ell)+\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(g^{c\kappa}(\ell+1)^{k}e^{-\ell/2}\right)

for the polynomial
p=1
or
0
respectively, and the exponents
k=c=0
, because

\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(e^{-\ell/2}\right)
. Then, the results of Sections

7
and
8
applied to the local type
T

allow us to conclude that these densities are indeed Friedman–Ramanujan in the weak sense. The
bound on the degree and exponent follow from the fact that we systematically integrate only
functions that are products of
f_{1}
and
f_{1}\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}
, i.e. there are
\leq 2^{3}
possible
products, and the uniformity in
8.1
with respect to the local type. The bounds
throughout the computation above further allow us to bound the constant and remainder of the
density by
\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(1\right)
.

The computation is the same for the average
(
10.14
) containing the counting function

N_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{tang}}
, with the difference that now, we sometimes multiply by the indicator
function
\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}(\ell)=\mathcal{O}\left(g^{\kappa/2}e^{-\ell/2}\right)
coming
from the tangles, and hence there is
\leq 3^{3}
possible products. The degree
and exponent is therefore still
\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
, but the bound on the norm and
remainder is now
\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(g^{3\kappa/2}\right)
, which is is enough to conclude
in the case of a type
T
filling a pair of pants.

Let us now consider the local topology “simple”. Similarly, we examine each term
in (
10.10
) and (
10.13
) one after the other. For
all the terms except the terms containing the summations over
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s}}

and
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s},b}
, this allows us to express the product of the density and
the function
\ell\mapsto\ell
as a linear combination of functions

\ell\mapsto f^{(\kappa)}_{m_{1}}(\ell)f^{(\kappa)}_{m_{2}}(\ell)
with bounded coefficients, where
the family of functions
(f_{i}^{(\kappa)})_{1\leq i\leq 15}
consists of all products of a
function of
Eq.

10.15
or
f_{5}(x):=x^{3}
(coming from the expression of the volume

xV_{1,1}(x)
) with
1
,
\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}
and
\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}
. We check all of these
functions satisfy the property (
10.17
), which is enough to conclude for these
terms.

Now, the last cases we need to consider are the cases where we sum over local topological types

T
in
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s},b}
, which are
different because they correspond to situations where the geodesic
\gamma
has an intersection
with one of the
\kappa
-short loops. Let us first look at this term in (
10.10
).

As a first step, we compute the density appearing for the local topology

[S_{1,1},(\gamma,\beta)]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, where
(\gamma,\beta)
is a pair of simple loops on
S_{1,1}
that
intersect exactly once. We pick Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates
(\ell,\theta)
on the once-holed
torus associated to the geodesic
\gamma
, with the origin of twist taken for
\beta
. As a
consequence, the density we wish to compute can be expressed as:

\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{xx_{1}\ldots x_{j}V_{g-1-j,1+2j}(x,x_{1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{j},x_{j})}{V_{g}}\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}(\ell_{\ell,\theta,x}(\beta))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\theta

where
\ell_{\ell,\theta,x}(\beta)
denotes the length of
\beta
in the once-holed torus of
boundary length
x
with Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates
(\ell,\theta)
. By

10.13
, we need to compute

\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}I(\kappa)^{j}}{j!}\frac{V_{g-1-j,1+2j}}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}(\ell_{\ell,\theta,x}(\beta))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

It is therefore enough to prove that the integral

J_{\kappa}(\ell):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa]}(\ell_{\ell,\theta,x}(\beta))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\theta

is a Friedman–Ramanujan function.

Let us equip
S_{1,1}
with the metric associated with the parameters
(x,\ell,\theta)
. We
denote as
p
the length of the orthogeodesic linking the two boundaries of length
\ell
on the
pair of pants
S_{1,1}\setminus\gamma
, and
r
the length of
\beta
for this metric. Then,

\begin{cases}\cosh\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)=\cosh\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)/\cosh\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\\
\cosh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)=\cosh(p)\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)-\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right).\end{cases}

We perform the successive changes of variables

(x,\theta)\rightarrow(r,p)\rightarrow(R,P)
for
R:=\cosh\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)
and

P:=\cosh\left(\frac{P}{2}\right)
, which yield

J_{\kappa}(\ell)=16\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\iint_{\frac{1}{\tanh\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)}<P<R<\cosh\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}R\,\mathrm{d}P}{\sqrt{(R/P)^{2}-1}}.

We then check that the integral above converges to a finite limit as
\ell\rightarrow+\infty
,
and that the difference between the integral and its limit is
\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(e^{-\ell/2}\right)
. This
implies that
J_{\kappa}
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function.

Same goes when we examine the term
[S_{1,1},(\gamma,\beta,\partial S_{1,1})]_{\mathrm{loc}}
in
(
10.13
). Indeed, the expression is the same, except there is an
additional indicator function
\mathds{1}_{[0,\kappa\log(g)]}(x)
. We note that

\theta\leq r\leq\kappa
, and hence the integral we need to compute is smaller than

\int_{0}^{\kappa\log(g)}\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{-\kappa}^{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(g^{\kappa/2}\right),

which implies that the corresponding density is an element of
\mathcal{R}
.

In order to conclude, we now prove that the contributions of all other local topological types in

\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}^{2\mathrm{s},b}
are

\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(g^{c\kappa}\|F(\ell)e^{\ell/2}\|_{\infty}\right)
. We only provide the details for the former
case. From the cases above, we know that the summation over
j
is not an issue, so we shall only
examine the case
j=0
.

Let us fix a simple loop
\beta
on
S_{1,1}
. Then,
\beta
is a pair of pants decomposition of

S_{1,1}
, and therefore we can use Dehn–Thurston coordinates to enumerate all simple loops
on
S_{1,1}
(see
[
36
]
). As a consequence, the data of a simple loop
\gamma
is
entirely determined by the pair of integers
(i,t)
, where
i
is the intersection number of

\gamma
and
\beta
, and
t
the number of twists of
\gamma
around
\beta
. We denote as
x

the length of
\partial S_{1,1}
and
y
the length of
\beta
. Then, the quantity we wish to
estimate can be expressed as

(10.18)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{\kappa}\int_{0}^{y}\sum_{(i,t)}F(\ell_{x,y,\theta}(\gamma_{i,t}))\frac{V_{g-1,1}(x)}{V_{g}}x\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\theta

where
(i,t)
runs over a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
\gamma_{i,t}
is the simple loop of Dehn–Thurston
coordinates
(i,t)
, and
\ell_{x,y,\theta}(\gamma_{i,t})
denotes its length for the metric

(x,y,\theta)
. Note that the twist parameter
\theta
is only taken in the interval
[0,y)
,
because for any
(i,t)
, the image of
\gamma_{i,t}
by a Dehn twist around
\beta
corresponds to
another term
(i,t^{\prime})
in the sum.

We use the usual bound
V_{g-1,1}(x)/V_{g}=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{x/2}\right)
, and make the following observations. On
the one hand, for small enough
\kappa
, the neighbourhood of width
1
around
\beta
is a
cylinder, by the collar lemma. It follows that the number of intersections between any geodesic

\gamma_{i,t}
of length
\leq L
and
\beta
satisfies
i\leq L/2
. Similarly, we have that

t\leq L/y
. On the other hand,
i\neq 0
because
\beta
and
\gamma
are two simple distinct
loops on
S_{1,1}
, and
i\neq\pm 1
because that is the case we treated above. Hence,

|i|\geq 2
. It is easy to prove, using arguments similar to the double-filling arguments in

Section

8.1
, that this implies that
x\leq\ell_{x,y,\theta}(\gamma_{i,t})+2y
. Altogether, these bounds allow us to say that the quantity
(
10.18
) is bounded, in absolute value, by

\displaystyle\|F(\ell)e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{L+2\kappa}\int_{0}^{\kappa}\int_{0}^{y}\frac{L^{2}}{y}\,x\,e^{y}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\mathcal{O}_{\kappa}\left(L^{4}g^{\kappa}\|F(\ell)e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\|_{\infty}\right)

which is enough to conclude.
∎

10.4.
Proof of
10.1

We now have all the elements needed to proceed to the proof of
10.1
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Let us take a free parameter
\kappa\in(0,1)
(we shall make further
assumptions on
\kappa
throughout the proof). Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\kappa\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon\text{ and }X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(X\notin\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\kappa\right).

We saw in
10.3
that

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(X\notin\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa^{2}+g^{\frac{3}{2}\kappa-1}\right)
. Furthermore,
Mirzakhani proved in
[
27
, Theorem 4.7]
that, provided that
\kappa<0.002
,

\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\kappa\right)
goes to zero as
g\rightarrow+\infty
. As a consequence, if we
prove that, for any small
\kappa>0
,

(10.19)

\lim_{g\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\kappa\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon\text{ and }X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\right)=0

then we obtain that

\limsup_{g\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}\leq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa^{2}\right).

We will therefore be able to deduce
10.1
by letting
\kappa
go to zero.

Now, let
h_{L}
denote the test function from
3.7
, with
L:=6\log(g)
. Let us fix an
integer
m\geq 0
, to be determined later. Motivated by the discussion in

Section

3.4
, we shall apply the Selberg trace formula to the function

H_{L,m}:=\mathcal{D}^{m}h_{L}
, where
\mathcal{D}=1/4-\partial^{2}
.

We use
3.10
to bound
\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})
in the event that

\lambda_{1}\leq 2/9-\epsilon
. Indeed, we have
2/9=1/4-(1/6)^{2}
, and hence taking

\alpha=1/6
, we obtain that provided
\epsilon
is small enough, there exists a constant

C_{\epsilon}>0
such that, for any
X
satisfying
\lambda_{1}(X)\leq 2/9-\epsilon
,

\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1}(X))\geq C_{\epsilon}\,e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)L}=C_{\epsilon}\,g^{1+6\epsilon}.

It follows that, if
\kappa\leq\lambda_{1}\leq 2/9-\epsilon
, then the Fourier transform

r\mapsto(1/4+r^{2})^{m}\hat{h}_{L}(r)
of
H_{L,m}
, taken at
r=r_{1}
, is larger than

C_{\epsilon}\kappa^{m}\,g^{1+6\epsilon}
. As a consequence, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\kappa\leq\lambda_{1}(X)\leq\frac{2}{9}-\epsilon\text{ and }X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\hat{H}_{L,m}(r_{1})\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}\geq C_{\epsilon}\,\kappa^{m}g^{1+6\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\hat{H}_{L,m}(r_{1})\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}\big]}{C_{\epsilon}\,\kappa^{m}\,g^{1+6\epsilon}}

by Markov’s inequality (here, we have used the fact that
\hat{H}_{L,m}(r_{1})\geq 0
, because

\lambda_{1}\geq 0
and, by hypothesis,
\hat{h}_{L}(r_{1})\geq 0
). This reduces the problem to
finding an integer
m
such that, for any small enough
\kappa
,

(10.20)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\hat{H}_{L,m}(r_{1})\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}\big]=\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon,\kappa}\left(g^{1+5\epsilon}\right)

which shall now be our objective.

By positivity of
\hat{H}_{L,m}
on
\mathbb{R}\cup i[-1/2,1/2]
, the expectation

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\hat{H}_{L,m}(r_{1})\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}\big]
is smaller than the
expectation of the Selberg trace formula applied to
H_{L,m}
, multiplied by the indicator of

\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
. Following the proof of
3.11
with this additional
indicator function, we prove that

(10.21)

\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\big[\hat{H}_{L,m}(r_{1})\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{g}}\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)\,H_{L,m}(\ell_{X}(\gamma))}{\exp\left(\frac{\ell_{X}(\gamma)}{2}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}(X)}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}\left((\log g)^{2}g\right).

By
6.8
, there exists a constant
C_{\kappa}>0
such
that, if
X\in\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\kappa}
, then any closed geodesic of length

\leq L=6\log g
